                              Bochner–Riesz multipliers
                                in three dimensions
                                              OpenAI

                                       September 24, 2026


                                             Abstract
       We prove the three-dimensional Bochner–Riesz conjecture in its strict-order formulation.
    The main result is boundedness of the Bochner–Riesz multipliers on L3 (R3 ) for every positive
    order. Interpolation and duality then give the full conjectured strict range.

                                                  1
1     Introduction
We prove the Bochner–Riesz conjecture in three dimensions in its strict-order formulation. The
main estimate is boundedness on L3 for every positive order; interpolation and duality then give
the full range of exponents.
   For f ∈ S(R3 ), use the Fourier transform
                                                     Z
                                          fb(ξ) =          f (x)e−2πix·ξ dx,
                                                      R3

where S denotes the Schwartz space. For δ > 0, define
                                                 Z
                                   Tδ f (x) =         (1 − |ξ|2 )δ+ fb(ξ)e2πix·ξ dξ.                    (1.1)
                                                 R3

Theorem 1.1. For every δ > 0, there is a finite constant Cδ such that

                              ∥Tδ f ∥L3 (R3 ) ≤ Cδ ∥f ∥L3 (R3 )         (f ∈ S(R3 )).                   (1.2)

Consequently Tδ has a unique bounded extension to L3 (R3 ).

    Theorem 1.1 gives
                                                               1 1 1
                                                                                      
           ∥Tδ f ∥Lp (R3 ) ≲p,δ ∥f ∥Lp (R3 ) ,        δ > max 3 − − , 0 ,                  1 ≤ p ≤ ∞.   (1.3)
                                                               p 2 2
Corollary 12.4 supplies the interpolation argument, including the bounds for complex orders.
The order is fixed before choosing any constants. Dilation gives the same bounds for multipliers
(1 − |ξ|2 /R2 )δ+ at every R > 0; for finite p in (1.3), these means converge to f in Lp as R → ∞.
This recovers Fourier inversion by arbitrarily mild spherical smoothing at the central exponents
3/2 ≤ p ≤ 3.

1.1   The problem and its development
The problem belongs to the theory of summability of Fourier expansions. Riesz’s work on one-
dimensional summation methods [29] preceded Bochner’s spherical means for multiple Fourier
series [1, 2]. The multiplier (1 − |ξ|2 )δ+ softens the boundary of the frequency ball; the question is
how much smoothing is needed to preserve an Lp norm. Radial inversion and radial summability
were studied by Herz and Welland [12, 40]. For general functions the geometry of frequency
directions is essential. Using a Kakeya construction, Fefferman proved that in dimension at least
two the order-zero ball multiplier is unbounded on Lp for p ̸= 2 [8], while Carleson and Sjölin
established the conjectured positive-order range in the plane [5].
    Geometric decompositions connect this problem with the arrangement of long thin tubes and
with Fourier restriction. Córdoba’s work on radial multipliers and Kakeya maximal functions
developed this connection through rectangle decompositions and almost orthogonality [6, 7].
Bourgain combined geometric maximal-function estimates with improved restriction and multiplier
bounds in three dimensions [3]. The bilinear restriction theorems of Wolff for the cone and Tao for
the paraboloid and elliptic surfaces [35, 41] led, through Lee’s multiplier reduction, to sharp-order
Bochner–Riesz estimates for max{p, p′ } ≥ 10/3 [17]; here p′ is the Hölder conjugate of p. The
threshold exponent follows from Lee’s printed strict range by interpolation with L2 , keeping a
positive margin in the order.

                                                            2
    Polynomial partitioning and subsequent multiscale refinements brought further progress on
restriction [11, 37]. For the multiplier itself, Wu obtained the conjectured strict-order range when
max{p, p′ } ≥ 13/4 [42]. Guo, Oh, Wang, Wu, and Zhang developed a pseudoconformal approach
and an induction on two scales for transferring restriction methods to Bochner–Riesz estimates [10].
More recently, Gao, Wu, and Xi established the strict-order multiplier range max{p, p′ } ≥ 22/7
[9, Corollary 1.3]. Wang and Wu’s related diagonal extension estimate holds for p > 22/7 [38,
Theorem 0.2]. The connection also runs in the other direction: Tao showed that the Bochner–Riesz
conjecture implies the spherical restriction conjecture [34]. Our argument directly establishes the
scalar multiplier bound on L3 (R3 ) for every fixed positive order. Zipeng Wang has also proposed
a proof of the Bochner–Riesz conjecture in all dimensions [39, version 6, Theorem One].
    Let S 2 = {ω ∈ R3 : |ω| = 1} and let σ be surface area measure on S 2 . For bounded measurable
g : S 2 → C, define                           Z
                                  Eg(x) =          g(ω)e2πix·ω dσ(ω).
                                              S2
Tao’s implication applied to (1.3) gives, for every real p > 3,

                                   ∥Eg∥Lp (R3 ) ≤ Cp ∥g∥L∞ (S 2 ,σ) ,

where Cp < ∞ depends only on p. This is the spherical extension estimate also proved in OpenAI
[23, Theorem 1.1]. For the separate diagonal extension theorem on compact smooth positively
curved surfaces, possibly with smooth boundary, see OpenAI [21, Theorem 1.1]; that general-surface
statement is not deduced here from the spherical multiplier theorem.
    An independent route through critical local smoothing gives the same full strict fixed-radius
range in (1.3), uniformly in the radius [22, Corollary 11.2]. It also gives strong maximal Bochner–
Riesz bounds and almost-everywhere summation for 3 ≤ p < ∞ and δ > 1 − 3/p [22, Theorem 11.1].
These results are not inputs to the argument here.
    The geometric input here is the sharp planar Furstenberg estimate of Ren and Wang [28].
It belongs to a line of projection theory that includes the classical work of Marstrand and
Kaufman [15, 19] and the discretized sum-product and projection methods of Bourgain [4]. Oberlin
formulated a sharp prediction for exceptional projection directions [20]. Important advances toward
the Furstenberg problem include the packing-dimension results of Orponen and Shmerkin [24, 31],
their joint Hausdorff-dimension improvement [25], and their proof of the conjectured bound when
the associated family of affine lines has equal Hausdorff and packing dimensions [26]. Building on
that regular case, Ren and Wang removed the additional equality assumption. We use their finite
incidence theorem. Section 3 derives the form needed for probability weights, auxiliary tags that
may share a slope, and incidence graphs of positive product mass; Section 2 supplies the finite
conditioning used in later applications.

1.2   From geometric growth to oscillatory cancellation
The proof has two stages. We first control the information gained by observing positive masses
moving along lines. We then transfer that control to wave packets, whose squared amplitudes
are positive but whose superposition is oscillatory. The main obstruction is at the uncertainty
boundary: an earlier packet cannot be assigned a position as precisely as a later observation
requires. The second stage records this discrepancy as fine velocity information and shows that
too much growth would make that information exceed its finite capacity.




                                                    3
Positive masses on lines. A particle T carries bounded data (V, A) ∈ R2 × R2 and moves
along c 7→ A + cV . Fix a small parameter ϵ. An aperture observation at duration t records velocity
in a rectangle of widths ϵb , ϵa , b ≤ a, and position in a transported rectangle of widths ϵb+t , ϵa+t .
Its orientation is also recorded. For 0 < γ < 1, the weight

                                      w(b, a) = a + b − γ(a − b)

rewards fine resolution while penalizing eccentricity. The score in (4.1) subtracts the particle
information required by the observation and adds twice the time entropy conditional on the particle.
Its largest asymptotic increase per unit duration is denoted by β. Theorem 4.2 proves β ≤ γ.
    To prove this bound, assume excessive growth and localize a sequence whose scores approach
the largest possible rate. The aspect penalty makes the orientations of such observations coherent.
Choosing the least possible aspect produces a saturated path: its intermediate observations attain
the same growth rate. There are two alternatives. An isotropic extremizer is excluded in Section 5
by combining time-fiber richness, planar projections, and a Loomis–Whitney argument. In the
other alternative, the orientation and longitudinal resolutions advance at a fixed ratio m > 0.
    The latter case produces four data coordinates with resolution speeds 0, 1, m, 1 + m. Their
particle-information profile is Lipschitz, but ordinary almost everywhere differentiability does
not give a derivative on the particular plane selected by the path. Section 6 proves the needed
trace and affine approximation there. Planar projections then transfer lower bounds between the
coordinate rates. When m = 1, the middle coordinates share a speed and only their joint rate
is available. Two conditional projection tests and a reciprocal pinned-direction argument supply
the missing inequalities. The latter is related to the radial-projection bootstraps of Shmerkin and
Wang and of Orponen, Shmerkin, and Wang [27, 32]. Section 8 combines these rate constraints
with the saturated path’s contact and jump conditions to obtain a contradiction.

Oscillatory packets. Write λ−1 = ϵHP . At duration t, a packet has velocity depth ut =
(HP − t)/2 and position depth ut + t. The packet score in (9.6) uses squared oscillatory amplitudes,
and its largest growth rate is denoted by βq . Sections 9–10 construct finite decompositions,
control their tails, and track the loss when only part of an assigned atom class is retained. These
constructions use the Fourier-series localization and almost orthogonality underlying classical
wave-packet methods [10, 35].
    Away from the uncertainty boundary, a coarser observation faithfully locates the packets, so
the positive-mass estimate applies. At the boundary, a separate coherent estimate is required. It
leaves a layer of fine velocity information, which must be carried to the preceding step. Section 11
proves that hypothetical growth βq > γ/2 increases this information by at least (4βq − 2γ)h, up
to controlled errors, at every backward step of fixed length 2h. The layer holds at most 2h units,
up to vanishing errors. A fixed finite number of steps therefore yields a contradiction and proves
Theorem 9.1: βq ≤ γ/2.

The multiplier. Packet growth gives the oscillatory estimate of Theorem 12.1, with an arbitrarily
small positive power loss. On a physical shell of radius λ, the Bochner–Riesz kernel has amplitude
of order λ−2−δ . The pseudoconformal distance-phase change of variables used in Guo et al. [10]
puts this shell into the required phase class. Section 12 verifies all phase and amplitude bounds
uniformly in the shell and obtains the operator norm Oδ,ν (λ−δ+ν ). Choosing 0 < ν < δ proves
Theorem 1.1 by summation. A beta-integral representation supplies the complex-order bounds
used in Stein’s analytic interpolation theorem [33], completing (1.3).


                                                   4
1.3   Finite laws and order of limits
Entropy permits a common language for these geometric and analytic steps. We use finite entropy
and relative entropy [16, 30], conditional copies and submodularity [36], and homogeneous profiles
across scales. Multiscale entropy and comparable-partition methods have important precedents in
Hochman [13], Hochman and Shmerkin [14]. Section 2 proves the precise finite-law statements
used here.
    Small normalized mutual information yields a comparison with a product law after trimming;
it does not give exact independence. Theorem 2.7 specifies which labels are fixed and which
remain free, so later conditioning uses the correct comparison population. For the characteristic-
plane argument, first fix a homogeneous component and its limiting profile, then choose the
approximation scale, and only afterwards take the finite precision small relative to that scale.
Theorem 6.1 and Corollary 6.2 state these successive steps.
    Packet composition also keeps two finite populations distinct. Its upper estimate concerns a
retained superposition, while its earlier lower test uses the whole assigned cap class. Proposition 10.1
records the loss from retention. Section 11 carries the remaining fine velocity information through
a fixed finite number of cuts, using the one-step statement of Proposition 11.4.
    Throughout, H, I denote entropy and mutual information divided by log(1/ϵ), whereas Hnat , Inat
use natural logarithms. The notation log1/ϵ (r) uses the normalized units. The Planck depth is
always HP , distinct from entropy. A positive depth increment is fixed before the small-parameter
limit; when increments subsequently shrink, that additional limit is stated separately.


2     Finite entropy, conditioning, and passage to profiles
The geometric arguments will use conditional point and parameter populations whose masses
must remain controlled after labels are fixed. We first develop the finite probability estimates
that justify those operations, and then explain their passage to limiting entropy profiles. All
probability spaces in the finite statements are finite, with cardinalities allowed to depend on ϵ. Put
ℓ = log(1/ϵ) and write Hnat , Inat for natural-log entropy and mutual information, with H = Hnat /ℓ
and I = Inat /ℓ. The exact particle index T may have an arbitrarily large alphabet; no bound on its
entropy is required. Every comparison measure is specified explicitly and need not be a marginal
of the law being estimated.

2.1   Entropy estimates with unrestricted parent alphabets
Our conventions are the usual finite entropy and relative entropy [16, 30]. Conditional copies and
entropy submodularity also feature in Tao’s entropy calculus [36]; the comparison and retention
bounds required here are proved below.
    We use the chain rule, nonnegativity of conditional mutual information, and Hnat (X) ≤ log |X |
for an X -valued observation. Here are the particular consequences, including quantitative restriction
estimates, that we will need.
Lemma 2.1 (Determination, masses, and restriction). Let A, B, D, T be finite observations. Then
                                        Inat (T ; B) ≤ Inat (T ; A) + Hnat (B | A),               (2.1)
                     |Hnat (B | T ) − Hnat (A | T )| ≤ Hnat (B | A) + Hnat (A | B).               (2.2)
If, outside an event of probability ζ, B has at most N possible values given A, and |B| ≤ M , then
                               Hnat (B | A) ≤ log 2 + log N + ζ log M.                            (2.3)

Proof. The chain rule gives Inat (T ; B) ≤ Inat (T ; A, B) = Inat (T ; A) + Inat (T ; B | A), proving (2.1).
Apply the chain rule to Hnat (A, B | T ) in both orders to obtain (2.2). Adjoining the indicator
of the exceptional event costs at most log 2; conditioning on its two values gives (2.3). For (2.4),
normalize m to a probability law and use nonnegativity of its relative entropy from the law of B.
Expanding the conditional relative entropy gives (2.5). Its expectation is at most KL(QB,D ∥PB,D ),
which by marginalization is at most KL(Q∥P) ≤ log K.

    We call a determination of zero cost when the right side of (2.3), divided by ℓ, tends to zero.
This includes bounded ambiguity between comparable translated grids, provided the translation is
recorded. When |B| ≤ ϵ−C , vanishing exceptional probability is sufficient for the last term.

Lemma 2.3 (Finite splitting and resampling). For any finite tag Q,

                   0 ≤ Hnat (D | B) − Hnat (D | B, Q) ≤ Hnat (Q),
                         |Inat (T ; D | Q) − Inat (T ; D)| ≤ Hnat (Q),
                                     Inat (U ; V | Z, Q) ≤ Inat (U ; V | Z) + Hnat (Q).

The conditional quantities on the left are averages of the quantities on the individual tag fibers. If
U, V are conditionally independent given Z under P, and R ≤ KP, then

                                        IR
                                         nat (U ; V | Z) ≤ log K.

Given a joint law of (T, C, V ) and a kernel κ(u | T, C), one may adjoin U by this kernel while
preserving that entire joint law. The new U is conditionally independent of V given (T, C), and
its joint law with (T, C) is the prescribed kernel law.

         Inat (U ; V | Z, Q) − Inat (U ; V | Z) = Inat (U ; Q | V, Z) − Inat (U ; Q | Z) ≤ Hnat (Q).
For the dominated law, decompose conditional relative entropy against PU |Z ⊗PV |Z into conditional
mutual information plus the two nonnegative marginal relative entropies. Its average is at most
KL(RU,V,Z ∥PU,V,Z ) ≤ log K. Finally the resampled joint law is exactly P(t, c, v)κ(u | t, c); summing
and conditioning prove all its stated properties.

    Joint bins give a sharper version for increments. Suppose G is any parent, possibly containing
T , and on retained triples (g, b, d),

        ϵb0 +a ≤ P(B = b | G = g) ≤ ϵb0 −a ,                ϵd0 +a ≤ P(B = b, D = d | G = g) ≤ ϵd0 −a .

Dividing these inequalities gives conditional surprises for D | (G, B) in [d0 − b0 − 2a, d0 − b0 + 2a]
and at most ϵ−(d0 −b0 +2a) retained children per (g, b). Indeed, the sum of the lower joint masses
cannot exceed the upper parent mass. Lemma 2.1 therefore bounds entropy errors under a density
loss K by 2a + log K/ℓ, independently of the size or entropy of the parent. These are estimates
for retained support and for sampled conditional values; no assertion about every untrimmed
conditional fiber is needed.

Proof. Starting with ν, repeatedly remove all points with Flj = v whenever its current positive

remove support, and normalization has precisely the claimed effect on masses.

    The next theorem specifies the laws compared when labels are fixed. A point may be an exact
finite particle or a tuple of spatial observations. Additional finite tags may be included in a label.

Theorem 2.7 (Finite stars and conditioning with named comparison laws). Let π(p, b) be a
probability law with µ = πP , λ = πB . Let E be an allowed set with π(E) ≥ 1 − ζ and suppose

                             1E (p, b)π(p, b) ≤ Kµ(p)λ(b),                    K ≥ 1.                    (2.19)

    All these conclusions concern a fixed finite star. They supply no lower bound for the density
of an arbitrary graph of cyclic incidences. If K, M0−1 , m−1 , u−1 , η −1 and α−1 have logarithms
o(ℓ) and n, Nfac are fixed, every displayed loss is ϵ−o(1) . For a normalized retained incidence law
ρ ≤ Aµ ⊗ λ, its support has (µ ⊗ λ)-mass at least A−1 , simply by integrating the domination
inequality. This is the dense-graph conclusion used in the projection theorem.

2.3   Countable profiles and the order of the limits
The use of entropy at successive resolutions is related to the local entropy averages and comparable-
partition arguments of Hochman and Shmerkin [14, Sections 3.3 and 4.2] and Hochman [13,
Section 3]. Here we need a finite-law diagonal with explicit retention properties, which we establish
directly.
    A conditional observation is homogeneous with exponent d if its realized conditional surprise,
divided by ℓ, converges to d in probability and its normalized conditional entropy also converges
to d. When further restrictions will be made, we first retain the uniform bins of Lemma 2.4.
Convergence in probability√by itself is insufficient for this purpose. For example, let ℓ → ∞,
give one atom mass 1 − e− ℓ , and distribute the remaining mass uniformly among ⌈eℓ ⌉ atoms.
Normalized√ surprise converges to zero in probability, but restriction to the latter atoms has
density e ℓ and entropy tending to one in normalized units. The support and bin requirements in
Lemma 2.4 exclude precisely this issue.

Lemma 2.9 (Profiles, countable homogenization, and diagonals). The following three statements
hold.
   1. Suppose for each ϵ → 0 there is a countable list (Xj , Yj )j≥1 , with |Yj | ≤ ϵ−Aj for each fixed
      j. One can partition all but o(1) of the law into ϵ−o(1) parts, each of mass at least ϵo(1) , such
      that the following holds. From any choice of one retained part at each scale, a subsequence
      can be extracted on which every listed observation is homogeneous. The same exponents are
      inherited by every sequence of laws supported on those parts whose density relative to their
      normalized laws is at most ϵ−o(1) . The assertion is simultaneous for joint observations and
      observations conditional on an exact particle index, when these are included in the list.
   2. Let v range over a compact rectangle of depth parameters. Suppose observations Yv , condi-
      tional on any X, obey, uniformly for fixed u, v, the two ambiguity bounds

                        log N (Yv | Yu , X), log N (Yu | Yv , X) ≤ Cℓ∥u − v∥1 + o(ℓ),

      and have bounded normalized alphabet sizes at one reference depth. Their normalized entropy
      profiles have subsequences converging on all depths to a C-Lipschitz function, after extraction
      on a countable dense set. Homogeneous surprise exponents on that dense set extend to the
      same function at every fixed depth, provided the ambiguity bounds also hold on the retained
      support.


                                                  13
  3. Suppose a finite construction at each fixed h > 0 has finitely many normalized errors tending
     to zero as ϵ → 0. Given any sequence of permissible hk ↓ 0, and any countable sequence of
     such requirements, a diagonal can be chosen so that the first k requirements have errors at
     most hk /k and hk log(1/ϵk ) → ∞. Thus errors may be made o(h) before passing to the scale
     ϵh . This assertion supplies no rate uniform in h or in the limiting profile.

Proof. For the first assertion, apply Lemma 2.4 to lists of length qk ↑ ∞, meshes ak ↓ 0, and, for
example, σ = 1, θk ↓ 0. At stage k the number Jk is finite and independent of ϵ. Choose the
stages sufficiently slowly that qk ϵ → 0 and log(Jk /θk ) = o(ℓ). The discarded mass tends to zero
and every retained part has the claimed lower mass. For each fixed j the bin centers stay in the
compact interval [0, Aj + 1]. Successive extraction and a diagonal make all those centers converge.
Equations (2.13) and (2.14), with η ↓ 0 sufficiently slowly and log(1/η) = o(ℓ), prove homogeneity.
The same estimates prove its asserted inheritance for any dominated sequence; no exceptional set
from before binning is reused. All bounds depend on the child alphabets only.
   For the second assertion, the chain rule bounds the difference of conditional entropies by the
two ambiguity logarithms. A bounded sequence of profiles therefore has, by diagonal extraction
on a dense countable set, a limit satisfying the Lipschitz inequality there. Extend it continuously.
Approximation of any fixed parameter by dense parameters and the same inequality proves
convergence there. For surprises, the identity

             − log P(Yv | X) + log P(Yu | X) = − log P(Yv | Yu , X) + log P(Yu | Yv , X)

is exact at sampled values. For an observation with at most N values on each conditional fiber, the
conditional-surprise tail above log N + t has probability at most e−t , by summing masses less than
e−t /N . Apply this to both terms. Since conditional surprises are nonnegative, their difference in
absolute value√is at most the larger of their two upper bounds outside probability 2e−t . Take, for
instance, t = ℓ, then approximate v by the dense set. This proves the assertion about surprise.
    For the last assertion, fix hk first. The defining small-parameter convergences give a sufficiently
small ϵk for the first k errors to be at most hk /k. Decrease ϵk further so that hk log(1/ϵk ) ≥ k
and the scales decrease strictly. This inductive choice proves the claim. If a profile must first be
chosen and differentiated, choose its regular points and admissible hk before making this final
small-parameter selection.

    In applications with a block length h, “subpower loss” means log K = o(hℓ), not merely o(ℓ).
For example the logarithm of a conditional k-factor core constant in Theorem 2.7 is bounded by a
fixed linear combination of

                n log(K/α),    log(1/M0 ),   log(1/m),    log(1/η),    k log(2Nfac /u).

These quantities must be chosen o(hℓ) with h fixed first. The finite numbers of observations and
replicas are also fixed at that stage. Lemma 2.9 then permits increasing them slowly and shrinking
h afterwards. When an increment has a much larger parent entropy, (2.6) and the uniform child
counts give the same o(h) error without multiplying by that parent entropy.
    Finally, the use of universal bounds after conditioning is a selection principle for a specified
array of conditional laws. At each scale fix a class Aϵ of laws and parameters such that every
sequence selected from these classes is admissible for the asserted universal bound. In particular,
all vanishing-error hypotheses must have common deterministic envelopes within the array: a
hypothesis log K = o(ℓ) is represented there by log K ≤ rϵ ℓ with one rϵ → 0. If Fϵ (P, ϑ) has


                                                  14
lim sup Fϵ ≤ 0 along every such selected sequence, then

                                            sup     Fϵ (P, ϑ) ≤ o(1).
                                         (P,ϑ)∈Aϵ

Otherwise choose a violating law and parameter at each of a sequence of scales. The same upper
bound holds for any average over conditional laws in this array, regardless of the number of
conditioning cells. The envelopes may depend on the construction; this argument asserts no
uniform convergence over all possible sequences satisfying an unspecified subpower condition.
When the universal statement is initially given for fixed parameters, this argument requires stability
under convergent parameters; compactness alone does not supply that stability. The geometric
applications below verify it by bounded box enlargements and O(∥ϑ − ϑ′ ∥) changes in depth
weights.


3    The planar projection estimate
The geometric input is the discrete planar Furstenberg theorem of Ren and Wang. We state
precisely the consequence used below, including weighted populations, extra parameter labels, and
incomplete incidence graphs. No restriction estimate in three dimensions is used as an input.
    For a bounded set E, let |E|ρ denote the number of half-open dyadic ρ-squares meeting E,
where replacing a scale by a comparable dyadic scale is harmless. A finite family of dyadic ρ-squares
P is a (ρ, d, C)-set if

                      #{p ∈ P : p ∩ B(z, r) ̸= ∅} ≤ Crd #P,             ρ ≤ r ≤ 1,

up to a fixed enlargement of the ball. We use dyadic tubes in a fixed bounded slope chart. The
same definition for tubes refers to their slope–intercept parameters. For tubes meeting one fixed
ρ-square, this nonconcentration condition is equivalent, within fixed factors, to the corresponding
condition on slopes.

Theorem 3.1 (Ren–Wang, discrete form). Fix 0 < s ≤ 1, 0 < d ≤ 2, and α > 0. There are
η0 = η0 (α, s, d) > 0 and ρ0 = ρ0 (α, s, d) > 0 with the following property. Suppose P is a nonempty
(ρ, d, ρ−η0 )-set of squares, with 0 < ρ < ρ0 . For each p ∈ P, let T (p) be a family of tubes meeting
p, of cardinality comparable to M , and forming a (ρ, s, ρ−η0 )-set. Then

                                         T (p) ≳α ρ− min{1,d,(d+s)/2}+α M.
                                   [
                               #                                                                 (3.1)
                                   p∈P

The statement is unchanged by fixed bounded domains, finitely many coordinate charts, or fixed-
factor enlargements of the grids and tubes, after decreasing η0 , ρ0 if necessary.

    This is Theorem 4.1 of Ren and Wang [28], expressed using its Definition 3.1 of a nice
configuration. That definition already permits each T (p) to be a selected subfamily of the tubes
meeting p. The reduction below additionally handles probability weights, auxiliary tags that may
share a slope, and incidence graphs of positive product mass. The fixed-factor variants follow
by taking comparable dyadic scales, covering by boundedly many grid pieces, and absorbing the
resulting fixed constants into the input loss. The order of the quantifiers is important: the desired
output loss α is fixed before the admissible input loss η0 .
    A tag is a slope together with any auxiliary labels; distinct tags may have the same slope.
Keeping the full tag allows its set of neighboring points to depend on that additional information.

                                                       15
Theorem 3.2 (Weighted projection bound with parameter tags). Fix 0 < d ≤ 2, 0 < s ≤ 1, and
α > 0, and fixed bounded point and slope domains. There are η > 0 and ρ1 > 0 such that the
following holds for 0 < ρ < ρ1 . Let µ be a finite probability population of points in R2 . Let ν be a
finite probability population of tags b, with a slope a(b) assigned to each tag. Suppose

                     µ(B(z, r)) ≤ ρ−η rd ,                       (a∗ ν)(I) ≤ ρ−η rs               (3.2)

for all balls and intervals of radius or length comparable to r ∈ [ρ, 1]. Let G be a graph of point–tag
incidences with (µ×ν)(G) ≥ ρη . For each tag b, suppose that the projection πa(b) (x) of its neighbors
can be covered by at most M intervals of length ρ. Here πa (x1 , x2 ) = x2 − ax1 . One may multiply
these maps by scalars whose absolute values have fixed positive lower and upper bounds. Then

                                      M ≥ ρ− min{1,d,(d+s)/2}+α .                                 (3.3)

Changing the implicit fixed factors in the domains, projections, and interval lengths changes only
η, ρ1 .
    In particular, along any sequence with subpower nonconcentration constants, graph product
mass ρo(1) , and conditional projection count at most ρ−l−o(1) , one has

                                       l ≥ min{1, d, (d + s)/2}.                                  (3.4)

The limiting assertion for d = 0 is immediate.
Proof. Absorb any allowed scalar factors into the interval lengths, and use πa (x1 , x2 ) = x2 − ax1 .
Normalized orthogonal projections also have this form on boundedly many direction charts, after
an orthogonal coordinate change and such a bounded scalar factor. Write q = (µ × ν)(G), and
independently sample

                         N = ⌈ρ−d ⌉    points,      Q = ⌈ρ−s ⌉      whole tags.

The tags, including any information besides their slope, are sampled without alteration.
    We give the finite loss accounting. In this proof C denotes a fixed numerical constant, enlarged
a finite number of times; it does not depend on ρ, η, or the population cardinalities. For fixed
η > 0 and sufficiently small ρ, the sampled multisets have, simultaneously for all dyadic balls or
intervals at scales between ρ and 1,

                                  #{xi ∈ B(z, r)} ≤ Cρ−3η (r/ρ)d ,                                (3.5)
                                                         −3η         s
                                      #{a(bj ) ∈ I} ≤ Cρ       (r/ρ) .                            (3.6)

Moreover, the failure probability is smaller than every fixed power of ρ. Indeed each count is
binomial, its expectation is bounded by ρ−η N rd , or ρ−η Qrs , and the displayed threshold has an
additional factor at least a fixed multiple of ρ−2η . The standard exponential Markov estimate

                                P(X ≥ t) ≤ (e EX/t)t ,         t ≥ EX,

follows by applying Markov’s inequality to exp(θX) and optimizing θ. Here the smallest nonvacuous
threshold is a positive multiple of ρ−3η . A union bound over the polynomially many dyadic cells
and scales proves the assertion. Every ball or interval is covered by boundedly many such cells of
comparable scale.
    The expected sampled edge count is qN Q, whereas its maximum is N Q. The occupancy failure
event therefore contributes at most o(qN Q) to this expectation. Some sample obeys (3.5)–(3.6)

                                                  16
and has at least qN Q/2 edges. Delete point vertices of degree less than qQ/4. At least qN Q/4
edges remain, hence there are at least qN/4 remaining point vertices.
    A finest point square contains at most Cρ−3η sampled vertices. Keep one remaining vertex in
each occupied square; the resulting point-square family has cardinality at least C −1 qρ3η N . Each
selected vertex still has at least qQ/4 incident sampled tags.
    For a tag and one of its allowed projection intervals, its slope and projection bin specify a
tube through the corresponding incident point. Rounding these data to the tube grid and using
a bounded enlargement costs only fixed factors. At one selected point square, only boundedly
many different intercept bins in a fixed slope bin can give tubes through that square. By (3.6), a
tube there can be represented by at most Cρ−3η of the sampled tags, up to the same bounded
neighboring-bin multiplicity. Remove such coincidences. Each selected point is incident to at least
C −1 qρ3η Q distinct tubes.
    Pigeonhole the point vertices according to dyadic ranges of this last degree. There are
O(log(1/ρ)) ranges. Keep a range containing at least that fraction of the selected points, and prune
the tube families to a common size M0 within a factor of two. For small ρ, all these operations
give
                                    #P ≥ ρCη N,      M0 ≥ ρCη Q.                                (3.7)
The original sampled count bounds remain upper bounds on every retained subset. Dividing
them by the retained cardinalities in (3.7) proves that P is a (ρ, d, ρ−Cη )-set and every T (p) is a
(ρ, s, ρ−Cη )-set. The constants can be taken uniform through the finite grid changes.
     On the other hand, each of the Q sampled tags has at most M allowed projection bins, so the
total number of distinct tubes is at most CQM . Put

                                      χ = min{1, d, (d + s)/2}.

Apply Theorem 3.1 with output loss α/4. Choose η sufficiently small that Cη < η0 (α/4, s, d) and
Cη < α/4, and then choose ρ1 sufficiently small for all preceding bounds and fixed constants. The
theorem yields
                           CQM ≳α ρ−χ+α/4 M0 ≥ ρ−χ+α/4+Cη Q.
Absorbing the fixed constants by another loss less than α/2 gives (3.3).
    For the last assertion, apply the finite theorem at any fixed α > 0. Its hypotheses eventually
hold because their losses are subpower. Comparison with the assumed projection count gives
l ≥ χ − α; let α ↓ 0.

Remark 3.3 (Incidence laws and conditional applications). Often an incidence graph is supplied by
a trimmed subprobability σ rather than directly by µ × ν. If σ(G) ≥ m and σ ≤ Kµ × ν, then

                                         (µ × ν)(G) ≥ m/K.

Thus subpower mass and subpower product domination give exactly the graph hypothesis of
Theorem 3.2. Uniform lower bounds on retained conditional atom masses give the required
projection support counts. Uniform upper bounds at every fixed prefix, followed by a sufficiently
fine finite scale mesh, give the required ball-mass bounds. The conditioning and homogenization
results in Section 2 justify those operations when applied in the prescribed order. In each application
below we specify which point population and parameter population are being compared; an entropy
lower bound without this uniformization is not a substitute for (3.2).



                                                  17
4     Extremal positive transport and its aspect
Fix 0 < γ < 1, and put

                  w(b, a) = (1 + γ)b + (1 − γ)a = a + b − γ(a − b),              0 ≤ b ≤ a.

All entropies in this section are divided by log(1/ϵ). The particle T is a random variable carrying
two bounded vectors V (T ), A(T ) ∈ R2 ; its position at time c is X(c) = A + cV . The bounded real
variable c may have any joint law with T . The observations used below are finite. For a finite
label Y and arbitrary T , H(Y | T ) denotes the average entropy of the conditional probabilities
E[1{Y =y} | σ(T )], divided by log(1/ϵ), and I(T ; Y ) = H(Y ) − H(Y | T ). Thus no entropy of T is
used. A fixed enlargement of the bounded domains is allowed. There is no Planck parameter in
this section.

4.1   Apertures and the universal growth rate
Use nested grids for time and write Ct for the cell of c of length comparable to ϵt , together with
its chosen representative. At t = 0 one cell contains the time domain and its representative is
zero. The finitely many boundary choices in this convention have entropy O(1/ log(1/ϵ)). For
an unoriented normal R, let QR be an orthogonal frame whose first vector is tangent and whose
second vector is normal. Orientations of aspect e are recorded in cells of angular size comparable
to ϵe . Nested angular grids, or a finite collection of nested slope grids, may be used. Choose a
representative normal for each recorded angular cell; both rectangular grids use its frame.

Definition 4.1 (Standard aperture observation). At duration t and horizon L, a standard
observation G records

              (t, b, a, Ct , Ra−b , [V ]b,a;R , [X(Ct )]b+t,a+t;R ),   0 ≤ b ≤ a,   a + t ≤ L.

Here the bracket denotes the rectangular grid cell, in the recorded frame, with side lengths ϵb , ϵa ,
or respectively ϵb+t , ϵa+t . Its aperture weight is w(G) = w(b, a). The shape and orientation may
be chosen by a stochastic kernel; extra finite labels are permitted. Equivalently, a label may locate
the data in bounded enlargements of the indicated rectangles. Adding the actual grid cells then
costs O(1/ log(1/ϵ)) information.

     Depths can be rounded on a mesh ξ = ξϵ ↓ 0 with log(1/ξ) = o(log(1/ϵ)). The number of
shape choices in a bounded triangle is then subpower. In this terminology a factor is subpower
if its normalized logarithm tends to zero. Dyadic spatial widths give a finite menu of apertures:
choose orientation spacing comparable to the minor-to-major axis ratio and translates spaced by
the corresponding widths. Every rectangle is contained in a fixed enlargement of one in this menu.
Indeed an angular error at most a constant times the axis ratio moves a tangent uncertainty into
at most a constant multiple of the normal uncertainty. This verifies the assertion for both columns.
Discarding extra labels never increases particle information. Define the root supremum by

                                τL (T ) =        sup       {Ew(D) − I(T ; D)}.
                                            D at duration 0

The supremum is taken for the current particle law and the current precision, before any limit. For
a fixed finite menu it is a supremum over all stochastic assignments to compatible caps. Rounding
depths down on the mesh and adjoining the actual target cells costs O(ξ) + O(1/ log(1/ϵ)) in root


                                                         18
score, uniformly on fixed domain, horizon, and enlargement bounds. Thus these menus recover
the unrestricted root supremum in the limit.
    The score balances three quantities. The aperture weight rewards finer localization, particle
information charges what the observation reveals about T , and conditional time entropy measures
the time information left after the particle is known. The root supremum provides the zero-duration
benchmark for this balance.
    Write St = H(Ct | T ) and

                                                                           Ft (G) − τL (T )
               Ft (G) = Ew(G) − I(T ; G) + 2St ,         β = sup lim sup                    .      (4.1)
                                                                   ϵ→0            t

The supremum ranges over all fixed 0 < t ≤ L < ∞ and all sequences of the systems just defined.
Domain bounds are fixed within each sequence. Fixed affine normalizations put them in one
bounded domain without changing a limit. The label G determines, up to bounded ambiguity, a
root aperture of the same shape: recover A = X(Ct ) − Ct V and coarsen its position rectangle.
Consequently Ft (G) − τL (T ) ≤ 2St + o(1) ≤ 2t + o(1). For a single particle and independent
uniform time, the choices b = a = L − t give τL = 2L + o(1) and Ft = 2L + o(1). Thus

                                                0 ≤ β ≤ 2.                                         (4.2)

Theorem 4.2 (Positive-transport growth). For the aperture systems of Definition 4.1, the rate in
(4.1) satisfies β ≤ γ.

    We prove this bound by examining a sequence whose growth approaches β. Localization and
the least-aspect construction produce a saturated path. Its tangent reduction has two alternatives:
an isotropic system, excluded in Section 5, or a path whose aspect grows in proportion to time.
Sections 6 and 7 constrain the local information rates of the latter path; Section 8 combines those
constraints with saturation to finish the proof. Until that point we use only (4.2) and the definition
of β.
    The rate in (4.1) is unchanged if the underlying probability spaces are required to be finite.
Fix the complete finite root menu and project G to its geometric coordinates, which can only
decrease particle information. For every possible output/time pair (g, k), use the two compatibility
tests for V and A + c̄k V , where c̄k is the recorded time representative. Partition particles by all
these tests and by membership in every root cap. The finite signature κ(T ) depends on T alone,
not on the sampled time. Choose an actual (V, A) in each positive signature, with finite particle
index T f . Give (T f , G, Ct ) the old law of (κ(T ), G, Ct ), and realize each time label by a point in
its actual cell. The resulting finite law is compatible and has the same expected weight, with

                           I(T f ; G) ≤ I(T ; G),    H(Ct | T f ) ≥ H(Ct | T ).

For roots, averaging a compatible kernel within signatures preserves its output marginal and
weight and decreases information; conversely, every signature kernel lifts through κ(T ) with equal
information. The frozen-menu root suprema therefore agree exactly. The uniform menu error
above is o(1), so every general-law score excess is at most that of a finite law plus o(1). We take
these finite laws before forming the conditional systems and independent copies below.
    We make explicit the uniformity implicit in that definition. For any fixed domain bound B,
K < ∞, and tmin > 0, there is a function ωϵ (B, K, tmin ) → 0 such that

                   Ft (G) ≤ τL (T ) + βt + ωϵ (B, K, tmin ),      tmin ≤ t ≤ L ≤ K.                (4.3)


                                                    19
Here the menu, its depth mesh, and its fixed enlargement convention are chosen as deterministic
functions of ϵ, common to all systems in the supremum. It also applies to every conditional system
with these bounds. To see this, a failure selects systems and parameters with a fixed positive
excess and a convergent subsequence of (t, L). Changing depths by ν costs at most CK,γ ν + o(1):
cover the larger rectangle by the finer cells, including its changed frame in the count. Coarsening
time by ν loses at most ν + o(1) of conditional time entropy. Transport to the coarser time uses
an uncertainty ϵt−ν times the measured velocity rectangle, which fits the earlier position rectangle.
Finally, coarsening both depths of a root test by at most ν loses at most 2ν + o(1) in weight;
this proves the same continuity for the root supremum when its horizon changes by ν. Fixed
parameter slack therefore converts the selected systems to a sequence contradicting (4.1). The
same argument at t = 0 uses the root supremum directly, with no division by time.
    Whenever we use conditional arrays below, we first fix the finite list of observations, the depth
mesh, all trimming thresholds, and the positive time increments. Their exceptional probabilities
and normalized errors are then bounded by one deterministic function qϵ → 0 on every retained cell.
Cell coordinate bounds and covering multiplicities are common constants. Thus every selection of
one retained system per precision remains an admissible sequence in (4.1). The conditional-array
selection principle at the end of Section 2 applies to precisely this array; it does not require a
uniform rate over unspecified subpower envelopes.

4.2   Splitting and cap bounds
Call an observation saturated if, along the sequence under discussion, Ft (G) − τL (T ) → βt. An
extremizer below always means such a sequence, not a maximizing measure at finite precision.

Lemma 4.3 (Splitting saturated laws). Let Z be a finite tag. If ∆ = βt + τL (T ) − Ft (G), and ∆z
is the corresponding deficit for the law conditional on Z = z, then

                                        E∆Z ≤ ∆ + 3H(Z).

In particular subpower splittings preserve saturation on typical parts, simultaneously for any fixed
finite list of saturated observations.

Proof. Combining conditional root tests and including Z in their label gives EτL (T | Z) ≤
τL (T ) + I(T ; Z). The exact chain rules give

                  EFt (G | Z) − Ft (G) = −I(T ; Z | G) + I(T ; Z) − 2I(Ct ; Z | T ).

Subtract and use that each of the last two nonnegative costs is at most H(Z). By (4.3), each
conditional deficit is at least −ωϵ , uniformly over the cells. Its positive part therefore tends to
zero in mean when the original deficit and tag entropy do. Markov’s inequality proves the last
assertion. No lower bound on an individual cell probability is used for this selection.

Lemma 4.4 (Greedy cap regularization). After a subpower splitting and removal of probability
o(1), a particle law can be restricted to classes with a bounded number h such that

                        Pr(cap(D)) ≤ ϵw(D)−h−o(1) ,        τL (T ) = h + o(1).                 (4.4)
                        T

The cap bound is uniform over the root menu and its fixed enlargements. For a further law
Q ≤ ϵ−η PrT on such a class, τL (Q) ≤ h + η + o(1). Hence a subpower restriction or tilt preserving
the displayed score profiles preserves saturation.

                                                 20
Proof. In the finite root menu, repeatedly select a cap maximizing w(D) + log1/ϵ (µrem (cap(D)))
and remove its remaining contents. Selected contents Ei are disjoint and cover the law. A selected
cap cannot be selected again with positive mass, so the procedure is finite. The successive maxima
hi decrease. Split the indices into intervals [h, h + ξ] and keep each entire Ei in its bin. At the
first removal in this bin every cap has remaining mass at most ϵw(D)−h−ξ . The absolute mass of
its intersection with the whole bin has the same upper bound.
     Let the bin have probability p. Under its normalized law every cap thus has mass at most
  −1
p ϵw(D)−h−ξ . The deterministic assigned-cap test has score
                 X µ(Ei )                           µ(Ei )
                                                             
                               w(Di ) + log1/ϵ                     = E[hi | bin] + log1/ϵ (1/p) .
                i in bin
                           p                           p

Conversely, for any compatible stochastic test D, conditional relative entropy on its cap gives
                                                                  1
                                                                           
                                 I(T ; D) ≥ E log1/ϵ                     .
                                                            PrT (cap(D))

The two estimates put the conditional root supremum between h+log1/ϵ (1/p) and h+ξ+log1/ϵ (1/p),
up to the menu ambiguity. The same inequality proves the assertion about Q.
    For completeness, very negative bins can be discarded: a fixed number of zero-depth caps
cover the domain, so a remaining maximum below −λ forces remaining probability at most Cϵλ .
The other maxima are at most 2L + o(1). Choose the mesh slowly, and discard bins of mass
less than ϵηϵ , where ηϵ ↓ 0 slowly enough that the number of bins times ϵηϵ tends to zero. Then
log1/ϵ (1/p) = o(1) on every retained bin. This proves (4.4). Apply Lemma 4.3 when the input is
saturated. Before any later subpower tilt, impose the uniform probability bins of Lemma 2.4; its
conditional relative-entropy estimate preserves the stipulated profiles. Their score tends to βt + h,
while the cap bound gives τL (Q) ≤ h + o(1) and (4.3) gives the reverse inequality.

4.3   Anisotropic localization
Lemma 4.5 (Localization and composition). Let G have fixed shape (b, a) at t, put e = a − b,
and fix 0 ≤ r < t, 0 ≤ d ≤ e. Let J be the aperture at r of shape (b, b + d) whose frame is the
depth-d prefix of the frame of G. Set ζ = H(Rd | T, Cr ). Conditional on J, there are bounded line
systems with final shape (0, e − d), duration t − r, and horizon L′ = e − d + t − r. With τJ′ their
root suprema,

                                              Ft (G) ≤ β(t − r) + w(b, b + d) − I(T ; J)
                                                               + 2Sr + EτJ′ + 2ζ + o(1),             (4.5)
                w(b, b + d) − I(T ; J) + 2Sr + EτJ′ ≤ βr + τL (T ) + o(1).                           (4.6)

Here parameters are fixed before ϵ → 0. Uniform versions have errors Cξ + C log(C0 )/ log(1/ϵ) + ωϵ
for depth-rounding mesh ξ, fixed enlargement bound C0 , and the compact parameter bounds in
(4.3). If ζ = o(1) and G is saturated, typical conditional systems are saturated and near-maximizing
compositions give a saturated predecessor at r > 0.

Proof. Write vJ , xJ for the velocity and position representatives of J, cJ for its time representative,
and S = QRd diag(ϵb , ϵb+d ). The coordinates

               v ′ = S −1 (V − vJ ),   a′ = ϵ−r S −1 (A + cJ V − xJ ),          c′ = ϵ−r (c − cJ )


                                                       21
are bounded on this conditional system, and its trajectory is a′ + c′ v ′ . More explicitly,

                          a′ + c′ v ′ = ϵ−r S −1 A + cV − xJ − (c − cJ )vJ ,
                                                                          


so the moving anchor being subtracted is known from J. Both columns use the same spatial
map; the position column has the additional factor ϵ−r . If θ is the angle from the coarse frame to
the final frame, then | sin θ| ≤ Cϵd . In these units the final velocity uncertainty matrix, with its
harmless scalar factor removed, is

                                   N = diag(1, ϵ−d )Qθ diag(1, ϵe ).

Its largest singular value lies between positive fixed constants: the first column has length at least
one and all entries are bounded. Its determinant has absolute value ϵe−d . Its other singular value
is therefore comparable to ϵe−d . The final position matrix is ϵt−r N . This proves the asserted final
aperture.
    The same calculation proves the needed determination of J by G. Rotate the velocity uncertainty
to the coarse frame, coarsen its normal width from a to b+d, and transport the position uncertainty
from Ct to Cr . Since |Ct − Cr | ≤ Cϵr , the transported velocity uncertainty fits the position widths
ϵb+r , ϵb+d+r . Thus only a bounded number of cells is possible. This argument compares uncertainty
rectangles, and imposes no bound on the displacement of their centers. In particular

                           H(J | G) = o(1),       H(J | T, Cr ) ≤ ζ + o(1).

After adding the actual transformed cells at bounded cost, the final label G′ satisfies I(T ; J) +
I(T ; G′ | J) ≤ I(T ; G) + o(1). Also, since J includes Cr ,

                      0 ≤ St − Sr − H(Ct | T, J) = I(Ct ; J | T, Cr ) ≤ ζ + o(1).

The local final weight is exactly w(0, e − d) = w(b, a) − w(b, b + d). Apply (4.3) in each conditional
system and average; these identities give (4.5).
    For composition, take a local root test of shape (B, A), 0 ≤ B ≤ A ≤ L′ , and frame Qϕ . Its
global uncertainty is
                                       M = SQϕ diag(ϵB , ϵA ).
Let p ≤ q be its two singular-value depths. The determinant identity, the upper bound for the
largest singular value, the lower bound for the smallest one, and submultiplicativity of the condition
number give

                  p + q = 2b + d + A + B + o(1),        q − p ≤ d + A − B + o(1),
                  p ≥ b + B − o(1),       q ≤ b + d + A + o(1) ≤ a + t − r + o(1).

Consequently
                   w(p, q) ≥ w(b, b + d) + w(B, A) − o(1),       q + r ≤ L + o(1).
The position column has the same singular directions and the extra factor ϵr , so this is an
admissible global aperture at r. Rounding its depths down removes the displayed horizon slack at
a vanishing cost. Include J in the composed label and add its actual cells at bounded ambiguity.
Its information is at most I(T ; J) + I(T ; D′ | J) + o(1). Choose conditional root tests within any
prescribed η > 0 of τJ′ , apply the global bound at r, and then let η ↓ 0. At r = 0 use the defining
root supremum instead. This proves (4.6).


                                                  22
    Finally, if both endpoints of these inequalities agree in the limit, their nonnegative limiting
deficits have zero mean. The uniform lower bounds on all conditional deficits permit the same
Markov selection as in Lemma 4.3. The composition of the chosen conditional root tests gives the
predecessor assertion. The finite errors quoted in the statement come respectively from rounding,
bounded covering multiplicities, and (4.3).

   The case r = d = 0 removes the common depth b. Replacing the local horizon by its smallest
permitted value e + t cannot lower a score minus its root supremum; the universal upper bound
then forces saturation. Replacing ϵ by ϵt makes the duration one and divides all depths, weights,
and normalized entropies by t.

4.4   The least aspect and saturated predecessors
We first isolate the rectangle comparison used twice below. Two equal-shape apertures (b, b + e)
with angular separation of depth v ∈ [0, e] have a common coarsening of shape (b, b + v) and
an intersection aperture of shape (b + e − v, b + e). The common label is determined by either
original label, once an angle-depth bin of width ξ is supplied, at information cost O(ξ) + o(1).
For the intersection, one normal constraint has width ϵb+e ; solving the other normal constraint
along its tangent gives width Cϵb+e /| sin θ|. The safe endpoint of the angle bin gives the asserted
tangent depth with an O(ξ) loss. Coarsening to normal depth b + v proves the common-rectangle
assertion. These arguments apply separately to both columns, with the common time factor when
the duration is positive. The weight gain is

                   w(b + e − v, b + e) + w(b, b + v) − 2w(b, b + e) = 2γ(e − v).                      (4.7)

   Figure 1 shows the two rectangles responsible for this weight gain.

                   Common coarsening                                Intersection
                                   D2                                                       D2

                                      θ ≍ ϵv   D1                                                D1
          ϵb+v                                                                         ϵa

                                                                         ϵa−v
                                                            each original aperture:    ϵb × ϵa
                                  b
                              ϵ
                       depths (b, b + v)                           depths (a − v, a)


Figure 1: Aperture geometry for two common-shape observations. The centered schematic has
0 < ϵ < 1, b ≤ a and 0 ≤ v ≤ a − b, with angle θ ≍ ϵv . Here a = b + e in the notation of (4.7);
the two comparison rectangles give its weight gain. The dashed common rectangle contains both
apertures up to fixed factors; the shaded intersection is contained in a rectangle with depths
(a − v, a). All indicated side lengths are orders of magnitude, and the picture displays an interior
value of v. The same calculation applies to the position column at duration r after multiplying
both side lengths by ϵr .

Proposition 4.6 (Least-aspect limiting extremizer). There exists a saturated sequence at duration
one with b = 0, endpoint aspect e = m, and horizon 1 + m, where m ≥ 0 is the least aspect of any


                                                    23
such saturated sequence. One may moreover require Yj conditional on Xj to be homogeneous for a
prescribed countable list of finite pairs (Xj , Yj ), provided that for each fixed j there is 0 ≤ Aj < ∞,
independent of ϵ, such that |Yj | ≤ ϵ−Aj for all sufficiently small ϵ. No cardinality bound is imposed
on Xj , which may include the exact particle T .

Proof. Choose systems whose normalized rates tend to β, split by narrow shape bins, remove b
by localization, and normalize time. Each individual inner sequence now has a fixed finite aspect
e; its rate defect can be made arbitrarily small. We need a bound for e independent of which
sequence was selected.
    Sample two final labels independently given T , and let D1 , D2 be their root coarsenings of
shape (0, e). If the rate defect is η, then, since S1 ≤ 1 + o(1) and β ≥ 0,

                             I(T ; Dj ) ≤ (1 − γ)e − τL (T ) + 2 + η + o(1).

Tag their separation depth v on a fixed mesh and use the common and intersection labels above.
Conditional independence gives

  2e − (1 + γ)Ev − τL (T ) ≤ I(T ; D1 , D2 ) + O(ξ) + o(1)
                                                                                   
                            ≤ 2 (1 − γ)e − τL (T ) + 2 + η − (1 − γ)Ev − τL (T ) + O(ξ) + o(1).

Indeed the information identity for the pair subtracts I(D1 ; D2 ), which is at least the common-label
entropy minus its two determination errors. That entropy is at least its particle information, and
the root test bounds the latter below by its weight minus τL . The separation tag has entropy o(1)
at each fixed mesh. Rearrangement yields

                                 2γE(e − v) ≤ 4 + 2η + O(ξ) + o(1).

Thus a fixed K = Kγ < ∞ gives a positive, uniformly bounded below probability of agreement
within a bounded number of cells at depth (e − K)+ . For each T , maximize the conditional
probability of a coarse orientation cell. The conditional collision probability of two independent
draws is at most a fixed constant times this maximum. This deterministic predictor therefore
succeeds, to bounded cell ambiguity, on an event of probability at least cγ > 0. On that event
the coarse orientation has conditional entropy o(1) given T . Binary splitting preserves a rate
defect tending to zero on this event. Localization with r = 0, d = (e − K)+ produces a conditional
system with residual aspect at most K and rate defect tending to zero. Uniform conditional deficit
bounds justify selecting one such system even when the conditioning cells are numerous.
    It remains to take the least aspect. The preceding construction, followed by a diagonal,
produces at least one saturated sequence with bounded aspect and reduced horizon. Let m be the
infimum of its possible limiting aspects. Choose such sequences with aspects ej → m. For the jth
sequence choose its precision small enough that its score defect, finite-menu errors, and the errors
for the first j requested profiles are less than 1/j. Coarsen an endpoint of aspect ej to aspect
m when ej ≥ m; if needed allow a depth error |ej − m| before this coarsening. The score loses
at most Cγ |ej − m| + o(1), whereas reducing the horizon to 1 + m reduces the subtracted root
supremum. The universal bound forces the resulting diagonal to saturate. For the homogeneity
assertion, apply Lemma 2.4 to finite initial lists of these pairs under the stated bounds on the child
alphabets, and choose their meshes and errors before the corresponding precision. Lemma 2.9
then retains the countable list. In the applications below the children are finite tuples of grid
observations at fixed depths in bounded coordinates; the exact particle and other unrestricted
conditioning data occur only among the parents.

                                                   24
Proposition 4.7 (Saturated path and coherence). The sequence in Proposition 4.6 can carry,
on one joint law of (T, c), saturated observations Gu at every positive dyadic time u ≤ 1. Their
shapes satisfy

b(1) = 0,   a(1) = m,     b(r) ≥ b(t),   a(r) + r ≤ a(t) + t    (r < t),        e(u) := a(u) − b(u) ≥ mu.

They can be constructed so that H(Re(u) | T, Cu ) = o(1). There is a constant Kγ , independent of
r, t, for which the orientations at r < t agree with probability tending to one at every positive depth
                                                                                      
                   d < min(e(r), e(t)) − Kγ |b(r) − b(t)| + |a(r) − a(t)| + t − r .                 (4.8)

All statements refer to the inner ϵ limit with the times fixed.

Proof. On a finite dyadic mesh, work backwards from 1 using Lemma 4.5 with d = 0. A composed
predecessor has minimum depth at least the later b and maximum depth at most the later a plus
the time difference. Split its variable shape into narrow bins and use Lemma 4.3 to preserve all
equalities already imposed. Drop redundant labels. Before making this split, sample each incoming
predecessor from its kernel given (T, Cr ), independently of the previously retained finer time and
later labels. This operation preserves its law with (T, Cr ) and preserves the entire later joint law.
In particular the underlying time variable is never resampled.
    Subsequent finite binning and trimming introduce at most their normalized entropy or relative-
entropy cost into the averaged conditional mutual information, by Lemma 2.3. Retain typical
parts on which this cost and all finitely many score defects are bounded by a common deterministic
qϵ → 0, using Markov thresholds fixed before selecting parts. The same lemma justifies the kernel
resampling with base (T, Cr ); at this stage no observation involving a free replica is fixed. Passing
to finer finite meshes, and then to a diagonal, gives the asserted common sequence and its dyadic
shapes. Shape bounds follow from the fixed horizon. A saturated observation at u, localized with
r = d = 0 and normalized to duration one, has aspect e(u)/u; minimality gives e(u) ≥ mu.
    Here are details of coherence. Put D = |b(r) − b(t)| + |a(r) − a(t)| + t − r. Convert the
observation at t to the shape (b(r), a(r)) at r, using its recorded frame rounded to the target
precision and recording the actual target cells. Its additional conditional log-cardinality is at most
CD + o(1). To check this bound, rotate the old uncertainty into the new frame: the tangent
uncertainty entering the normal coordinate has exponent at least b(t) + e(r) ≥ a(r) − |b(t) − b(r)|.
The other side-length changes cost at most the absolute differences of the two depths. Transport
from Ct to Cr adds ϵr times the velocity uncertainty, which has the same bound in position units
divided by ϵr . Counting the finer cells in each resulting rectangle gives the stated estimate. The
weight changes by at most 2D, and St − Sr ≤ t − r + o(1). Thus this converted test and the
original test at r have total deficit at most Cγ D + o(1) at duration r.
    Call these two labels D1 , D2 , their common label Dc , and their intersection label Di . They
contain Cr , and their remaining coordinates are deterministic functions of T, Cr and their respective
orientations. The resampling construction therefore gives

                           I(D1 ; D2 | T ) ≤ Sr + o(1),      H(Dc | T ) ≥ Sr .

The pair information identity and common-label determination imply

               I(T ; Di ) ≤ I(T ; D1 ) + I(T ; D2 ) − H(Dc ) + I(D1 ; D2 | T ) + O(ξ) + o(1)
                        ≤ I(T ; D1 ) + I(T ; D2 ) − I(T ; Dc ) + O(ξ) + o(1).


                                                    25
Both Dc , Di have the same time Cr and obey the original horizon. Apply the universal score
bound to each. Their two time contributions cancel the corresponding contributions in D1 , D2 ,
giving
                             2γE(e(r) − v) ≤ Cγ D + O(ξ) + o(1).
This inequality also holds on any separation event of fixed positive limiting probability: binary
splitting preserves the two original saturations, and the extra conditional dependence costs only
o(1). On the event v ≤ d its left side is at least 2γ(e(r) − d). Choose Kγ larger than the preceding
constant divided by 2γ, and then let the angle mesh tend to zero. A positive limiting probability
of separation at a depth in (4.8) is impossible.
    Apply the same comparison to two independent replicas of a single label given (T, Cu ). With
D = 0 they agree at every depth e(u) − ν, ν > 0, with probability tending to one. For each
base value, a coarse cell and its bounded neighbors then contain all but o(1) of the conditional
orientation mass on average. Choose such a cell deterministically from the base. The entropy
bound obtained by separating this event from its complement is o(1) at depth e(u) − ν; refining the
orientation costs at most ν + o(1). Let ν ↓ 0. This proves the asserted zero conditional orientation
entropy.

4.5    Isotropic tangents or a matched aspect
Proposition 4.8 (Tangent reduction). One of the following alternatives holds for the least aspect
m.
  1. If m = 0, there is a saturated sequence of duration and horizon one, with isotropic endpoint
     (b, a) = (0, 0) and homogeneous conditional time profile

                            H(Cu | T ) = su + o(1),        0 ≤ u ≤ 1,     0 ≤ s ≤ 1.

  2. If m > 0, the saturated path can be represented by a single orientation R with depth-mu
     prefixes at time u, for every 0 < u ≤ 1, and H(Rmu | T, Cu ) = o(1). Its shapes satisfy

                    e(u) = mu,        b(u) is Lipschitz,      0 ≤ −b′ (u) ≤ 1 + m      a.e.

      There is κ > 0 such that its limiting conditional orientation entropies obey

                             H(Rm(u+h) | T, Cu ) ≥ κh,        0 < u < u + h < 1.                 (4.9)

      The alternatives and the positive lower bound persist after homogeneous splittings that preserve
      the saturated path.
Proof. The nesting inequalities make b nonincreasing and a(u) + u nondecreasing; extend them
to their continuity points. In particular e(u) + u is nondecreasing, so e has bounded variation
and its singular distributional derivative is nonnegative. Choose a common differentiability point
x ∈ (0, 1) for these functions. Take dyadic r < x < t with η = t − r ↓ 0 and with both distances
from x comparable to η. Differentiability gives uniform linear approximations throughout [r, t],
with error o(η). Choose a finite dyadic chain

                                     r = u0 < u1 < · · · < uN = t

whose largest interval length is o(η), and put

           Dj = |b(uj+1 ) − b(uj )| + |a(uj+1 ) − a(uj )| + uj+1 − uj ,      Dmax = max Dj .
                                                                                       j


                                                   26
The uniform linear approximations give Dmax ≤ C maxj (uj+1 − uj ) + o(η) = o(η). Here the chain
may become longer as η ↓ 0, but it is finite and fixed at each inner small-parameter limit.
   Let emin = minj e(uj ) and choose a positive δη = o(η). Set
                                                                   
                                    d = emin − Kγ Dmax − δη + .

When d > 0, it lies strictly below the coherence depth for every adjacent pair in the chain.
Equation (4.8) therefore makes all their frames agree at depth d, up to bounded neighboring cells,
with probability tending to one. Transitivity costs at most a fixed number depending on this chain
of neighboring cells, and the union of its finitely many exceptional events still has probability
tending to zero. The frame at r is determined by (T, Cr ) to zero normalized entropy at this depth.
The entropy ambiguity bound hence gives the same determination for the terminal depth-d frame.
If d = 0, no orientation information is required.
    The uniform linear approximation for e also gives

                                 e(t) − emin ≤ max(e′ (x), 0)η + o(η).

Since emin ≥ 0, the definition of d yields, in either case,

                  e(t) − d ≤ e(t) − emin + Kγ Dmax + δη ≤ max(e′ (x), 0)η + o(η).

All depths, the chain, and its angular mesh are chosen before the inner limit. The depth loss
is Kγ Dmax at this one common depth; the number of links enters only the zero-cost angular
ambiguity.
    Localize the terminal observation at r, using its common depth b(t) and this d. Its local
horizon is η + e(t) − d. Saturation and zero conditional orientation entropy give saturated
conditional systems. With small parameter ρ = ϵη , their duration is one and their aspect is at most
max(e′ (x), 0) + o(1). All local coordinate bounds are uniform, and their horizons stay bounded.
Minimality of m therefore implies m ≤ max(e′ (x), 0). If m > 0, this yields e′ (x) ≥ m almost
everywhere. Since the singular derivative of e is nonnegative,
                                                Z 1
                                e(1) − e(u) ≥         e′ (v) dv ≥ m(1 − u).
                                                 u

Together with e(u) ≥ mu and e(1) = m, this proves e(u) = mu. The original nesting inequalities
now give
                              0 ≤ b(r) − b(t) ≤ (1 + m)(t − r),
which proves the Lipschitz assertion and extends the path to every time.
    We specify the selection of shrinking-scale limits, also for later use. For the jth interval first
fix its endpoints, its finite coherence chain, and the first j time-prefix fractions. Homogenize their
joint and conditional probability bins, then choose the inner precision so that all depth errors,
normalized conditional entropies, uniform-bound errors, and retained score deficits are less than
ηj /j 2 . Use uniform typical sets before any further restriction. The sum of the conditional deficits is
then o(ηj ) in mean; select typical localization cells with each required error o(ηj ). Only afterwards
divide by ηj and take the outer diagonal. This is an application of Lemma 2.9 with the error
measured in the new units. No modulus of differentiability uniform over profiles is used.
     If m = 0, use a common regular point with e′ ≤ 0 in the tangent construction above. The set of
such points has positive measure: otherwise e′ > 0 almost everywhere, and its nonnegative singular
derivative would force e(1) > e(u) ≥ 0, contrary to e(1) = 0. The nondecreasing 1-Lipschitz

homogeneous time profile Su is differentiable almost everywhere, so the point can also be chosen

    Intersect these cells with the typical localization cells above. Since Cr is fixed by J and the local
time grids correspond to Cq up to bounded ambiguity, the estimates are conditional on the local
particle. Differentiability gives (Sq − Sr )/η = ks + o(1). The residual aspect and excess horizon
are o(1) in the new units; coarsen these vanishing depth errors and apply the universal bound to
recover a saturated isotropic sequence. Increasing the finite prefix lists gives the homogeneous
linear time profile and proves the first alternative.

                                                      28
    Suppose henceforth m > 0. Fix a dyadic u > 0. On a sufficiently fine finite chain from u to
1, coherence and the Lipschitz width bounds identify all frames at every depth strictly below
mu. The terminal orientation R thus determines the frame at u at that precision. Let the gap
from mu tend to zero. Both the old and new rectangles then determine each other with vanishing
log-cardinality error; recording the same data in the endpoint-prefix frame preserves its score.
Taking a diagonal over dyadic u, and then using Lipschitz interpolation of the widths and time
grids, supplies the common-prefix representation at every u.
    Finally, if no κ > 0 satisfies (4.9), there are fixed intervals (uj , uj + hj ), chosen after their inner
limits, with
                                    h−1
                                      j H(Rm(uj +hj ) | T, Cuj ) −→ 0.

Apply Lemma 4.5 to the saturated observation at uj + hj , with r = uj , b = b(uj + hj ), and
d = e(uj + hj ). Its conditional final shape is exactly isotropic and its local horizon is hj . The error
2ζ in (4.5) is o(hj ). The other composition errors are made o(hj ) by first fixing the interval and
then choosing the inner precision. The local deficits consequently have mean o(hj ) and a uniform
lower bound of the same order. Select a local system with deficit o(hj ) and replace ϵ by ϵhj . This
produces a saturated isotropic sequence of horizon one, contrary to m > 0. This contradiction
proves the uniform positive constant. It uses neither a positive lower bound for hj nor one for uj :
both are fixed positive numbers at their own inner stage.
    The same arguments apply after any homogeneous splitting preserving the path’s scores. Such
a path is still a least-aspect extremizer, so the final contradiction again supplies a positive constant.
One can also fix a projective orientation chart by a finite split. In that chart use rows (1, 0) and
(−R, 1), with bounded slope R and nested slope prefixes. The row frame and orthogonal frame
have bounded condition number, angular and slope precisions are comparable, and the resulting
rectangle observations differ by bounded covering multiplicities. All conclusions therefore hold in
this row convention.

Remark 4.9 (Homogeneous profiles for subsequent local arguments). In either alternative of
Proposition 4.8, one may first impose a fixed finite list of pairs (Xj , Yj ) satisfying |Yj | ≤ ϵ−Aj
for fixed 0 ≤ Aj < ∞ and all sufficiently small ϵ. The children may be joint grid observations
or geometric prefixes. No cardinality bound is imposed on the conditioning variables Xj , which
may include T . Apply Lemma 2.4 and Lemma 4.3, select a saturated homogeneous component,
and only then define its limiting profiles and choose a differentiability or trace point. The profiles
need not equal those before this initial splitting. The positive constant in (4.9) is obtained for the
selected component by the same least-aspect contradiction.
    To spell out the later inheritance, suppose homogeneous probability bins for a parent P and
a refinement Q have exponents dP , dQ with error η; both may be conditional on an additional
base W , including the exact particle. Here dP is the exponent for the child P , and dQ is the
exponent for the joint child (P, Q), identified with Q when Q already records P ; both children are
conditional on W . If the exact particle is present, it belongs to W . On their common uniform
typical set, ratios of joint and parent masses give

     Pr(Q = q | P = p, W = w) ≤ ϵdQ −dP −2η ,             #{q : (p, q, w) retained} ≤ ϵ−(dQ −dP )−2η .

These are respectively an upper conditional mass and an upper retained support count; they are
stronger than an averaged entropy inequality. If a subsequent normalized law ν obeys ν ≤ ϵ−θ µ,
discard parent values whose marginal likelihood νP,W /µP,W is below ϵλ . Their ν-probability is
at most ϵλ , and on the remaining parents the conditional mass bound loses at most θ + λ in its
exponent. Support counts are inherited under restriction. The conditional relative-entropy identity

                                                     29
in Lemma 2.4 supplies the corresponding lower entropy bound with loss θ. There is no factor
depending on the number of parent values. Repeat this operation for the fixed finite list, then take
a diagonal. For a block of length h, choose η, θ, λ = o(h) at its inner stage before using ρ = ϵh as
the small parameter. Cap regularization may be imposed at the same stage to retain the root
bound (4.4) under these operations.


5     Exclusion of isotropic extremizers
We exclude the isotropic alternative in Proposition 4.8. The argument has two geometric steps.
Three sufficiently separated slopes would force more point entropy than saturation allows, so one
can retain a slope population in a thin strip. That strip supplies an earlier anisotropic aperture; a
two-slope projection estimate in the remaining block then forces a conditional time rate greater
than one. Throughout both changes of scale we retain uniform conditional probability bounds on
particle fibers, as well as the corresponding entropy inequalities.

Proposition 5.1 (Isotropic exclusion). Suppose that 0 < γ < 1 and that the classical universal
growth rate satisfies β > γ. There is no saturated isotropic system of duration and horizon 1
furnished by Proposition 4.8, with homogeneous conditional time profile H(Cu | T ) = su, 0 ≤ u ≤ 1.

    Here and below, a limiting assertion about homogeneous probabilities is used on the trimmed
laws of Lemma 2.4. In particular, it supplies uniform probability bounds on the retained joint
population. An exceptional set of small probability in an untrimmed conditional law is not treated
as a uniform probability bound. We give the finite estimates that implement this distinction in
the present argument.

5.1   Finite incidence counts and regular increments
In the next two elementary estimates ρ is a mesh size, and all coordinates belong to a fixed
bounded set. Constants may depend on that set, but not on ρ. A point cell in dimension d + 1 has
coordinates (c, x) ∈ R × Rd , and a slope bin has representative v ∈ Rd and diameter O(ρ). The
intercept map is
                                        πv (c, x) = x − cv.

Lemma 5.2 (Counting intercepts on retained fibers). Suppose an incidence population uses at
most N point cells. For every retained exact particle and slope bin incident to it, suppose there are
at least m distinct retained time children in the same base cell and slope bin. Suppose further that
these children lie on one line of true slope within O(ρ) of the bin representative. Then, for each
slope bin, all its retained incidences use at most CN/m intercept cells of side ρ.

Proof. Fix the slope bin. A particle’s intercept x − cv varies by O(ρ) along its retained children.
Its distinct time children give distinct point cells. For every occupied intercept cell b, choose one
particle witnessing an incidence in b. The particle’s m point cells have intercepts in a fixed bounded
enlargement of b. A point cell can belong to the enlargements of only C intercept cells, since v is
fixed. Counting the pairs consisting of an occupied intercept cell and one of these witnesses gives
m#{b} ≤ CN . This counts point cells, and imposes no bound on the number or entropy of exact
particles. The estimate concerns the population before replica labels are fixed; its support upper
bound continues to hold on every subsequent subset.




                                                 30
   We will use the following explicit form of the star calculation in Theorem 2.7. Let q be a
normalized point–slope law with actual marginals µ, ν, and suppose

                                  q(p, v) ≤ Kµ(p)ν(v),                 µ(p) ≤ M −1 .                    (5.1)

Sample r slopes independently conditional on p. The resulting law qr satisfies both comparisons
