These follow by multiplying q(vi | p) ≤ Kν(vi ), keeping one factor unestimated for the second
inequality. If λ is the slope-tuple marginal, then the tuples on which λ/ i ν(vi ) < L−1 have
                                                                          Q

λ-mass at most L−1 , by summing against the product probability. Outside that set,

                                        qr (p | v1 , . . . , vr ) ≤ K r Lµ(p).                          (5.4)

Thus fixing a typical tuple leaves at least M/(K r L) point cells. An event of independent slope
probability η has star probability at most K r η. All applications below first trim pairs, discard
point fibers of inadequate retention, and replace comparison marginals by the actual retained
marginals using Theorem 2.7. Consequently (5.1) holds for the normalized retained law. The fixed
labels will always be precisely these two or three slope replicas; no cyclic incidence configuration is
used.
Lemma 5.3 (Two elementary projection inequalities). Let E be a set of occupied ρ-cells in a fixed
bounded region.
   1. In R3 , let v1 , v2 , v3 ∈ R2 be bounded slopes and put D = |det((1, v1 ), (1, v2 ), (1, v3 ))| > 0. If
      πvi (E) uses at most Ni cells, with bounded enlargements permitted, then

                                                  |E|2 ≤ CD−6 N1 N2 N3 .                                (5.5)

   2. In R2 , for two bounded scalar slopes v1 , v2 with |v1 − v2 | ≥ ρa , if their intercepts use at most
      N1 , N2 cells, then
                                            |E| ≤ Cρ−2a N1 N2 .                                       (5.6)
Proof. For the first assertion choose a witness point in each occupied cell, and let U have columns
(1, vi ). Replace each U −1 z by its ρ-grid cell, obtaining a finite grid set Y . Since ∥U ∥ ≤ C, each
cell of Y accounts for at most C original cells, so |E| ≤ C|Y |. The map πvi U annihilates the ith
coordinate. On the other two coordinates it is an invertible matrix of determinant of absolute
value D and inverse norm at most C/D. Thus each original intercept cell accounts for at most
CD−2 cells of the coordinate projection of Y omitting coordinate i. Rounding U −1 z adds only
O(ρ) to its intercept, because πvi U has bounded norm.
     For completeness, the discrete Loomis–Whitney inequality [18] used here is

                                          |Y |2 ≤ |π12 Y ||π13 Y ||π23 Y |.

To see it, write fij for the indicators of the three projected sets. The sum

    Our anchored observations are nested only to bounded ambiguity. Formula (5.7) is applied
to their limiting profiles. Its exact finite counterpart can instead use the nested dyadic cells of
the physical point (c, A + cV ): at every fixed depth u these and (Cu , [A + Cu V ]u ) determine each
other with a bounded number of possibilities. All entropy differences and conditional mutual
informations consequently differ by O(1/ log(1/ϵ)). This error is taken to zero before h.

5.2   The isotropic entropy bounds
For a vector Y , write [Y ]d or Yd for its isotropic grid observation at scale ϵd . Assume the system
in Proposition 5.1 exists. Write

                      Ou = (Cu , [A + Cu V ]u ),         O = O1 ,     α = 2 − 2s + β.

The exact particle T determines A, V . In an anchored observation it leaves only the time label
random, so H(Ou | T ) = su. Write τ for the root supremum, equivalently its uniformized cap level
in the limiting system. The terminal weight is zero. Saturation therefore gives

                               β = −I(T ; O) + 2s − τ = 3s − H(O) − τ.                             (5.8)

Since τ ≥ 0 and I(T ; O) ≥ 0, we have β ≤ 2s and

                          H(O) ≤ 3s − β,            H(Vd | O) ≥ αd    (0 ≤ d ≤ 1).                 (5.9)

To prove the second inequality, test at duration 1 − d with velocity depths (d, d) and position
depths (1, 1). Denote this observation by O.
                                          e Its weight is 2d, and its conditional time entropy is

    Homogenize jointly O, Vd , and their conditioning variables, for the finite lists of depths used

                                     P(Vd = q | O = o) ≤ ϵαd−η                                   (5.10)

5.3   Concentration of slopes in a thin strip
The profile u 7→ H(Ou ) is nondecreasing and 3-Lipschitz, starts at zero, and has endpoint at most
3s − β. There is consequently a set of positive measure of regular points k ∈ (0, 1) with

                                      p := ∂k H(Ok ) < 3s − β/2.                                 (5.11)

Choose such a point satisfying (5.7) with Xh = Vh , whose entropy is at most 2h. Along a sequence
h ↓ 0,
                   H(Ok+h | Ok ) = (p + o(1))h,       I(Vh ; Ok+h | Ok ) = o(h).            (5.12)
    Inside a typical Ok -cell translate time and position to the cell center and dilate both by ϵ−k .
Put ρ = ϵh , and call the child point observation P . Bounded changes from anchored to physical
point cells do not affect any exponent. Homogeneity, the conditional fiber rule, and Lemma 2.5,
followed by the actual-marginal conclusion of Theorem 2.7, give a normalized retained pair law
q(P, Vh ) satisfying the following bounds, with e = e(h) → 0:

            # supp P ≤ ρ−(p+e) ,              µ(P = z) ≤ ρp−e ,             q ≤ ρ−e µ ⊗ ν.       (5.13)

These conclusions hold outside base cells of probability tending to zero. One may enlarge e(h)
to absorb all fixed numerical constants and all o(1) errors in (5.12). To be explicit about the
conditioning: the mean information in block units tends to zero; discard base cells where it exceeds
its square root, apply the likelihood-ratio truncation in each remaining cell, and discard point
fibers losing more than half their mass. Homogeneous joint and marginal bounds then supply the
two point estimates in (5.13). This procedure introduces only another quantity tending to zero
into e.
    We also trim on the exact particle fibers before sampling replicas. A retained time child,
conditional on T, Ck in the original base law, has probability at most ρs−e . Lift all pair restrictions
to that law, and discard particle fibers whose conditional retention there is below ρe . If the pair
restrictions have total retention m0 , these discarded fibers have normalized retained mass at most
ρe /m0 , by (2.8). Here m0 is bounded below before this step; more generally a subpower lower
bound is absorbed by increasing e. Each remaining particle has at least ρ−s+2e distinct retained
time children, by dividing its retained mass by the original child mass bound. Given T, Ck , the
anchored base observation is fixed. Thus this is a statement in the very same base cell used for P ;
no particle entropy bound is involved. For a slope bin of size ρ, all these points have intercept
within O(ρ) of the particle’s intercept. Lemma 5.2 gives

                           #πv (retained neighbors of v) ≤ Cρ−(p−s+3e) .                         (5.14)


                                                  33
Apply one final simultaneous marginal core to this pruned law. Its retention is at least one half, by
Lemma 2.6, and the pair comparison again uses its actual point and slope marginals. Increasing e
absorbs the finite factors, so (5.13) and (5.14) hold together for this final pair law. The core need
not preserve the rich particle fibers: the intercept support bound was established before it, and
survives by support inclusion. Use this same order of operations in the two-replica application
below.
    Fix ϑ > 0 so small that 12ϑ < β/8. We claim that, in every retained base cell, some strip
of thickness Cρϑ has slope mass at least ρ5e , after enlarging e(h) → 0 once more. If all such
strips had mass less than ρ5e , two independent slopes would have distance at least ρϑ except with
probability Cρ5e ; a ball of that radius is contained in a strip of comparable thickness. Conditional
on the first two, the third would lie at distance at least ρϑ from their line except with the same
probability. Hence, outside an independent event of probability Cρ5e ,

                                 |det((1, v1 ), (1, v2 ), (1, v3 ))| ≥ ρ2ϑ .

For the three-replica star, (5.2) bounds the exceptional probability by Cρ2e . Choose L = ρ−e in
(5.4). A good tuple then leaves a point population of at least ρ−p+5e cells, and each projection has
the support bound (5.14). Lemma 5.3 implies

      2p − 10e ≤ 3(p − s + 3e) + 12ϑ + oρ→0 (1),          hence        p ≥ 3s − 12ϑ − 19e − oρ→0 (1).

This contradicts (5.11) for all sufficiently small h, with ρ taken sufficiently small afterwards. We
take e > 0 even when an error vanishes, so that the exceptional probabilities above tend to zero in
this inner limit.
    Choose one such strip measurably in each base cell, using a finite grid menu with bounded
enlargement, and restrict incidences to it. The total retention is at least ρ5e times the mass of
the retained base cells. No strip index is fixed globally: its index is a function of Ok and will be
included in the earlier test through that base label. With

                              l = ϑh,       |V · n(Ok ) − w(Ok )| ≤ Cϵl ,                           (5.15)

round the unit normal and offset at precision ϵl . This changes C only. The Radon–Nikodym bound
for the restriction is ϵ−O(eh) . Since ϑ is fixed and e(h) → 0, its normalized logarithmic cost is o(l).
    The bins for O, the required Vd , and the particle-conditional time probabilities are trimmed
before this restriction. Lemma 2.4 and its conditional retention rule therefore preserve their
exponents with errors o(l), including the particle-conditional time exponents and (5.10). The
absolute root cap bound gives τnew ≤ τ + o(l). The terminal numerator before subtraction
of τ changes by o(l), because its observation and conditional time exponents were recorded.
Consequently the new terminal score is at least β − o(l), and universality bounds it above by
β + o(l). We henceforth use this retained law and its own root supremum; its terminal score is
β + o(l).

5.4    An earlier anisotropic observation
Take h small enough that k < 1 − l, and put a = 1 − l. In the frame with normal n = n(Ok ) and
tangent n⊥ , form                                              
                               B = Ok , Oa , [(A + Ca V ) · n]1 .                       (5.16)
The coarse observation Oa locates tangent position to depth a; its normal position is additionally
located to depth 1. The strip supplies normal velocity to depth l, and tangent velocity is bounded.

                                                     34
The rounded frame is known to depth l. Thus B, with boundedly many additional data cells if
necessary, is an admissible aperture with velocity depths (0, l), duration a, horizon 1, and weight
(1 − γ)l.
    It is determined by O to bounded ambiguity. Indeed O coarsens to Ok , Oa to bounded ambiguity,
hence determines the chosen strip up to the same fixed number of choices. For its fine normal
position use
                       (A + Ca V ) · n = (A + C1 V ) · n + (Ca − C1 )w + O(ϵ),                (5.17)
where the error is bounded by Cϵa ϵl = Cϵ. Conversely every component of the anchored B is fixed
by (T, Ca ), since Ca fixes Ck . In particular,

                                         H(O | T, B) = sl + o(l).                               (5.18)

The conditional time profile on the retained law follows from the ratios of the recorded probabilities
at depths a and 1; the strip restriction costs o(l) on typical particle fibers. Adjoining standard
data cells to B uses deterministic functions of (T, Ca ) and leaves this argument unchanged.
    We prove the information saving

                                   I(T ; O) − I(T ; B) ≥ sl − o(l).                             (5.19)

The main point is a lower bound for the last block’s joint time and tangent position entropy.

5.5   Slope bounds inside the last block
For 0 ≤ u ≤ 1 let Fu record, in addition to B, time to depth a + ul and tangent position at that
time to the same depth. Within a B-cell translate time and tangent position to their depth-a
centers and dilate both by ϵ−a . The resulting coordinates are bounded, the new mesh parameter
is ρ∗ = ϵl , and the tangent slope X = V · n⊥ remains bounded. The filtration Fu has capacity
2 per unit depth. Its base F0 adds at most bounded information to B, and F1 is determined by
(O, B) to bounded ambiguity. Given T, B, its conditional entropy profile is su + o(1) in block
units, because its new data are fixed by the particle and the time cell.
    For each fixed d ∈ (0, 1] and each fixed filtration depth u, the retained conditional tangent
slope law satisfies
                                             αd−o(1)
                                                        for intervals J of length ρd∗ .
                                     
                    P X ∈ J | B, Fu ≤ ρ∗                                                        (5.20)

Here, as throughout, the inequality is uniform on a further trimmed subprobability, followed by
normalization on fibers of subpower retention. We verify this transfer, since a bare conditional
entropy inequality would not suffice.
    First apply (5.10) at velocity depth dl on the jointly trimmed law. Adjoin the finite list of
labels (B, Fu ) to O. Each such list has at most K possible values given O, where K is fixed when
the list is fixed. For a positive tolerance ζ, discard realized values whose conditional likelihood
given O is below ρζ∗ . Their total probability is at most Kρζ∗ . On the remaining values, conditioning
on the adjoined label multiplies a conditional velocity-bin probability by at most ρ−ζ      ∗ . More
explicitly, write κh = O(e(h)/ϑ) for an upper bound on all prior restriction costs in last-block
units. Whenever a retained subprobability has mass at least ρκ∗ h , discard conditioning fibers
whose retention is below ρκ∗ h +ζ . Formula (2.8) bounds their normalized retained mass by ρζ∗ ;
normalization costs at most κh + ζ in the conditional exponent. For the fixed finite number of
such operations, the total extra loss is Cκh + Cζ. It tends to zero by first taking the inner limit,
then h ↓ 0 and ζ ↓ 0. It incurs no factor involving the number of O-cells or B-cells.

                                                   35
    For a fixed B-cell, its normal strip has thickness O(ϵl ). Its intersection with a tangent interval
of length ϵdl meets only C velocity grid cells of depth dl, since d ≤ 1. Thus the same uniform
upper bound holds for this tangent interval conditional on O, B, Fu . Averaging over compatible
O-labels gives (5.20), with the explicit extra exponent loss ζ and the retention losses. Taking those
losses to zero proves the asserted form. Every bound is imposed on the retained joint population
before averaging; no exponentially small bound for untrimmed exceptional probabilities is needed.
    We now make the order of limits precise. Fix ϑ first. Choose the regular sequence h ↓ 0 above.
For each fixed h record the finite lists of O, Vd , and particle-conditional time bins before the strip
choice. Construct B, Fu and perform the transfer just proved after choosing the strip. The later
increment binnings are performed after this transfer. Take ϵ small enough that all inner mesh
and bin errors, divided by l, tend to zero. A diagonal, with h ↓ 0, gives last-block systems with
parameter ρ∗ , exact limiting time profile su, and (5.20) at a countable dense list of (u, d). Bounded
coordinate capacities extend the entropy profiles to other depths. For the conditional mass bound
at a subsequently chosen fixed filtration depth u, repeat the direct O → (B, Fu ) determination
and likelihood truncation above; it has the same bounded multiplicity. A requested slope depth
d is obtained by using recorded depths d′ < d and then letting d′ ↑ d. Thus the mass bound is
inherited from that comparison, independently of continuity of entropy. A later increment length
r > 0 is held fixed before each inner limit; only then is r ↓ 0. Thus errors o(l) from the first
construction are never divided by an unchosen infinitesimal r. No bound on H(B)/l is asserted or
needed.

5.6   Two-slope expansion and the score contradiction
We show, in the last-block limits just constructed, that

                                H(F1 | B) ≥ 2s    in units log(1/ρ∗ ).                           (5.21)

Write f (u) = H(Fu | B) in these units. It is nondecreasing and 2-Lipschitz, with f (0) = 0. Apply
(5.7) to Xr , the tangent slope prefix of depth r, conditional on B. Its entropy is at most r, so
the integrated mutual information is at most r2 . At almost every regular interior u there is
consequently a sequence r ↓ 0 for which the increment has negligible information about Xr , in
units of its own length.
    In a typical (B, Fu )-cell let P1 be the time–tangent-position increment, at mesh ρ = ρr∗ .
Homogenize its probabilities and the pair with Xr . Let p1 denote its point exponent. The
likelihood-ratio and actual-marginal steps used in (5.13) give, with e → 0 along the regular
sequence,
                   # supp P1 ≤ ρ−(p1 +e) ,    µ1 (z) ≤ ρp1 −e , q1 ≤ ρ−e µ1 ⊗ ν1 .
The particle-conditional time exponent is s. Trimming exact particle fibers and applying Lemma 5.2
therefore bounds every retained intercept support by Cρ−(p1 −s+3e) .
    Fix 0 < a0 < 1. Formula (5.20), at depth a0 r, gives the actual retained slope marginal the
interval bound ν1 (J) ≤ ραa0 −o(1) for intervals of length ρa0 . The two-replica star consequently has
|v1 − v2 | ≥ ρa0 outside a set of probability O(ραa0 −2e−o(1) ). Since α > 0, this probability tends to
zero by taking r sufficiently far along the regular sequence and then the inner small-parameter
limit. Pin a good tuple using (5.4) with L = ρ−e . At least ρ−p1 +4e point cells remain. Their
original intercept support bounds are still valid. Formula (5.6) now gives

              p1 − 4e ≤ 2(p1 − s + 3e) + 2a0 + o(1),       p1 ≥ 2s − 2a0 − 10e − o(1).


                                                  36
First let the regular-increment errors vanish, and then let a0 ↓ 0. This proves the lower entropy
rate p1 ≥ 2s.
    Here is why the cellwise argument yields f ′ (u) ≥ 2s almost everywhere, even if new homogeneous
parts have different point exponents. Their conditional increment entropies average to f (u + r) −
f (u), and all have capacity at most 2r + o(r). If f ′ (u) < 2s by a fixed amount, a positive fraction
of the conditional population must have point exponents below 2s by a smaller fixed amount.
The mean conditional information is o(r), so Markov’s inequality removes a vanishing fraction
while imposing the pair comparison above. The uniform time and slope bounds survive on the
remaining typical fibers. Homogenizing a finite list costs a subpower, and thus selects a part with
all these properties and the same fixed deficit. The preceding statement about inherited bounds
can also be checked directly: in a fixed conditional cell, restriction to a part E of mass m gives
νE (J) ≤ m−1 ν(J). Here m = ρo(1) , so the slope exponent is preserved. Discard exact particle
fibers retaining less than mρζ of their original conditional time law; their normalized retained
mass is at most ρζ . The time exponent therefore loses at most log1/ρ (1/m) + ζ = o(1) + ζ. These
estimates justify the simultaneous selection without any count of conditioning cells. The two-slope
inequality contradicts the fixed deficit. Since f is Lipschitz, integrating f ′ (u) ≥ 2s proves (5.21).
    Returning to the original units, (5.21) and the determination of F1 by (O, B) give

                                       H(O | B) ≥ 2sl − o(l).

Together with (5.18), this gives I(T ; O | B) ≥ sl − o(l). Since H(B | O) = o(l), the information
chain rule proves (5.19).
    Finally let QG denote the undivided score numerator, including subtraction of the root
supremum of the retained law. The same root supremum occurs for B and O. Their time entropies
differ by sl + o(l), so

                       QB − QO = (1 − γ)l + I(T ; O) − I(T ; B) − 2sl + o(l)
                                  ≥ (1 − γ − s)l − o(l).

On the other hand terminal saturation and the universal duration bound give QO = β + o(l) and
QB ≤ β(1 − l) + o(l). Divide by l and pass through the specified limits to obtain

                              1 − γ − s ≤ −β,          s ≥ 1 + β − γ > 1.

This contradicts the scalar time capacity s ≤ 1 and proves Proposition 5.1.


6    The matched plane and its traces
The trace construction applies to the matched process of Proposition 4.8 for any least aspect m > 0.
In the proof of Theorem 4.2, this is the alternative remaining under β > γ after Proposition 5.1.
Its saturated apertures have shapes

                          (b(u), a(u)) = (b(u), b(u) + mu),       0 < u < 1,

with horizon L = 1 + m, a Lipschitz function b, and 0 ≤ −b′ ≤ 1 + m almost everywhere.
Their labels use one common time filtration and the prefixes of one orientation, as supplied by
Proposition 4.8.



                                                  37
6.1   The information profile and the contact relation
Work in one bounded slope chart, using the row frame from the end of Proposition 4.8. The
particle T carries (V, A). At label time v put Ev = (Cv , Rmv ) and set

      X = V1 ,      Y = A1 + Cv X,         W = V2 − Rmv X,             Z = A2 + Cv V2 − Rmv Y.   (6.1)

For data depths L = (LX , LY , LW , LZ ) let O(L, v) record Ev and these four variables at their
respective grid precisions. We allow the depths to vary on compact subsets of

                          LY − LX ≤ v, LW − LX ≤ mv,
                                                                                                 (6.2)
                         LZ − LW ≤ v,        LZ − LY ≤ mv,             0 < v < 1.

They may range over any fixed bounded extension of the depths of the apertures; a negative depth
gives only boundedly many bins.
    When m = 1, impose LY = LW = LU at this point and regard U = (Y, W ) as one datum of
capacity two, with L = (LX , LU , LZ ). The profile and all the trace statements below are then
defined on this grouped domain, and the middle datum has one joint trace rate.
    The finite matched apertures occupy a particular plane in the depth domain. At time u, an
aperture with common depth i has velocity depths (i, i+mu) and position depths (i+u, i+(1+m)u).
Define
                     Πm (i, u) = (i, i + u, i + mu, i + (1 + m)u, u), m ̸= 1,
                                                                                            (6.3)
                      Π1 (i, u) = (i, i + u, i + 2u, u),              m = 1.
The coordinate orders are (LX , LY , LW , LZ , v) and (LX , LU , LZ , v), respectively. The finite
matched observation is O(L, u) with (L, u) = Πm (i, u). Every inequality in (6.2) is an equality on
this plane. The part corresponding to admissible apertures is

                      Am = {(i, u) : 0 < u < 1,          i ≥ 0,   i + (1 + m)u ≤ L}.             (6.4)

The path observations are the points i = b(u), up to the zero-cost grid choices already established
for the common row frame. The bounded extension of the data depths gives a neighborhood within
this plane across the boundary of Am . The full depth domain still has the one-sided faces in (6.2).
     We now make one homogeneous choice before defining the limiting functions. Start with a
countable dense family of admissible depth configurations and the countable family of all finite
tuples formed from their observations, including the versions conditional on T . Also include the
score observations at dyadic path times. At each stage use only a finite initial list and finitely many
path times. Apply the cap regularization of Lemma 4.4, then the uniform bins of Lemma 2.4. By
Lemma 4.3, select typical components whose finitely many score deficits lie under one deterministic
vanishing envelope. Let the lists grow and the bin meshes shrink slowly with the precision. The
diagonal of Lemma 2.9, as in Remark 4.9, gives one sequence of finite laws pϵ carrying all the
listed homogeneous profiles. Every listed path observation is saturated along this sequence in the
limit. Only after this sequence is chosen do we define

                        fϵ (L, v) = Ipϵ (T ; O(L, v)),        Sϵ (v) = Hpϵ (Cv | T ).

Lemma 2.9 and the box comparisons below give a limiting profile f on (6.2); write S for the
limiting time profile. The finite root suprema are bounded, so pass to a further subsequence and
write τ for the limit of τL (T ). The limiting score along the path is continuous: b, S are Lipschitz,
and the box comparisons give continuity of f along Πm (b(u), u). Saturation at the dense dyadic

                                                    38
times therefore extends to every 0 < u < 1. The positive constant in (4.9) is obtained for this
selected component by the least-aspect argument; it need not be the constant before the split.
The symbols f, S, τ below refer to this component, whose profiles may differ from those before the
split. Later, a trace point and a positive block length will be chosen for this fixed f before the
required real-depth bins and finite precision are selected.
    The information of a matched aperture is therefore the restriction
                                                                  
                                             Φ(i, u) = f Πm (i, u) .                                 (6.5)

The weight of the aperture at (i, u) is 2i + (1 − γ)mu. Rearranging the universal score bound and
using saturation on the path gives

          Φ(i, u) ≥ 2i + (1 − γ)mu + 2S(u) − βu − τ,                 with equality when i = b(u).    (6.6)

We call this equality the contact relation. The local projection arguments will constrain the
information rates at almost every plane point with i > 0, while saturation is known on this
particular contact curve. We therefore need both local affine approximations on the plane and
control of how the rates cross the curve.
    At a differentiability point of b put p = −b′ (u). A direction Vc = ∂u − c∂i preserves the data
depth i + cu, and its crossing factor at the contact curve is p − c. Here the clocks are 0, 1, m, 1 + m,
with the two middle clocks grouped when m = 1. Figure 2 shows these relations.

    Ambient almost-everywhere differentiability does not solve the local problem: the entire
matched plane lies on the boundary of (6.2), and could lie in the ambient exceptional set. We
will obtain the needed traces from the signs of the mixed derivatives. Their affine approximation
will produce finite entropy blocks for Section 7; their measure identities will later pass those rate
constraints to the contact curve.

                                                        39
    Here are the properties of f supplied by the finite observations. It is locally Lipschitz and
nondecreasing in every coordinate; its data derivatives lie between zero and the capacity of that
coordinate. Its label derivative is nonnegative and locally bounded. Every mixed derivative
involving two different coordinates, including a data coordinate and v, is a nonpositive distribution
in the interior. We verify the signs, especially the one involving the label.
    Advance v to v ′ > v. Write a = Cv′ − Cv and r = Rmv − Rmv′ . Nested labels give |a| ≲ ϵv ,
|r| ≲ ϵmv , and the change of data is
             X ′ = X,   Y ′ = Y + aX,      W ′ = W + rX,        Z ′ = Z + aW + rY + arX.           (6.7)
In a data box, the uncertainty in Y ′ is bounded by a constant times ϵLY + ϵv+LX , and that in W ′
by a constant times ϵLW + ϵmv+LX . The uncertainty in Z ′ is bounded by
                             C ϵLZ + ϵv+LW + ϵmv+LY + ϵ(1+m)v+LX .
                                                                           

The four inequalities in (6.2) bound these by the respective target widths. The inverse shear has
the same bounds. Consequently, conditional on Ev′ , the old and new data descriptions at fixed
admissible depths determine each other with bounded ambiguity. This assertion also holds at each
corner of any coordinate rectangle contained in the domain.
    At fixed labels, a data refinement is determined by the particle and labels. Its increase in
particle information is therefore exactly its conditional entropy, up to the bounded bin ambiguities.
A further data refinement can only decrease this conditional entropy. For a label refinement,
express both ends of the data refinement in the old frame conditional on Ev′ , using (6.7). The two
information increments being compared are then
                        H(Dfine | Ev , Dcoarse )   and   H(Dfine | Ev′ , Dcoarse ),
up to O(1/ log(1/ϵ)); the latter is no larger than the former. Here Dcoarse includes every other
data coordinate. Thus each mixed rectangle difference of fϵ is nonpositive up to an error vanishing
as ϵ ↓ 0. Passing to the limiting profile proves the mixed signs, first for rectangle differences and
then for distributions, by integrating the rectangle inequalities against nonnegative smooth test
functions and shrinking the rectangles.
     Refining a scalar datum by d costs at most d + O(1/ log(1/ϵ)) in normalized entropy; for U the
bound is 2d + O(1/ log(1/ϵ)). Advancing the labels by d costs at most (1 + m)d + O(1/ log(1/ϵ)).
The old data are recoverable from the new observation, and the change of data conditional on
the new labels has bounded ambiguity. These facts give monotonicity and the asserted Lipschitz
bounds. For two nearby points on flat faces, move first to a common slightly finer label so that
coordinatewise comparison is admissible. This proves the same bounds up to those faces. The
argument uses vector conditional entropy unchanged when Y, W are grouped.
     These comparisons also upgrade the dense diagonal to locally uniform convergence. Fix a
compact subset K of (6.2) and a compact relative neighborhood K + of it. Uniformly for nearby
z, z ′ ∈ K + , the box bounds give
                         |fϵ (z) − fϵ (z ′ )| ≤ CK ∥z − z ′ ∥1 + OK (1/ log(1/ϵ)).
The same estimate holds at the faces: the common finer label needed there advances time by
OK (∥z − z ′ ∥1 ), and stays in the domain for nearby points because K + has label times bounded
away from the endpoints. For a fixed sufficiently small δ > 0, choose a finite δ-net z1 , . . . , zN for
K from the dense family in K + . The same Lipschitz bound for f then gives
              sup |fϵ (z) − f (z)| ≤ max |fϵ (zj ) − f (zj )| + 2CK δ + OK (1/ log(1/ϵ)).
              z∈K                   1≤j≤N


                                                    40
First let ϵ → 0 along the selected sequence, using convergence at these finitely many net points,
and then let δ ↓ 0. Thus fϵ → f locally uniformly on the full closed-sided domain, including its
faces.

6.2    An abstract trace theorem
We now isolate the consequence of these mixed signs for a plane whose data depths move at distinct
speeds. The grouped middle datum at m = 1 is what makes the speeds distinct in that case.
    Let n ≥ 2, let c1 < · · · < cn be distinct real numbers, and let E be a nonempty set of ordered
pairs (j, k) with j < k. For an open interval I define the closed-sided region

                      D = {(L, v) ∈ Rn × I : Lk − Lj ≤ (ck − cj )v for (j, k) ∈ E}.

Its interior is obtained by making these inequalities strict. Define

                       Π(i, u) = (i + c1 u, . . . , i + cn u, u),
                             K = {ξ ∈ Rn+1 : ξk − ξj ≤ (ck − cj )ξv for (j, k) ∈ E},                               (6.8)
                            Vj = ∂u − cj ∂i .

The cone K has nonempty interior and is invariant under translation by either tangent vector
e = (1, . . . , 1, 0) or t = (c1 , . . . , cn , 1). In particular, for h ≥ 0, Π(i, u) + hξ belongs to D for ξ ∈ K,
provided its label time belongs to I.

Theorem 6.1 (Characteristic-plane traces). Suppose f is locally Lipschitz on D, and, in its

gradients has been used.

6.3    Finite approximation after selecting the block scale
The next consequence specifies exactly what is required of the finite entropy systems. Its exceptional
set is a set of base points on the plane; probabilistic trimming of conditional cells is a separate use
of Lemma 2.4 and Theorem 2.7.
Corollary 6.2 (Quantified finite profile approximation). Assume Theorem 6.1, and let fϵ → f
locally uniformly on D along the sequence of finite systems under consideration. Fix a bounded
measurable base patch Q of positive measure with compact closure in R × I, a radius R ≥ 1, an
error η > 0, an exceptional proportion 0 < τ < 1, and a maximum block length h∗ > 0. There exist
                            0 < h < h∗ ,        G ⊂ Q,        |Q \ G| ≤ τ |Q|,
and a compact neighborhood K0 ⊂ D containing all Π(q) + hξ with q ∈ Q, ξ ∈ K ∩ BR , such that
the following holds. If
                                 ∥fϵ − f ∥L∞ (K0 ) ≤ ηh/4,                             (6.19)
then, simultaneously for every q ∈ G and every ξ ∈ K ∩ BR ,
                            |fϵ (Π(q) + hξ) − fϵ (Π(q)) − hA(q) · ξ| ≤ ηh.                       (6.20)
In particular, for every finite family of such displacements, every information increment between
two of them differs from its affine value by at most 2ηh. An alternating sum of r profile values
whose affine contributions cancel has absolute value at most rηh.
    All dependencies have the order
                           f, Q, R, η, τ, h∗     −→       h, G, K0   −→       ϵ0 .
For every member of the converging finite sequence with ϵ < ϵ0 , (6.19) holds. If finitely many
entropy identities also incur a total normalized ambiguity error at most B/ log(1/ϵ), one may,
after choosing h, require in addition
                                           log(1/ϵ) ≥ B/(ηh).
Each such combined identity then has at most one additional ηh of error. More generally one
may prescribe any sequences ηℓ , τℓ ↓ 0 and h∗,ℓ ↓ 0 and select successively hℓ < h∗,ℓ , then the inner
approximation thresholds. If ℓ τℓ < ∞, almost every point of Q belongs to all but finitely many
                            P

of the resulting Gℓ .

                                                     44
Proof. On Q, the supremum error in (6.14) tends to zero almost everywhere along the chosen
sequence and is uniformly bounded by the Lipschitz bounds times R. Choose a term h < h∗ small
enough that the subset on which this error exceeds η/2 has measure at most τ |Q|, and that all
shifted label times stay inside I. Its complement is G. A compact neighborhood K0 exists because
Q and the displacement set are bounded. The two profile approximation errors in the difference
in (6.20) total at most ηh/2, proving that inequality. Subtracting increments, or summing the
individual errors, gives the finite-family assertions. Uniform convergence supplies ϵ0 after h has
been fixed. The ambiguity condition and the last exceptional-set assertion follow respectively by
addition of errors and summability of the measures of Q \ Gℓ .
    There is no uniform choice of h for all Lipschitz profiles in this corollary. The translation moduli
in (6.9), and hence the block scale, depend on the limiting profile. At a fixed nonexceptional plane
point one may equivalently use its sequence in (6.14), choose a block from that sequence, and then
make the finite-system errors small compared with that block.
    For the profile (6.2), the clocks are cX = 0, cY = 1, cW = m, cZ = 1 + m, ordered increasingly
when applying the theorem. Write the trace fields as x, y, w, z, D. At m = 1 the clocks are 0, 1, 2,
the fields are x, q, z, D, and q = aU ; notation such as y + w means q and asserts no separate traces
for Y, W . Thus (6.12) reads
                                        (∂u − (1 + m)∂i )z ≥ ∂i D.                                 (6.21)
The distinction between grouped and separate depths is necessary: f (LY , LW ) = max(LY , LW )
has nonpositive mixed derivatives, but its separate first derivatives have different traces from the
two sides of LY = LW . On the grouped domain it is simply f (LU ) = LU .

6.4    Traces on a contact curve
We need a second trace statement within the plane. It uses bounded variation in one direction at
a time; it does not require the entire gradient of each rate field to be a measure.
Lemma 6.3 (Transverse traces on a Lipschitz curve). Let b be Lipschitz on an interval J, and
let Γ = {(b(u), u) : u ∈ J} lie in an open plane region. Suppose bounded fields aj , D in this
region satisfy Vj aj ∈ Mloc and ∂i D ∈ Mloc , where Mloc denotes locally finite signed measures.
Put p(u) = −b′ (u) at points where this derivative exists. For almost every u ∈ J the field D has
two strong traces D− , D+ and every aj with cj ̸= p(u) has two strong traces a−         +
                                                                                   j , aj . Specifically,
at q0 = (b(u0 ), u0 ) the mean of |aj − a±
                                         j (u0 )| tends to zero on the corresponding tangent half-ball

                     Bρ (q0 ) ∩ { ±(i − b(u0 ) + p(u0 )(u − u0 )) > 0 },               ρ ↓ 0,
and likewise for D. The same assertion holds with the actual sides ±(i − b(u)) > 0. No assertion
is made about the field whose speed equals p(u0 ).
    If, in addition, Vn an ≥ ∂i D, then at almost every transverse point p ̸= cn ,
                                   D− − D+ ≥ (cn − p)(a+    −
                                                       n − an ).                                  (6.22)
These conclusions apply to curves on the boundary of an aperture region whenever the fields are
defined in an ambient neighborhood; one then retains only the side lying in that region.
Proof. We first justify the one-direction slicing fact being used. If g ∈ L∞ on a rectangle with
coordinates (s, r) and ∂s g is a finite measure, then for almost every r the one-dimensional function
gr (s) = g(s, r) has bounded variation, and its derivative measures satisfy, on smaller rectangles,
                                        Z                             Z
                               ∂s g =       Dgr dr,        |∂s g| =       |Dgr | dr.              (6.23)


                                                      45
Here integration of measures means integration of their values against compactly supported
continuous test functions. To verify this, convolve g in the rectangle. The integrals of |∂s gϱ |
on a smaller rectangle are bounded by the variation of ∂s g on a slightly larger one. Choose a
subsequence with gϱ (·, r) → g(·, r) in L1 (ds) for almost every r. The definition of one-dimensional
variation as the supremum over finite partitions, or over test functions bounded by one, and
Fatou’s lemma give integrableR variations of the slices. Fubini and one-dimensional integration by
parts then identify ∂s g with Dgr dr. The variation equality follows by taking the supremum
over a countable dense family of scalar test functions on rational subintervals, selecting these tests
measurably in r, and approximating the resulting bounded tests by continuous ones. Exhaustion
gives (6.23) locally.
    Suppose first the curve is a Lipschitz graph s = h(r) in these coordinates. The one-dimensional
representatives have limits T ± (r) = gr (h(r)±) for almost every r. Fix a compact graph patch and
define                                                                   
                            qϱ (r) = |Dgr | (h(r) − ϱ, h(r) + ϱ) \ {h(r)} .
For small fixed ϱ, these functions are integrable by (6.23), and they decrease to zero almost
everywhere as ϱ ↓ 0. Outside one null set we may assume that r0 is a Lebesgue point of
T + , T − and of every q1/k for which the graph patch permits that collar. If |r − r0 | < Cρ and
0 < ±(s − h(r)) < Cρ, then

                           |g(s, r) − T ± (r0 )| ≤ qCρ (r) + |T ± (r) − T ± (r0 )|

for almost every (s, r). Integrating in such a graph-side cylinder and dividing by ρ2 bounds the
result by
                            C
                              Z
                                         qCρ (r) + |T ± (r) − T ± (r0 )| dr.
                                                                        
                            ρ |r−r0 |<Cρ
For each fixed k, eventually qCρ ≤ q1/k . Lebesgue differentiation first, then k → ∞, shows that
this bound tends to zero. At a point where h is differentiable, the area between its graph and
its tangent in a radius-ρ disk is o(ρ2 ). Boundedness of g therefore gives the same strong mean
traces on tangent half-disks. Invertible linear changes of coordinates preserve this conclusion for
half-balls, because the corresponding ellipses contain and are contained in comparable balls.
    For aj use the coordinates
                                       s = u,     r = i + cj u,
so that Vj = ∂s and the curve has projection r = gj (u) = b(u)+cj u. On the set where gj′ = cj −p ̸= 0
this projection can be covered, apart from a null set, by countably many sets on each of which it
is bi-Lipschitz. Here is a direct reason. At a differentiability point with gj′ ̸= 0, choose integers
k, N so that |gj′ | > 2/k and

                   |gj (v) − gj (u) − gj′ (u)(v − u)| ≤ |v − u|/k     if |v − u| < 1/N.

Partition these points according to k, N , the sign of the derivative, and intervals of length less than
1/N . Any two points of one piece then have image distance at least |v − u|/k; the upper Lipschitz
bound is inherited from gj . On each image piece the inverse extends to a Lipschitz function h(r)
on an interval. The graph argument above applies to this extension. At almost every density point
of the original piece its tangent is the original tangent. The bi-Lipschitz parameter change takes
exceptional null sets back to null sets. This proves the traces for aj on its transverse set. For D,
simply take s = i, r = u, so the curve is already the graph s = b(r) and is everywhere transverse.
Taking the union of the finitely many exceptional sets proves the simultaneous assertion. The area
comparison also replaces tangent sides by actual sides.

                                                     46
    Finally, (6.23) identifies the part of the derivative measure on s = h(r) as (T + (r) − T − (r)) dr,
since a one-dimensional BV derivative has an atom equal to its jump. On each transverse inverse
patch, substitute dr = |cj − p(u)| du. The positive s side is the positive i − b(u) side precisely
when p − cj > 0, so reversing the sides when necessary gives the signed factor p − cj . Partition the
countable patches into disjoint measurable pieces before summing. There is no omitted measure on
a parameter-null subset of the curve: its projection under the Lipschitz map gj is null, and (6.23)
gives zero variation over such a projection. In the original (i, u) coordinates we have therefore
proved
                                                −
                   (Vj aj )|Γ = (p − cj )(a+
                                           j − aj ) du,    (∂i D)|Γ = (D+ − D− ) du              (6.24)
on the transverse part of the parameter set. To check the first sign and factor directly, set
                                                                                           −
φ(i, u) = i − b(u). Then Vj φ = p − cj , and differentiating a function with jump a+ j − aj across
φ = 0 gives exactly that factor times δ(φ) di du = (du)|Γ . This agrees with the change of variable
in the sliced jump measures, so it applies to the bounded fields just considered. Restrict the
nonnegative measure Vn an − ∂i D to the transverse part of Γ and use (6.24); its nonnegative density
is
                                                 −              −
                                 (p − cn )(a+            +
                                            n − an ) − (D − D ).
This is precisely (6.22). An aperture boundary requires no further trace argument: all statements
were proved in the ambient plane neighborhood before a side is selected.


7     Rate transfer at matched clocks
We keep the saturated homogeneous component pϵ selected in Section 6, with least aspect m > 0.
Its information and time profiles are f, S, and its matched-plane potential is Φ = f ◦ Πm from
(6.5). The row data and their admissible depths are those of (6.1) and (6.2). Write x, y, w, z for
their plane trace rates and D for the label rate. When m = 1, the middle datum is U = (Y, W )
and its single rate is q = aU . The matched component defining f, S, Φ is fixed before their trace
points. Later homogeneous selections concern the derived block laws and do not redefine these
profiles.
Proposition 7.1 (Matched-clock rate transfer). For almost every point (i, u) of the characteristic
plane with i > 0 and 0 < u < 1, put s = S ′ (u). There is a constant σ > 0, depending only on the
matched process and m, for which 0 < s ≤ 1 and the following assertions hold.
    1. If m ̸= 1, each of the chains x −→ y and w −→ z has the rule
                                     d>0      =⇒        e ≥ min(1, d + s),
      where d, e are its source and target rates. Each of x −→ w and y −→ z has the same rule
      with σ in place of s.
    2. If m = 1, then
               x > 0 =⇒ q ≥ s,        x > 0, z < 1 =⇒ z ≥ x + s,             z < 1 =⇒ q ≤ 2 − s.
The bounds include 0 ≤ x, y, w, z ≤ 1, or 0 ≤ q ≤ 2 in the tied case, supplied by the coordinate
capacities.
    The proof first extracts a finite point–parameter law from an affine entropy block. That law
will carry the parameter nonconcentration, coordinate support counts, and product comparisons
used by the projection tests. Distinct clocks give the four rate transfers directly. At tied clocks,
the joint rate q is supplemented by directions between parameter samples and two conditional
projection tests.

                                                   47
7.1   The selected profiles and the parameter children
We first verify what the already selected sequence pϵ supplies at the depths needed here. Its initial
homogeneous list contains all finite tuples from a countable dense family of admissible configurations,
including the indicated conditional probabilities given T . For two nearby configurations, give both
descriptions the finer label clock. The box comparisons in Section 6 give bounded ambiguity for
changing the data frame at a fixed admissible resolution. A depth change of size a introduces at
most ϵ−Ca+o(1) possibilities, also conditional on T , on each fixed compact depth range. Applying the
conditional-surprise counting bound from Lemma 2.9 to both descriptions extends the homogeneous
exponents from the dense family to every fixed admissible configuration. The same argument
applies to finite tuples. It does not require independent data refinements to remain admissible at
the coarse label clock.
    Write h(A) for the limiting normalized entropy of a listed observation. For a finite pair A, B,
the selected homogeneous probabilities give, in probability,
                            − log1/ϵ (pϵ (B | A)) = h(A, B) − h(A) + o(1).                        (7.1)
The same statement holds for listed probabilities conditional on T ; the probability of T itself is
never binned. After a trace point, a positive block length h, and a finite list of real configurations
have been chosen, take one event Eϵ on which all their original conditional surprises differ from
their limiting exponents by at most ωϵ = o(h). Its probability tends to one. On this event, ratios
of the joint and parent masses give uniform upper conditional masses and upper retained support
counts. The support count follows by dividing the upper mass of a parent by the lower mass of
each retained joint value.
     This finite real-depth event is imposed before any restriction which uses its bounds. If a later
law satisfies Q ≤ Kpϵ ( · | Eϵ ), with log K = o(hℓ) and ℓ = log(1/ϵ), its conditional entropy of each
listed child given its listed parent differs from the listed exponent by at most ωϵ + log(K/pϵ (Eϵ ))/ℓ,
by conditional relative entropy and the support count. The parent likelihood and child-support
thresholds in the proof of Lemma 2.4 give the same absolute error bound for realized surprise, with
log(1/η)/ℓ added, outside Q-mass at most 2η; choose η ↓ 0 with log(1/η) = o(hℓ). The dense-list
profiles remain the same under this high-probability restriction by Lemma 2.9. This verifies the
finite inheritance for the component selected in Section 6; it does not select a new f or assert
uniform bins at all real depths.
     For this component the prediction identities and the positive constant obtained from its
least-aspect argument are
             H(Rmv | T, Cv ) = 0,       H(Rm(v+a) | T, Cv ) ≥ κa    (0 < v < v + a < 1).          (7.2)
These are limiting normalized statements for this same pϵ .
   Fix a regular plane point (i, u) and a trace block length h > 0. Write E for the coarse
observation at Πm (i, u) and put ρ = ϵh . Given the coarse label, E is a function of (T, Cu , Rmu )
up to bounded grid choices, which we record when needed. Choose K > max(1, 1/m), fixed
throughout this construction, and define the normalized parameter increments
                    c = ϵ−u (Cu+Kh − Cu ),        r = −ϵ−mu (Rm(u+Kh) − Rmu ).
The full finite label θ records the advanced label and the bounded choices just mentioned. Before
all tests, fix one half-open dyadic tree on a fixed bounded box containing the normalized pair (c, r).
For every real 0 ≤ d ≤ 1, let Θd be its atom at level
                                       nρ (d) = ⌈d log2 (1/ρ)⌉ .

                                                  48
These atoms are exactly nested for all real depths: Θa is determined by Θb when a ≤ b, and each
Θa atom has at most
                                    4nρ (b)−nρ (a) ≤ 4ρ−2(b−a)
Θb children. The time prefix records Cu+dh and the normal prefix records Rmu+dh up to bounded
ambiguity over E. For other finite observations whose nesting holds only up to bounded choices,
we append the parent to the child when needed. Here h and the finite prefix list are fixed before
ϵ → 0.
   The chain rule gives
                    H(Cu+dh | E) ≥ H(Cu+dh | T, E)
                                  ≥ S(u + dh) − S(u) − H(Rmu | T, Cu ) − o(1),                 (7.3)
                   H(Rmu+dh | E) ≥ H(Rmu+dh | T, Cu , Rmu ) − o(1)
                                  ≥ (κ/m)dh − o(1).                                            (7.4)
For the first inequality, the data in E are determined by the particle and coarse labels, and
adjoining Rmu costs at most its conditional entropy. The second uses (7.2) at time increment
dh/m. Prediction also gives
                       κa ≤ H(Rm(u+a) | T, Cu ) ≤ S(u + a) − S(u) + o(1).
Consequently s = S ′ (u) ≥ κ > 0 at the regular points used below. Fix any 0 < σ < min(1, κ/m).
    At tied clocks there is also a bound between any two tested parameter prefixes. If m = 1 and
0 ≤ a < b ≤ 1, then conditional on E the symbol Θa is equivalent to (Cu+ah , Ru+ah ) up to the
recorded bounded choices. Therefore
              H(Θb | E, Θa ) ≥ H(Cu+bh | T, E, Θa )
                             ≥ S(u + bh) − S(u + ah) − H(Ru+ah | T, Cu+ah ) − o(1)
                             = S(u + bh) − S(u + ah) − o(1).                                   (7.5)
After T, Cu+ah have been given, the extra conditioning in E, Θa is a function of Ru+ah and the
bounded choices. This is the stated entropy cost and does not use an entropy bound for T .
   The homogeneous joint symbols (E, Θa ) and (E, Θb ) turn this last entropy inequality into a
bound on their probability ratio. On the common real-depth event,
                         pϵ (Θb | E, Θa ) ≤ ϵS(u+bh)−S(u+ah)−o(1)   (m = 1)                    (7.6)
at every retained child. The time and normal inequalities give the corresponding marginal prefix
bounds by the same ratio calculation. Lemma 2.2, with the coarse observation as C and each
listed parent containing C, selects base cells of adequate relative retention without an inverse
original cell mass. We will keep the event as an unnormalized allowed set inside the chosen base
cell until both product comparisons have been imposed.

7.2   One finite affine-block law
Center the common pre-label data in the boxes of E, dilate them to bounded coordinates, and
round the resulting point P at precision ρ. For a full label with normalized increment (c, r) define
its canonical post-coordinate symbols by applying the shear to this same rounded point and then
taking the indicated grid bins:
         Xθ = X,       Yθ = Y + cX,       Wθ = W + rX,          Zθ = Z + cW + rY + crX.        (7.7)

                                                 49
The same origin is transformed at every label, so the shears compose by adding (c, r) and the map
from frame 0 to frame 1 uses θ1 − θ0 . Applying the shear before or after rounding changes each
real output by O(ρ). The canonical bins and the physically recorded bins consequently determine
one another with bounded conditional ambiguity. We use the canonical symbols in the finite law
below: each is a genuine function of (P, θ). Their prefixes and joint numerator and denominator
symbols are included in the finite uniformization list. Bounded ambiguity transfers the entropy
identities to these symbols; their own uniform probability bins, rather than a pointwise assertion
from that ambiguity alone, will supply the retained mass bounds.
    Let I = {X, Y, W, Z} when m ̸= 1, and I = {X, U, Z} when m = 1, with U = (Y, W ). Write
Qj,θ for the canonical coordinate in group j ∈ I, and (Qj,θ )d for its prefix at precision ρd . Put
(aX , aY , aW , aZ ) = (x, y, w, z) in the distinct-clock case and (aX , aU , aZ ) = (x, q, z) in the tied
case. Choose the fixed K large enough that raising any individual data depth by at most h is
admissible after the label advances by Kh. For any fixed finite list of tj ∈ [0, 1], the affine trace
gives                                                       X

the refinement. The affine trace gives the displayed increment, and the canonical rounding costs
only bounded ambiguity.

These support bounds are in place before any label is replicated. The upper mass bounds can

intermediate radii with the mesh error. The mesh is fixed before its precision is selected.
    All later scalar and direction profiles are extracted from this already chosen sequence πn at
ρn → 0. They do not choose new hn . If a later profile selects a real parent depth and a positive
derivative increment, extend the nested surprises from the rational prefixes and impose a fresh
uniform event for that finite real list before the restrictions which use it. For each fixed increment,
errors tending to zero in block units also tend to zero after division by that increment. Only
then may the increment shrink. This is a subsequence of the existing block sequence, not a new
trace-scale choice.
    An arbitrary later restriction does not automatically preserve products of its new actual
marginals. If πb = wπ/mR with 0 ≤ w ≤ 1, then (7.14) bounds its pair and coordinate laws by
K0 /mR times the old named products. Decomposing the two relative entropies against those
products shows

Thus a subpower restriction retains small information, while its own product bounds are obtained
by applying Lemma 7.2 again after the required finite prefix refresh. The old upper prefix masses
are first preserved on typical parent fibers by Lemma 2.2; support counts persist by inclusion.
This is the procedure used when the tied-clock argument later selects a homogeneous Y profile.
In every later use of this one-label recoring procedure, reset π, µ, λ and ξjθ to its new law and
actual factors. Also renew K0 and one common deterministic finite-test subpower envelope for the
products and tested prefix losses in the current working units. A fixed positive shortening by a
factor a preserves subpower loss, since o(L) = o(aL) when both are expressed in the shortened
units.

7.3    Replication and the first projection tests
For 1 ≤ krep ≤ 3, sample labels θ0 , . . . , θkrep −1 independently given P with the conditional kernel
of π. Their exact star law is

                                      k      Y
                             Π ≤ K0 rep µ          λj ,
                                               j
                                      k   −1               Y                                     (7.17)
                             Π ≤ K0 rep        π(P, θi )          λj   (0 ≤ i < krep ).

                                                           54
where every λj is a copy of λ. These are the named comparison laws used after replication.
     Let R = wΠ/mR be a graph restriction chosen before conditioning. Fix a set J of full labels and
leave J c free. The conditioning calculation in Theorem 2.7, applied to (7.17), has two consequences
on typical fixed-label values. First, for each i ∈ J, the point law is dominated by a subpower
times π(P | θi ); pushing this comparison through the coordinate map at label i retains its product
against the named factors ξjθi . Second, after the theorem’s point-likelihood trim, the joint law of
the point and the free labels is compared with its actual conditional point marginal times the
copies λj for j ∈/ J.
     Feed all coordinate products needed at these fixed labels and the point–free-label comparison
into one application of Lemma 2.6. Repeated occurrences of a coordinate with different named
                                                θ0      θ1
factors are counted separately: for example, ξX    and ξX  may differ even though the map Xθ = X
is invariant. The simultaneous core replaces both by the same current actual point marginal of X.
If J = {0, 1} in the tied case, the resulting point law ν satisfies

                          νX,U0 ≤ K1 νX νU0 ,     νX,U1 ,Z1 ≤ K1 νX νU1 νZ1 ,                   (7.18)

and the full coordinate product at either label is available when required. Its coordinate marginal
prefix bounds retain the rates x, q, z with subpower losses. If label 2 is free, the same core
gives a point–label-2 comparison with the actual point marginal and a retained label comparison
whose absolute prefix masses are bounded by a subpower times those of the named pre-core label
law. Conditional child caps for the new label marginal also require the tested parent floors or
typical-parent likelihood thresholds; these are imposed for the finite list before those caps are used.
    Here and in later uses, the number of replicas is fixed and the finite factor list, graph retention
mR , likelihood thresholds, and core retention are chosen with common deterministic subpower
envelopes. The constants in Theorem 2.7 are fixed products of K0 , their inverse retentions and
thresholds, and the finite factor count. Their normalized logarithms therefore tend to zero together
on every retained conditional cell. The bound does not depend on the number of possible fixed-label
values. For example, if

               log(1/mR ), log(1/η), log(1/u), log(2Nfac ) ≤ bn Ln        with bn → 0

throughout a chosen conditional array, then its fixed-label comparisons and fixed-size cores have
constants at most exp(C(κn + bn )Ln ), with C depending only on the fixed numbers of replicas
and factors. This supplies one common subpower envelope for those cells.
    A subsequent point observation is fixed only if it is a function of P and the labels already in
J. Because the point factor after the core is actual, Corollary 2.8 preserves the same pre-slice
free-label comparison on every positive point slice. Any coordinate product needed within such a
slice is separately conditioned by Lemma 2.2, on typical values of the specified coordinate parent.
An observation involving a free label must instead be included among the fixed labels before this
argument, or be handled by a different verified comparison.
    Suppose first that m ̸= 1. Use two replicas and fix a typical label 0, leaving label 1 free. The
fixed-label core just described gives an actual point law for (X, Y0 ) with ball dimension x + y: its
coordinate product and the tested prefix upper masses bound a square of radius ρd by ρ(x+y)d−o(1) .
The free comparison is the retained pre-slice label-1 law, whose time marginal has dimension s.
The dominated incidence law has graph product mass ρo(1) . For each full label 1 the projected
coordinate
                                        Y1 = Y0 + (c1 − c0 )X



                                                  55
has at most ρ−y−o(1) bins on its retained neighbors by (7.16). The other component of the label is
kept as a tag. Theorem 3.2 gives

                                  y ≥ min{1, x + y, (x + y + s)/2}.                              (7.19)

    For the chain from w to z, fix also the full block symbols X, Y0 . The label-0 coordinate
product, conditioned on typical such values, gives the point population (W0 , Z0 ) dimension w + z.
Corollary 2.8 keeps the same free label-1 comparison. The last coordinate is Z0 + (c1 − c0 )W0 plus
a translation determined by the fixed symbols and the full free label, and its original support count
is still available. The projection theorem gives z ≥ min{1, w + z, (w + z + s)/2}. Interchanging
the two shears gives the chains from x to w and from y to z with σ in place of s; for the latter the
fixed point symbols are X, W0 . All slices are finite grid symbols, and the typical-conditioning loss
has no factor depending on their alphabet sizes.
    For any of these inequalities, if the source d is positive and the target e is below 1, the
alternative e ≥ d + e is impossible. The remaining alternative gives e ≥ d + s or e ≥ d + σ, as
required. This proves the distinct-clock part of Proposition 7.1.
    Now let m = 1. Equation (7.7) becomes

            U1 = U0 + (θ1 − θ0 )X,       Z1 = Z0 + flip(θ1 − θ0 ) · U0 + prod(θ1 − θ0 )X,        (7.20)

where flip(c, r) = (r, c) and prod(c, r) = cr. The trace controls the joint block U , so it supplies no
separate affine rates for Y, W . Homogenize the conditional Y -prefix probabilities on a subpower
part of the current one-label law, and write g(d) for their limiting entropy in block units. This is a
selection within the already fixed block sequence. At each finite stage take the post-split law as
the new p0 . Relative to the old one-label law, select typical values of every tested parent needed
for the parameter and X upper masses. For each tested nested parameter pair Θa , Θb , also impose
the capacity tail
                                      p0 (Θb | Θa ) ≥ τ C −1 ρ2(b−a) .
Here C = 4 is the capacity constant of the fixed parameter tree. There are at most Cρ−2(b−a)
grid children over each parent, so the failure of this condition has p0 -mass at most τ . Choose
τ with Ntest τ → 0 and log(1/τ ) = o(L), where Ntest is the number of these tested pairs and
L = log(1/ρ). Intersect all good-parent filters, capacity tails, and fresh Y and coordinate surprise
bins to form one allowed event H for p0 , and keep 1H p0 unnormalized until the repeated application
of Lemma 7.2. Its list contains all the tested numerator and denominator observations. The old
named products give the small information needed for this extraction; the simultaneous floors
against p0 preserve the inherited upper masses and selected Y exponents on the same retained
fibers. The new products use the actual marginals of the output, and its support is a subset of the
original coordinate support.
    Relabel this final one-label law as π, with actual point and parameter marginals µ, λ. If δ is the
repeated core’s threshold, its simultaneous prefix floors give, on every retained tested parameter
child,
                              λ(Θb | Θa ) ≥ δτ C −1 ρ2(b−a) = ρ2(b−a)+o(1) .                    (7.21)
This uses only the planar child capacity and supplies a lower mass for the short mesh denominators
used below. Put
                                                     g(d)
                                          g∗ = inf        .
                                               0<d≤1 d

The law π has the X–U product, the X and time upper masses, and the homogeneous Y prefix
masses and support counts on the same retained incidences. Each (P, θi ) marginal of its exact star

                                                  56
is this same law. If a later graph restriction is R = wΠ/mR , that marginal is bounded by π/mR .
Typical label likelihood thresholds therefore preserve its named prefix upper bounds, and support
counts can only decrease. Pushing the X–U product through U 7→ Y bounds a square of radius
ρr in (X, Y0 ) by ρxr+g(r)−o(1) on the retained typical labels. Both replicas use the profile of this
one law.
    At precision ρd , the (X, Y0 ) point population has ball dimension at least x + g∗ , whereas its Y1
projection count has exponent g(d)/d. Indeed g(r) ≥ g∗ r at every smaller prefix; the requisite ball
bounds follow from the homogeneous conditional masses. Fix label 0, leave label 1 free, and use
the same product comparison as for (7.19). At each fixed d > 0, Theorem 3.2 gives

                               g(d)
                                    ≥ min{1, x + g∗ , (x + g∗ + s)/2}.
                                d
Taking the infimum does not assume it is attained: test successively fixed d with g(d)/d approaching
the infimum, choosing all losses after d. If x > 0, the resulting inequality implies g∗ ≥ min(1, x+s) ≥
s. Since g(1) ≤ q, we have proved q ≥ s.

7.4   Directions between parameter samples
The reciprocal use of pinned directions and the local linearization below have close precedents in
the radial-projection bootstrap of Shmerkin and Wang and the thin-tube bootstrap of Orponen,
Shmerkin, and Wang [27, 32]. We prove the version needed here for the retained conditional pair
law, including its selection order and the resulting improvement of the directional rate.
    Throughout this subsection and its applications, set dir(0) = (1, 0) and give this fixed unit
vector the ordinary nested direction-grid prefixes. Thus the direction and scalar observations are
defined on the entire finite space, including coincident labels.
    We give the needed directional statement with its assumptions in finite probability form. A
child bound of rate s means the conditional parameter-prefix bound for λ(Θb | Θa ) in (7.15) at
every fixed tested pair of depths, on uniform trims and typical parent fibers. On each tested good
parent, it applies to the entire named conditional population, hence to all its positive child values.
This includes all intermediate radii after using a slowly growing finite mesh.

Proposition 7.3 (Directional bootstrap). Let 0 < s ≤ 1. Let a sequence of pair laws of bounded
planar parameters (A, B) be dominated, with subpower loss at precision ρ, by the product of two
populations having conditional child bounds of rate s. Assume that for each fixed t > 0, both
comparison marginals satisfy

                        sup µ{dist(·, ℓ) ≤ ρt } ≤ ρκt +o(1)   for some κt > 0,                   (7.22)
                          ℓ

and likewise for the other marginal. Suppose the direction-prefix probabilities conditional on either
pin are homogenized on the same pair law. For each finite test, assume a common event of subpower
pair mass on which the required original direction bins are uniform and the pair support lies over the
simultaneous tested good-parent fibers for the child bounds. Then, on uniform trims, the direction
of B − A conditional on either pin has prefix ball dimension at least s. The assertion is hereditary
under subpower restrictions which retain the stated child bounds and product comparisons.

Proof. Let P be the already homogeneous pair law in the hypothesis, with P ≤ Kpair µA ⊗ µB .
Write FB (d) for its conditional direction entropy given the full pin B, and similarly FA (d). The
nested direction grids make both profiles Lipschitz, vanishing at zero and absolutely continuous.

                                                  57
For each finite test choose one event H of mass mH = P(H) > 0 carrying both the original
conditional direction bins and the simultaneous good-parent filters from the hypothesis. Keep P, H
named, and put R = 1H P/mH . The inverse retention and all later thresholds use the common
subpower envelope for this test.
    Fix a differentiability point j ∈ (0, 1) of FB and a much smaller fixed increment a > 0. For
0 < η < 1/2, apply Lemma 2.2 with the observation Aj , both to R ≤ (Kpair /mH )µA ⊗ µB and
to its retention from P. Outside RAj -mass at most 2η, the conditional law Rj = R( · | Aj ) and
rj = P(H | Aj ) satisfy

                                                              Kpair
                    Rj ≤ Kj µA ( · | Aj ) ⊗ µB ,      Kj =          ,   rj ≥ mH η.
                                                              mH η
There is no inverse original parent mass. Fix such a parent among the tested child-bound fibers
supplied by the hypothesis, with center a0 , and write A = a0 + ρj ξ. Its named rescaled child
law has ball dimension at least s at precision ρa . Restrict to |B − a0 | ≥ ρo(1) using the displayed
product and the positive-dimensional ball bounds at each fixed positive depth. If its relative
retained mass is vj , write Gj for the normalized restricted graph; then

                                             Kj
                                      Gj ≤      µA ( · | Aj ) ⊗ µB ,
                                             vj

with vj−1 subpower.
   For each pin, the number of retained depth-(j + a) direction bins inside a depth-j bin is at
most
                                    ρ−[FB (j+a)−FB (j)]−o(1) .                           (7.23)
This follows by dividing the original P parent and child masses conditional on that pin, using their
uniform bins on H. Further fixing Aj only removes support. Computing the direction from a0
instead of A determines the coarse direction bin to subpower ambiguity. Taylor expansion of the
direction map shows that, in child units, the finer bins give intervals for the scalar projection of ξ
perpendicular to B − a0 , with error
                                          O(ρa ) + ρj−o(1) .
Indeed the second derivative of the direction map is O(|B − a0 |−2 ); dividing its O(ρ2j |B − a0 |−2 )
error by the first-order scale ρj /|B − a0 | gives the second term. It is smaller than the working
precision when a ≪ j. All translations and dilations have subpower size.
    Before resampling, apply Lemma 2.6 to the two named factors of Gj . With threshold 1/4 it
gives a normalized pair qj of raw mass Mj ≥ 1/2 satisfying

                                                Kj
                                      qj ≤ 16      (qj )A ⊗ (qj )B .
                                                vj

Its actual child and pin marginals are bounded respectively by (Kj /(vj Mj ))µA ( · | Aj ) and
(Kj /(vj Mj ))µB , so their ball and strip bounds persist; its support retains the direction counts.
Now sample two pins independently over (qj )A using the kernel of qj . Theorem 2.7 compares the
star with the independent product of these actual marginals and with either retained pair qj times
the other pin marginal. For any fixed τ > 0, an absolute sine of the angle at most ρτ a between the
two directions puts the second pin in a strip through a0 of width O(ρτ a ). Equation (7.22) makes
this event have probability tending to zero, even after the indicated product losses. Angles are
thus tested modulo π, including nearly antiparallel directions. Fix a typical pair with the absolute

                                                    58
sine of its angle at least ρτ a . The child retains its ball bound, and its two projections have counts
(7.23). Two such projection intervals locate it within a ball of radius O(ρ(1−τ )a ). Therefore

                             2 [FB (j + a) − FB (j)] ≥ s(1 − τ )a − o(1).

Here j, a, τ are fixed before ρ → 0. Then let a ↓ 0 and τ ↓ 0. We obtain FB′ (j) ≥ s/2 almost
everywhere. The same proof for the opposite pin uses the same homogeneous pair law. Integration
gives FA (d), FB (d) ≥ sd/2.
     Suppose now that both profiles have the lower bound ud, where 0 < u ≤ s. The original
conditional bins on H give the upper bound ρur−o(1) for each retained direction bin of depth r
given its pin, at every fixed required prefix. For this reciprocal step use Gj before the auxiliary
two-pin core. Since Aj is determined by A, the conditional law of B given A under P( · | Aj ) is
still that under P. At depths r ≤ a ≪ j, the directions about A and a0 differ by less than the
tested precision on the separation set. Average the original conditional upper bound on H over A
in this parent before normalizing either restriction. The actual pin marginal (Gj )B therefore obeys
the direction bound ρur−o(1) with the extra factor (rj vj )−1 , which is subpower. Apply Lemma 2.6
directly to Gj with its two named factors, again with threshold 1/4. If this core has raw mass
M ≥ 1/2, its actual pin marginal is at most M −1 (Gj )B , while its actual child marginal is at most
(Kj /(vj M ))µA ( · | Aj ). The former keeps the reciprocal direction bound and the latter the child
ball bound; the core supplies their actual product comparison. Thus Theorem 3.2 applies with
dimensions s, u and count (7.23). It gives

                             FB′ (j) ≥ min{1, s, (s + u)/2} = (s + u)/2,

and the same estimate for FA . Integrate and repeat. Starting at u = s/2, the finite iterates
increase to s. For each prescribed error only finitely many repetitions are needed. All tested
parents, increments, radii, and repetitions are fixed before choosing the small-parameter losses. A
diagonal over those finite choices gives FA (d), FB (d) ≥ sd. Apply Lemma 2.2 separately at each
full pin to the common original bin event H. On the same normalized law R = P( · | H), outside
pin mass at most η for each choice of pin, the actual conditional direction caps hold with the
additional factor (mH η)−1 . This proves the claimed conditional mass bounds without normalizing
an intersection of the two pin filters.

7.5    Three labels and the full-precision slices
Assume first that (7.22) holds for the one-label parameter population. Use the globally defined
parameter-tree atoms from the one-label construction. Their simultaneous bins, parent filters, and

same current λ on every retained tested child. These are the conditional masses of the named
one-label population used below.
    They cover a later fixed real parent j and increment a by a finite mesh. Choose tested depths
j− < j < j+ < b− < j + a within mesh size δmesh of j and j + a. For the compatible nested prefixes

     Let l(r) be the inherited scalar profile of ζ2,r given the full pair under R0 . The pair-only
