A slow diagonal makes the excluded mass vanish at t = ϑn . This pair-only filter leaves conditional
laws at retained pairs unchanged. With α = |θ1 − θ0 |, it gives α−1 ≤ ρ−ϑn = ρ−o(1) . On each
remaining pair b, feed the point–third-parameter comparison and the two coordinate products
supplied by the i = 0, 1 star comparisons into one simultaneous core. Let Mb be the total raw
retained mass relative to Rsc ( · | b), including the point-likelihood trim and all core deletions.
Then the new third marginal satisfies λ01 ≤ Mb−1 λ0b . All inverse retentions use the same subpower
envelope. Hence
                                      λ01 {ζ2,r ∈ I} ≤ ρsr−o(1) .                             (7.24)
The two coordinate products now use actual marginals of that same retained point law. This
establishes their coexistence with the scalar slope bound before any point-coordinate slice.
    Fix such a pair, and write θ1 − θ0 = αd, where α−1 has subpower size. Before the last trim
form the real linear coordinates

                               N = d⊥ · U0 = d⊥ · U1 ,            L = d · U1 .

Bin N, L at precision ρ. Enlarge the U0 symbol to include the N bin, which has boundedly many
possibilities given it, and use (N, L) as the U1 symbol. Rotated and original square bins determine
one another with bounded ambiguity.
   Let νe⋆ (P, θ2 ) denote the joint law just obtained from the fixed-pair core, with actual point
marginal νe. It has
                                      νe⋆ (P, θ2 ) ≤ A νe(P )λ01 (θ2 )
with subpower A, and its point marginal carries both coordinate products and the X, Z1 prefix
upper masses. The bounded new symbols change the coordinate multiinformations by O(1) in
natural-log units. Apply Lemma 2.5 and the simultaneous marginal core to these two coordinate
products on the point law νe alone. The likelihood events and all core deletions are functions of
the point and the already fixed pair. They therefore give a point-only weight 0 ≤ w(P ) ≤ 1 of
subpower retained mass M = Eνew > 0. Lift that weight to the joint law and put

                                        w(P )νe⋆ (P, θ2 )                  w(P )νe(P )
                       ν ⋆ (P, θ2 ) =                     ,    ν(P ) =                 .
                                              M                               M
The point–free-label comparison is then exactly

                                        ν ⋆ (P, θ2 ) ≤ A ν(P )λ01 (θ2 ).                      (7.25)

There is no new factor M −1 in this comparison: the same point weight and normalization occur in
its actual point marginal. The coordinate core gives (7.18) for the new finite symbols on ν, and
the X, Z1 upper masses lose at most the subpower factor M −1 .
    The fixed labels are still J = {0, 1} and label 2 remains free. Both N and X are finite functions
of the point and these fixed labels. By Corollary 2.8, every positive (N, X) slice of (7.25) keeps
the same pre-slice comparison λ01 and the actual sliced point marginal. In particular its scalar
slope bound remains available. No exact real coordinate is conditioned on.
    We prove carefully that the population (L, Z1 ) conditional on (N, X) has ball dimension at
least x + z. If a U0 cell with center u0 is fixed, then L is within O(ρ) of d · u0 + αX. The first
product in (7.18) and the marginal X bound give, for an interval I of length r ≥ ρ (absorbing the
constant enlargement),
                                   ν(L ∈ I | U0 = u0 ) ≤ K1 Cα−x rx ,                          (7.26)



                                                       61
where C is subpower. Average this inequality over u0 conditional on N = n. This proves the same
bound for L | N = n.
   The second product in (7.18), after rotating U1 to (N, L), gives the exact finite inequality

                                                  νX (t)νN (n)
                    ν(l, z | N = n, X = t) ≤ K1                ν(l | N = n)νZ1 (z).             (7.27)
                                                   νX,N (t, n)

The ν-probability of the values (t, n) where the displayed ratio exceeds A > 1 is at most A−1 :
sum νX,N (t, n) < A−1 νX (t)νN (n) over those values. On the remaining slices, (7.26) and the Z1
marginal bound imply

                       ν((L, Z1 ) ∈ I × J | N = n, X = t) ≤ K12 AC 2 α−x rx+z                   (7.28)

for intervals of length r. Take A → ∞ with subpower growth. Thus there is no loss proportional
to the number of full-precision X or N bins.
    The free label-2 law on these slices uses the same comparison λ01 from (7.24): the point-only
identity (7.25) and its (N, X) slicing were verified above. Hence the free scalar slope bound is
available on these slices.
    In (7.20), the change from frame 1 to frame 2 is a scalar projection of (L, Z1 ): its slope is
flip(d) · (θ2 − θ1 ), and the remaining terms are translations determined by (N, X) and the free
label. The support count of Z2 per label is still at most ρ−z−o(1) , because every restriction only
removed original neighbors. Apply Theorem 3.2 to (7.28) and (7.24). We obtain

                                 z ≥ min{1, x + z, (x + z + s)/2}.                              (7.29)

For x > 0 and z < 1 this is exactly z ≥ x + s.
    For the second test, return to a fresh copy of the pre-average point–label law R, restrict it to
its good label-0 values from Proposition 7.3, and marginalize label 2. This lifts the pair-only filter
to the same incidence law; the star comparisons and label-0 coordinate support remain on one
graph. The conditional direction law of label 1 at each retained label 0 is unchanged and has rate
s. Fix a typical label 0 and apply the simultaneous core to the point–label-1 comparison and the
label-0 coordinate product. Its new label-1 marginal retains the direction cap with a subpower
inverse mass loss. Then fix the point coordinates X, Z0 ; label 1 is free. The point population
is U0 , of dimension q on typical slices by that coordinate product. Its direction comparison is
this retained pre-slice label-1 marginal, for the direction flip(θ1 − θ0 ). The fixed-label core uses
the i = 0 comparison in (7.17); Corollary 2.8 preserves this same direction population on the
subsequent (X, Z0 ) slice. Equation (7.20) projects U0 to the Z1 support, giving

                                      z ≥ min{1, q, (q + s)/2}.                                 (7.30)

If q > 2 − s, both other terms exceed or equal 1, so z ≥ 1. This proves the last assertion in the
absence of strip concentration.
    All rotations and scalar slices just used have a finite-grid meaning. Rotated finest-scale squares
meet boundedly many original squares and conversely. Add the two descriptions and their bounded
neighbor tags when conditioning. Equation (7.20) then holds up to O(ρ); all interval bounds use
constant enlargements. The factors α−1 and the harmless angular separations can be bounded by
ρ−η for each fixed η > 0 before the limit. Replacing a finest scale by a ρ−O(η) enlargement changes
the displayed exponent inequalities by O(η), which is sent to zero afterwards.



                                                  62
7.6   Concentration in a strip
It remains to justify the alternative omitted above. Test the original retained one-label marginal λ
before sampling replicas. If (7.22) fails along some subsequence at a fixed depth t > 0, pass to
a subsequence and choose a strip of width ρt whose λ-mass is ρo(1) . This follows by choosing a
sequence on which the nonnegative exponent of the maximal strip mass tends to zero. Otherwise
a positive lower bound for that exponent supplies (7.22) at each fixed depth, and a diagonal
supplies all the depths used above. Subpower product domination then prevents concentration of
a replicated marginal that was absent from the one-label population.
    Restrict the one-label graph to the chosen strip and normalize it as the new p0 . Its mass in
the previous law is subpower, so the old named products give the small information required by
Lemma 7.2. Shorten the working block to a fixed positive depth no greater than t, if necessary,
and fix its prefix list before taking the small-parameter limit. Intersect the fresh bins with all
typical-parent filters needed for the inherited conditional bounds, keep this allowed restriction
unnormalized, and apply the lemma once for that finite list. Relabel its output π and its actual
pair marginals µ, λ. Its pair and coordinate products now use actual factors before any new
replicas are sampled, and its support still lies in the chosen strip. In these shorter block units
the coordinate rates remain x, q, z, by (7.8); the time and joint parameter rates remain at least
s. The strip is now as thin as the finest precision. Let d0 be its unit direction. Both coordinates
of d0 have exponent zero. Indeed, if (say) its first coordinate had magnitude at most ρb along
a subsequence with b > 0, the bounded strip would lie in a time-coordinate interval of a fixed
positive depth. Its subpower retained mass contradicts the time marginal bound in (7.15). The
second coordinate is treated using the positive normal marginal bound. In particular

                                | flip(d0 ) · d0 | = 2|(d0 )1 (d0 )2 | = ρo(1) .

    The scalar position along the strip has prefix ball dimension at least s: projection to the time
coordinate has this bound, and its slope along the strip has exponent zero. After sampling three
labels over the restricted graph, typical pairs have separation ρo(1) by this scalar bound. Their
differences are parallel to d0 up to finest-scale errors, so the scalar third slope flip(d0 ) · θ2 has
dimension at least s directly. Typical fixed-pair comparisons preserve it. The proof of (7.29),
including both products at labels 0, 1, now applies with d0 in place of d: perpendicular invariance
and parallel transfer have only finest-scale errors. Thus x > 0, z < 1 again gives z ≥ x + s.
    For the remaining test, start from a fresh copy of the strip star before the preceding fixed-pair
restrictions, then fix label 0 and X, leaving label 1 free. Set B0 = flip(d0 ) · U0 . If q > 2 − s, then
q > 1. A strip of width r for B0 is covered by O(r−1 ) balls of radius r in the bounded U0 -plane.
Hence its mass is at most Crq−1 , with subpower C. The label-0 coordinate product, after the
typical X slice, gives ball dimension at least q − 1 + z for (B0 , Z0 ). The free comparison law is
the retained label-1 marginal; its scalar position along the strip has dimension s. Equation (7.20)
projects this pair to Z1 with that scalar slope, up to a known translation and finest-scale errors.
Theorem 3.2 gives
                               z ≥ min{1, q − 1 + z, (q − 1 + z + s)/2}.
If z < 1 and q > 2 − s, each expression inside this minimum is strictly greater than z: for the last
one use q − 1 + s > 1 > z. This is impossible. Consequently z < 1 implies q ≤ 2 − s also in the
strip case.
    This completes the proof of Proposition 7.1. The order is the block sequence (hn , ϵn ) first, the
homogeneous scalar and direction profiles second, and their tested depths and positive derivative
increments third. At that third stage every test fixes its finite list before taking a subsequence

                                                      63
limit in n, and uniform trims precede all restrictions using their bounds. The derivative increment
is sent to zero only afterwards. Each prescribed accuracy in the directional bootstrap requires
finitely many iterations; a diagonal over those finite tests proves the exact inequalities. Thus
neither a new hn chosen from a later direction profile nor a uniform differentiability modulus
across profiles is required.


8     Closure of the classical growth argument
We complete the proof of Theorem 4.2. Fix 0 < γ < 1 and suppose for a contradiction that β > γ.
The least-aspect construction and isotropic exclusion leave the matched process with m > 0 used
in Sections 6 and 7. We keep the same saturated homogeneous component, so its functions b, S, Φ
and its root level τ are the ones selected before the trace point and block scales.
    On the aperture region (6.4), the potential Φ satisfies the obstacle (6.6) and meets it along
i = b(u). The trace theorem supplies the plane rates x, y, w, z, D, with the single middle rate
q = aU when m = 1. Proposition 7.1 constrains these rates at generic plane points with i > 0. At
almost every contact time, p = −b′ (u) belongs to [0, 1 + m] and s = S ′ (u) belongs to (0, 1]. We
now transfer the generic rate constraints to tuples on the inward sides of the contact curve, obtain
a positive tangent rate there, and combine them with the contact relation.
    All information and entropy expressions in this section are limiting normalized quantities. In
every comparison involving an additional fixed depth, its finite-scale ambiguity tends to zero before
that depth is sent to zero.

8.1    Rate tuples at a contact point
For distinct clocks put
                      B = x + y + w + z − 2,                                                      (8.1)
                  F (P ) = 2P − m − P x + (1 − P )y + (m − P )w + (1 + m − P )z.                  (8.2)
For tied clocks these definitions mean
                B = x + q + z − 2,         F (P ) = 2P − 1 − P x + (1 − P )q + (2 − P )z.         (8.3)
In either case F ′ (P ) = −B, with the rates held fixed.
Lemma 8.1 (Contact tuples). At almost every u0 ∈ (0, 1), set
                            b0 = b(u0 ),       p = −b′ (u0 ),    s = S ′ (u0 ).
The following assertions hold on each side of the contact curve that lies inward from the aperture
boundary. Minus denotes smaller i and plus denotes larger i.
    1. Rescale area measure on a fixed bounded region of positive area in a tangent half-plane and
       form the joint distribution of the generic plane rates. Tuples in a subsequential limiting
       support obey the capacity bounds and all the implications of Proposition 7.1, with the fixed
       value s. Every data rate with clock cj ̸= p, and also D, has a fixed value on each such support,
       equal to its one-sided strong trace. Here cj ∈ {0, 1, m, 1 + m}, with the two middle clocks
       grouped when m = 1.
    2. Every tuple on either side satisfies
                                           F (p) + D = 2s − β − γm.                               (8.4)

                                                    64
  3. There is a minus tuple with B ≤ 0 whenever that side is admissible, and there is a plus tuple
     with B ≥ 0 whenever that side is admissible.
  4. If both sides are admissible and p < 1 + m, then

                                     D− − D+ ≥ (1 + m − p)(z + − z − ).                        (8.5)

If b0 = 0, then p = 0 and the plus side is admissible. If b0 + (1 + m)u0 = L, then p = 1 + m and
the minus side is admissible.

Proof. Choose a common differentiability point of b, S which is a Lebesgue point of S ′ and a point
of the strong curve traces in Lemma 6.3. The assertions concerning aperture boundaries follow by
differentiating the nonnegative Lipschitz functions b(u) and L − b(u) − (1 + m)u at a zero. Their
derivative is zero at every such interior differentiability point.
    For h ↓ 0 take a locally uniform subsequential limit

                                    Φ(b0 + hξ, u0 + hη) − Φ(b0 , u0 )
                      ϕh (ξ, η) =                                     −→ ϕ(ξ, η).
                                                   h
Uniform local Lipschitz bounds give this compactness. On the tangent line ξ + pη = 0, contact
and differentiability give

                       ϕ(−pη, η) = kη,         k = −2p + (1 − γ)m + 2s − β.                    (8.6)

On each inward half-plane the obstacle becomes

                          ϕ(ξ, η) ≥ ℓ(ξ, η) := 2ξ + ((1 − γ)m + 2s − β)η.                      (8.7)

These assertions include the boundary line by continuity. They follow first on compact subsets of
the limiting aperture domain and then on its boundary by the common Lipschitz bound.
   The plane derivative identities (6.11) are

                     Φi = x + y + w + z,         Φu = y + mw + (1 + m)z + D,

with the grouped interpretation at m = 1. By Lemma 6.3, the expression

                   Φu − pΦi = −px + (1 − p)y + (m − p)w + (1 + m − p)z + D

converges strongly in mean on each tangent half-ball to a constant. Indeed every rate appearing
with nonzero coefficient is transverse to the contact curve; a possibly untraced rate has cj = p
and coefficient zero. Passing to weak derivatives of ϕh therefore shows that (∂η − p∂ξ )ϕ = k± is
constant on each half-plane. In coordinates r = ξ + pη, η, this implies ϕ(r − pη, η) = k± η + H± (r)
locally. Continuity at r = 0 and (8.6) give k± = k. Adding 2p − m proves (8.4).
    Here is a precise way to choose tuples and the horizontal signs. Fix a bounded strip

                              Ω+ = {(ξ, η) : |η| < a, 0 < ξ + pη < a}

on an admissible plus side, or its counterpart Ω− with −a < ξ + pη < 0. Sample the generic rate
vector at (b0 + hξ, u0 + hη) with uniform area measure on that strip, including S ′ (u0 + hη) in
the vector. Intersections with the actual inward domain, if necessary, change a set of relative
area o(1). Boundedness gives a weakly convergent subsequence of these probability laws. The
strong traces fix every transverse rate and D on the support, and the Lebesgue-point property

                                                   65
fixes the last coordinate to s. Each strict-hypothesis implication in Proposition 7.1 persists on this
support: a violation with a strictly positive source or a strictly subunit target would hold on an
open neighborhood disjoint from every sufficiently late sampled law. Thus all these tuples have
the claimed generic constraints and the identity just proved.
    For almost every fixed η, horizontal integration of (8.7), using equality at r = 0, gives
                Z 0                                         Z a
                      ∂ξ (ϕ − ℓ)(r − pη, η) dr ≤ 0,               ∂ξ (ϕ − ℓ)(r − pη, η) dr ≥ 0
                 −a                                          0

on the corresponding admissible sides. Since the limiting horizontal derivative is the weak limit of
B, the mean of B in the minus tuple law is nonpositive and in the plus tuple law is nonnegative.
A continuous function on a compact support cannot have nonpositive mean while being strictly
positive everywhere, nor nonnegative mean while being strictly negative everywhere. This supplies
the two required tuples. The same construction can be made for any fixed bounded subset of a
tangent half-plane; only finitely many such subsequences will be used below.
   Finally, the plane measure inequality (6.21) is

                                       (∂u − (1 + m)∂i )z ≥ ∂i D.

When p < 1 + m, both z and D have strong traces. Rescaling this inequality at the contact point
leaves piecewise constant limits on the two half-planes. Their distributional derivatives across
ξ + pη = 0 have coefficients (p − 1 − m)(z + − z − ) and D+ − D− , respectively. Positivity of the
limiting measure gives (8.5).

8.2   Aperture refinement forces a positive tangent rate
Lemma 8.2 (Positive X rate). At the contact points of Lemma 8.1, x− ≥ γ if p > 0. If p = 0, an
admissible plus tuple can be chosen with x ≥ γ > 0.

Proof. Fix such a point and let E0 be its contact observation, with labels Cu0 , Rmu0 and data
depths
                         b0 , b0 + u0 , b0 + mu0 , b0 + (1 + m)u0 .
Keep these labels and refine X, Y by a depth d, where 0 < d < mu0 . The resulting observation
is an admissible aperture with width depths (b0 + d, b0 + mu0 ); the old orientation prefix is an
allowed extra label. Passing to the required coarser orientation prefix of depth mu0 − d creates
normal velocity uncertainty at most O(ϵmu0 −d ϵb0 +d ) = O(ϵb0 +mu0 ); the position calculation has
the same extra factor ϵu0 on both sides. Thus the old-frame boxes and a standard aperture test
have bounded ambiguity. This aperture refinement is available also when m = 1; it does not use
the grouped auxiliary profile. Its weight increases by (1 + γ)d. Comparing its universal score
bound with the saturated score at E0 yields

                                  H(Xb0 +d , Yb0 +u0 +d | E0 ) ≥ (1 + γ)d.

The Y refinement, even after conditioning on the new X, has entropy at most d, by scalar box
counting. Consequently the nondecreasing Lipschitz profile

                      g(d) := H(Xb0 +d | E0 )   satisfies    g(0) = 0,         g(d) ≥ γd.        (8.8)

These profiles can be extracted along the same sequence, first on countably many depths and then
by their Lipschitz extension.

                                                      66
   Choose 0 < λ < min{1, m}, put v = u0 − h, and consider

                            b0 ≤ i ≤ b0 + λh,        h > 0 sufficiently small.                        (8.9)

We claim that E0 together with Xi determines the plane observation E(i, v) to zero information
cost. The earlier labels are coarsenings of those in E0 . With

                  c = Cv − Cu0 ,         r = Rmu0 − Rmv ,          |c| ≲ ϵv ,   |r| ≲ ϵmv ,

the change of coordinates is

              Yv = Yu0 + cX,      Wv = Wu0 + rX,           Zv = Zu0 + cWu0 + rYu0 + crX.

Only errors within the recorded boxes matter, since all their centers and the label shifts are
known. The old Y error fits depth i + v because i − b0 < h, and the old W error fits depth i + mv
because i − b0 < mh; the terms cX, rX fit those depths using Xi . For Zv the four error depths
are, respectively,

         b0 + (1 + m)u0 ,     b0 + (1 + m)u0 − h,         b0 + (1 + m)u0 − mh,       i + (1 + m)v.

All are at least the target depth i + (1 + m)v, by i − b0 < min{1, m}h. Thus the asserted
determination holds with bounded box ambiguity, which vanishes in normalized entropy.
    For a further fixed t > 0 with i + t < b0 + mu0 , conditioning on E0 , Xi therefore gives

                            H(Xi+t | E(i, v)) ≥ g(i − b0 + t) − g(i − b0 ).                          (8.10)

The left side is the increase of particle information when just LX is raised by t at the plane point.
This is an inward direction of the resolution cone. At almost every (i, v) the affine cone blow-up
in Theorem 6.1 supplies a sequence t ↓ 0 along which this increase divided by t tends to x(i, v).
At almost every i, the right side divided by t tends to g ′ (i − b0 ). Hence

                                         x(i, u0 − h) ≥ g ′ (i − b0 )

at almost every point of (8.9). Fubini’s theorem and (8.8) imply, for almost every sufficiently small
h,
                            Z b0 +λh
                                       x(i, u0 − h) di ≥ g(λh) − g(0) ≥ γλh.                         (8.11)
                             b0

    Integrate also over h ∈ [ρ, 2ρ] and send ρ ↓ 0. If p > 0, take λ < p as well. Differentiability
gives b(u0 − h) = b0 + ph + o(h), uniformly in this range, so the region lies on the minus side for
small ρ. Since cX = 0 ̸= p, the strong trace of x makes its average tend to x− . Inequality (8.11)
gives x− ≥ γ.
    If p = 0, the same region is on the plus side apart from area o(ρ2 ). Removing that part
changes the average by o(1), since 0 ≤ x ≤ 1. A subsequential tuple law from the remaining region
therefore has mean x at least γ, and its compact support contains a tuple with x ≥ γ. All its other
transverse components are the same plus traces used in Lemma 8.1. This proves the assertion
without requiring a trace for the characteristic component x.




                                                     67
8.3   The finite sign calculation
Lemma 8.3 (Algebraic rate bounds). Let m > 0, 0 < s ≤ 1, and let a rate tuple obey the capacity
bounds and the implications of Proposition 7.1. Put p∗ = (1 + m)/2. Then

                             x > 0, z < 1      =⇒       F (p∗ ) ≥ 1 + s,                     (8.12)
                             x > 0, z = 1      =⇒       F (0) ≥ 1 + s,                       (8.13)
                             x = 0, z < 1      =⇒       F (1 + m) ≥ 1 + s.                   (8.14)

Moreover, if x > 0, z < 1, and 0 ≤ p ≤ p∗ , replacing z by 1 in F (p) gives a number at least 1 + s.

Proof. First suppose m ̸= 1. If x > 0 and z < 1, the chains imply

                         w ≥ x,       y ≥ x + s,        z ≥ y,      z ≥ w + s.               (8.15)

Indeed x > 0 first gives positive y, w; if y or w were 1, the corresponding chain to z would force
z = 1. The non-saturated branches of the time chains then give y ≥ x + s and z ≥ w + s. The
other two chains give w ≥ x and z ≥ y. Thus

                              0 ≤ x ≤ w ≤ 1 − s,           x + s ≤ y ≤ 1.                    (8.16)

The midpoint identity

                                     (y − x) + (z − w) + m(w − x) + m(z − y)
                     F (p∗ ) − 1 =
                                                         2
proves (8.12) for every m > 0, including m < 1. If instead x > 0, z = 1, the first time chain gives
y ≥ s, so
                                   F (0) = 1 + y + mw ≥ 1 + s.
If x = 0, z < 1, the chain w → z gives w ≤ 1 − s whenever w > 0; this bound also holds for w = 0.
Hence
                           F (1 + m) = 2 + m − my − w ≥ 2 − w ≥ 1 + s.
   For the last assertion write the expression after replacement as

                             A = 1 + p − px + (1 − p)y + (m − p)w.

If 0 ≤ p ≤ 1 and p ≤ p∗ , the exact identity

                        A − (1 + s) = (1 − p)(y − x − s) + (1 + m − 2p)x
                                         + m(w − x) + p(1 − s − w)

is a sum of nonnegative terms by (8.16). In particular it covers the whole range 0 ≤ p ≤ p∗
when m < 1, even when m − p < 0 in the original expression for A. If 1 < p ≤ p∗ , necessarily
m ≥ 2p − 1 > p, and the exact identity

                          A − (1 + s) = (p − 1)(1 − y) + (m − p)(w − x)
                                          + (1 + m − 2p)x + (1 − s − x)

again has nonnegative terms. This covers the remaining range for m > 1 and agrees with the first
calculation at p = 1.

                                                   68
   For m = 1, use q rather than separate middle rates. The three relevant evaluations are

                 F (1) = 1 + z − x,      F (0) z=1 = 1 + q,     F (2) x=0 = 3 − q.

The tied-clock implications z ≥ x + s when x > 0, z < 1, q ≥ s when x > 0, and q ≤ 2 − s
when z < 1 prove (8.12)–(8.14). For 0 ≤ p ≤ 1 = p∗ the expression with z replaced by 1 is
A = 1 + p − px + (1 − p)q, and

                         A − (1 + s) = p(1 − s − x) + (1 − p)(q − s) ≥ 0.

Here x ≤ 1 − s follows from z ≥ x + s and z < 1.

8.4   Contradiction and completion
Completion of the proof of Theorem 4.2. Choose a contact point satisfying the preceding lemmas,
and keep its p, s fixed. The identity (8.4) has right side strictly below the algebraic threshold:

                                       2s − β − γm < 1 + s,                                   (8.17)

since s ≤ 1 and β > γ > 0. All label rates D are nonnegative.
    Suppose first that p ≥ p∗ . Since p > 0, the point cannot lie on the lower aperture boundary, so
the minus side is admissible. Choose there a tuple with B ≤ 0. Lemma 8.2 gives x = x− ≥ γ > 0.
For this tuple F is nondecreasing. If z < 1, then F (p) ≥ F (p∗ ) ≥ 1 + s; if z = 1, then
F (p) ≥ F (0) ≥ 1 + s. Both alternatives contradict (8.4) and (8.17). This includes p = p∗ and the
upper boundary value p = 1 + m. At the latter value z can lack a strong trace, but its coefficient
in F (p) is zero and the selected tuple is sufficient.
    It remains that 0 ≤ p < p∗ . In this range p < 1 + m, so the point cannot be on the upper
aperture boundary and the plus side is admissible. Choose a plus tuple with B ≥ 0, for which F
is nonincreasing. If it had z < 1, the case x > 0 would give F (p) ≥ F (p∗ ) ≥ 1 + s, and the case
x = 0 would give F (p) ≥ F (1 + m) ≥ 1 + s. Either is impossible. Thus this tuple has z = 1. Since
the Z clock is transverse for p < 1 + m, the fixed plus trace satisfies z + = 1, and every plus tuple
has that same value.
    If p = 0, take the plus tuple with x > 0 from Lemma 8.2. It too has z = 1, so F (0) ≥ 1 + s, a
contradiction. This handles the lower aperture boundary even though X is then characteristic and
need not have a strong trace.
    Finally suppose 0 < p < p∗ . Both sides are admissible, and x− > 0. The Z rate has strong
traces on both sides. If z − = 1, choose a minus tuple with B ≤ 0; its nondecreasing F would
satisfy F (p) ≥ F (0) ≥ 1 + s. Therefore z − < 1. For any minus tuple the jump estimate, z + = 1,
and D+ ≥ 0 give
                                     D− ≥ (1 + m − p)(1 − z − ).
Consequently
                            F (p) + D− ≥ F (p) + (1 + m − p)(1 − z − ).
The right side is exactly F (p) with its z entry replaced by 1. It is at least 1 + s by Lemma 8.3,
once more contradicting (8.4) and (8.17).
   The three ranges m < 1, m = 1, and m > 1 have all been included in the algebraic lemma.
At the intermediate values p = 1 or p = m, an untraced middle component has zero tangential
coefficient; the tuple argument and the nonnegative identities above remain valid. Thus no
matched-clock least-aspect extremizer can exist under β > γ. The isotropic alternative was already
excluded by Proposition 5.1. The contradiction proves β ≤ γ, completing Theorem 4.2.

                                                 69
9     Packet scores and finite decompositions
The classical theorem controls positive masses transported along exact lines. For oscillatory
packets, an observed mass is the square of a coherent sum, and an earlier packet reaches the output
within its own position uncertainty, up to controlled tails. We therefore begin with analytic mass
and decomposition estimates before introducing any positive auxiliary law. The packet score below
is designed to compose across a time cut. This section establishes its preliminary growth bound;
the next two sections reduce excess growth to the uncertainty boundary and exclude it by a finite
information argument.
    The transverse dimension is two and 0 < γ < 1. All entropies are normalized by ℓ = log(1/ϵ),
and log1/ϵ (r) = log r/ℓ. Constants in analytic estimates are independent of ϵ and of the input
function.

9.1   The phase class and the growth supremum
The longitudinal coordinate is c ∈ [0, 1], the input variable is v ∈ R2 , and the observation position
is x ∈ R2 . The phase is defined on [0, 1] × R2 × Uv , for an open neighborhood Uv of the bounded
input patch. We use real phases satisfying

                                     ∂v Φ(c, x, v) = ζ(v, x − cv).                               (9.1)

The function ζ is defined for every p ∈ R2 and on all bounded v-patches needed in the argument.
On each such patch, ∂p ζ(v, p) is symmetric and either

                               a0 I ≤ ∂p ζ(v, p) ≤ a1 I       for all (v, p),                    (9.2)

or the negative of this inequality holds. Here a0 > 0. For some fixed AΦ ≥ 1, all derivatives of
order j of this matrix are bounded by Aj+1       3                        −1

between applications; they are uniform within each sequence in which ϵ → 0. Input-only terms in

coordinates; this changes depths by at most log 2/ℓ.
    An observation O = (Ct , V, X) uses velocity and position lattices of spacings ρt and σt ,

The input belongs to L2 and has bounded support. The amplitudes have fixed compact supports

    At the root, the canonical mass is the supremum in (9.4) over amplitudes supported in
{|z| ≤ 100} with ∥∂ α ψ∥∞ ≤ 100|α|+1 (|α|!)10 . The root grids are fixed. The position cutoff is

the supremum definition: a failure for fixed bounds would select a sequence contradicting the
claimed bound on βq .

9.2   Bessel estimates and changes of scale
Fourier localization and almost orthogonality are the standard analytic ingredients of wave-packet
decomposition; see, for example, Tao [35] and Guo et al. [10]. We give the estimates with the
phase class and uniform amplitude bounds required by our packet score, including changes of scale
and quantified tails.

Lemma 9.2 (Uniform packet Bessel estimates). At a fixed time anchor, packets with natural
spacings ρ in velocity and σ = (λρ)−1 in position satisfy

finitely many phase and amplitude derivative bounds, support sizes, and lattice multiplicity, and
not on the number of sites or on the position cutoff.
    More precisely, packets at this same time and scale obey, for every integer M ≥ 1,

                     |⟨gV,X , gV ′ ,X ′   ⟩| ≤ CM 1{|V −V ′ |≤Cρ}       1+                           .   (9.10)

At a local root, where both natural spacings equal s, let F be any allowed phase-space cap and let

    P

Here a fixed enlargement of the support sizes is included in F R . Every axis of F is at least s;
consequently F R is covered by at most CR4 caps of the same widths.

Proof. Only overlapping velocity supports contribute. Put v = V + ρz. For the phase difference
at equal time, its gradient in z is

                                   λρ{ζ(v, X − cv) − ζ(v, X ′ − cv)}.

The braces equal B(v)(X − X ′ ), where B(v) is the integral of ∂p ζ along the segment between the
two spatial arguments. The sign and lower bound in (9.2) give gradient size at least a0 |X − X ′ |/σ.
Each higher derivative is bounded by Cj |X − X ′ |/σ. Repeated use of

                                                ∇φ · ∇z iφ
                                                       e = eiφ
                                                i|∇φ|2

therefore gives (9.10): derivatives of its coefficients are bounded by the corresponding inverse
gradient powers. The estimate with a factor 1 + |X − X ′ |/σ also covers bounded separation.
Taking M > 2, the row and column sums of the Gram matrix are bounded, since there are only
boundedly many overlapping velocity centers and the position lattice is two-dimensional. The
Schur estimate proves synthesis Bessel; its adjoint proves analysis Bessel.
   For supremum masses, choose independently at every site of an arbitrary finite subset an
amplitude whose squared pairing is within a fixed factor of that site’s supremum. The same Gram

                                                           72
estimate is uniform in all these choices. Taking the supremum and then exhausting the sites proves
the assertion.
    For (9.11), split the synthesis coefficients into F R and its complement. Analysis and synthesis
Bessel applied successively bound the first part by its coefficient square sum. For the other part,
a nonzero Gram entry either has spatial separation at least a fixed multiple of Rs, or is zero by
disjoint velocity supports. Summing (9.10) outside that spatial radius gives row and column sums
at most CM R2−M . The resulting operator norm squared is at most CM R4−2M . The inequality
|a + b|2 ≤ 2|a|2 + 2|b|2 proves the claim after changing constants. This argument is again uniform
over choices of canonical amplitudes. The covering count follows by enlarging each of the four
coordinates: an interval of length at least s, enlarged by Rs, needs at most CR original-length
intervals.

    Moving a center by a bounded number of its lattice spacings changes only an admissible
amplitude factor, after removal of a scalar phase. The same is true when the time anchor moves
inside its box and X is transported by that displacement times V . Indeed, after a scalar phase
is removed, the first derivative is bounded in packet units; the higher derivatives of the phase
change are bounded by Cj λρ2t ϵt = Cj . This also proves the bounded ambiguity assertions used
with shifted grids below.
    Fix 0 ≤ r < t, fixed widths b, a, and 0 ≤ b∗ ≤ b. The geometric localization label is
                                       J0 = (Cr , Vb∗ , (X + (Cr − Ct )V )r+b∗ ) .                                   (9.12)
Subscripts on vectors denote grid cells. The endpoint observation and its aperture each determine
this label to bounded ambiguity; adjoining the bounded neighboring-cell choices makes the
determination exact. Write VJ , XJ for its velocity center and its trajectory position at Cr . Let
sv = ϵb∗ , sc = ϵr , sx = sc sv . In exact dyadic coordinates take the actual length of the time box for

                v = V J + sv v ′ ,                                          c = Cr + sc (c′ − 1/2),                  (9.13)

new gradient function is
                                       ζ(v, XJ − Cr v + sx (p′ + v ′ /2)) − ζ(v, XJ − Cr v)

                                                                                                               ′

becomes an aperture at r after adding b∗ to both widths. Thus returning a score to the original
coordinates adds b∗ to its weight contribution. The local inputs used below are sums of windows
with centers in a fixed enlargement of the velocity cell of J0 . Their supports are therefore bounded
in v ′ , uniformly in the localizer.

9.3    A finite Fourier decomposition with quantified tails
We state error sizes before taking any limits. At fixed depths and bounded domains all the
observation lattices, retained atom sets, and cap menus below have at most Cϵ−Q elements for
some fixed Q. The logarithmic root cutoff contributes a factor which can also be absorbed in this
bound by increasing Q. This exponent does not bound the number of arbitrary extra labels; those
are accounted for separately.

of atoms
