fixed constants and proves all assertions.

   The tolerance in (9.19) is σr , the earlier position spacing. At the endpoint uncertainty scale it
can be larger than σt by a power. Thus this lemma alone does not identify coherent packet masses
with a positive law of straight trajectories at that finer precision.

9.4   Classes with cap count bounds
Here are explicit conventions for the finite errors in the remaining statements. Let ξ > 0 be the
mesh for coefficient and count exponents, and let S ≥ 1 bound the number of extra subdivision
and winner tags at each geometric localizer. Constants implicit in S may include powers of 1 + ℓ
from dyadic shape menus. With fixed data, η, ξ, A, an admissible packet error is
                                                                         1 + log(2 + ℓ)
                    epkt (ϵ) = C(η + ξ + log1/ϵ (S)) + Cdata,η,ξ,A                      .      (9.25)
                                                                                ℓ
The constants may be increased a finite number of times. There is no convergence assertion
uniform as η tends to zero: the order is first ϵ → 0, then η, ξ → 0, with log1/ϵ (S) → 0. If a
prescribed final error is needed, fix its small η, ξ first and choose all integration orders and ϵ0
afterwards.

                                                      76
Lemma 9.4 (Greedy cap classes). In a geometric localizer, bin nonnegligible coefficients so that
                                                
                                  | log1/ϵ |cT |2 − log1/ϵ (m) | ≤ ξ                            (9.26)

on each bin. Each bin admits a partition into classes, with a count parameter g and fixed assigned-
cap widths, having the following properties. If Nall is the full class size, then for every allowed
local root cap F ,
                      log1/ϵ (#{T in the class : T ∈ F }) + w(F ) ≤ g + epkt .                (9.27)
An empty count imposes no restriction. There is an assigned cap E = E(T ) whose nonempty fibers
in the full class satisfy

                           log1/ϵ (#{T : E(T ) = E}) ≥ g − w(E) − epkt .                        (9.28)

There are only a number of classes polynomial in 1 + ℓ, with constants depending on the fixed
exponents and ξ. Any further tagged subdivision preserves (9.27); its retained size is denoted by
N ≤ Nall . The lower bound (9.28) always refers to the full class.
Proof. Use the finite cap menu at local root widths up to min(L − r − b∗ , ur − b∗ ), with bounded
enlargements so every allowed cap is covered by boundedly many menu caps of the same widths
up to fixed factors. On the current remaining population R, choose a cap maximizing

                                     log1/ϵ (#(R ∩ E)) + w(E).

Assign its entire remaining contents to it and remove them. The successive maxima decrease.
Group removals by an exponent interval of length ξ for this maximum and by the two assigned
widths. Call an upper endpoint of the maximum interval g.
    The union in one such class lies inside the population present at its first removal. At that
stage every cap count plus its weight is at most g, which proves (9.27). For a removal belonging
to this class, its own maximum is at least g − ξ, proving (9.28). Replacing an arbitrary cap by
boundedly many menu caps changes the normalized bound by at most a fixed constant divided by
ℓ. Rounding widths changes the weight by the same amount. The available coefficient exponents,
after subtracting log1/ϵ ∥f ∥22 , lie in a fixed bounded interval after the cutoff in Lemma 9.3; so do
the maxima, since counts have exponent at most Q and weights are bounded at fixed depths. The
number of bins is therefore bounded in terms of ξ, and the dyadic width choices contribute only
O((1 + ℓ)2 ). Finally, subdividing can only decrease each cap count.

    For an endpoint observation in a localizer, choose a winning class by the largest absolute
pairing with its class superposition. Here and later it is enough to keep endpoint masses satisfying

                                             mO ≥ ϵA0 ∥f ∥22                                    (9.29)

for a sufficiently large fixed A0 . To see this without any assumption on the law, normalize ∥f ∥2 = 1.
The number of endpoint sites is at most Cϵ−Q , and w is bounded by a fixed constant W . A law on
masses smaller than the displayed threshold has score at most 32 Q + 12 W − 32 A0 + O(1/ℓ), whereas
K0 ≥ 0. Choose A0 > Q + W + 2. Splitting any law into this part and its complement changes
its averaged score by at most 3 log 2/(2ℓ). The low-mass part consequently cannot increase a
nonnegative upper growth bound.
    Choose the tail order with A > A0 /2 + 1. Write q = CA ϵA−A0 /2 , and reduce ϵ0 so q < 1/2. If
there are at most S possible winning parts, the triangle inequality gives the finite bound

                          mO (f ) ≤ (1 − q)−2 S 2 mO (hwin     2     win
                                                       J ) ≤ 4S mO (hJ ).                       (9.30)

                                                     77
Its loss in the mass term of the score is at most 3 log1/ϵ (S) + 3 log1/ϵ (1 − q)−1 . Thus no power-
                                                                                   

sized tail is discarded without comparison to an output mass floor. The label J below consists
of J0 , the mass and cap bins, and any extra subdivision and winning tags. The geometric part
is determined by both endpoint data and aperture; the additional tag entropy is bounded by
log1/ϵ (S).

9.5    The local root and the earlier test
Proposition 9.5 (Local root comparison). Fix a winning retained class as above, and use
translated coordinates (9.14). Let K0loc be the canonical root supremum of its input, including the
norm option. Set
                                                   3              1
                               AJ = log1/ϵ (N ) + log1/ϵ (m) + g.                            (9.31)
                                                   2              2
Then, with the error convention (9.25),

                                          K0loc ≤ AJ + epkt .                                     (9.32)

For any chosen marginal law on the tagged localizers there is an earlier test, using the original
input at time r, with score
                                                           3             1
                                                                             
                  Prall ≥ H(J) + b∗ + E log1/ϵ (Nall ) +     log1/ϵ (m) + g − epkt .              (9.33)
                                                           2             2
At r = 0, the right hand side is a lower bound for K0 up to the displayed error. The earlier test
uses the uniform law on each whole class before the final retained subdivision.

Proof. The local root natural spacings are both s = ϵur −b∗ . Let Mtot be the sum of all canonical
root masses of the retained superposition, and let MF be their sum in a root cap F . By analysis
and synthesis Bessel and the mass bin,

                                          Mtot ≤ Cϵ−ξ N m.                                        (9.34)

Take R = ϵ−η in (9.11). Its near set is covered by at most Cϵ−4η root caps of the same widths.
Lemma 9.4 therefore bounds its coefficient square sum by Cmϵw(F )−g−ξ−4η−epkt . The far term is at
most CM ϵη(2M −4) ϵ−ξ N m. Both N and the possible differences w(F ) − g have bounded exponents.
Choosing M sufficiently large, with those bounds fixed, absorbs the far term in the same estimate,
with an additional fixed constant. Consequently

                             log1/ϵ (MF ) ≤ log1/ϵ (m) + g − w(F ) + epkt .                       (9.35)

All canonical tests up to the logarithmic position cutoff are covered by the uniform Bessel argument;
no regularity constant grows with this cutoff.
    For an arbitrary root test law, the log-sum inequality gives

       H(O) + E log1/ϵ (mO ) ≤ log1/ϵ (Mtot ) ,    H(O | D) + E log1/ϵ (mO ) ≤ E log1/ϵ (MD ) .

The second inequality is applied separately on each aperture label; its observation support lies
in that label’s cap. Adding the first inequality and half the second, then adding 12 Ew(D), gives
(9.32). The norm option is bounded by 32 (log1/ϵ (N ) + log1/ϵ (m)) + epkt . Zero-width caps cover the
bounded local atom population in a fixed number of pieces, so (9.27) implies log1/ϵ (N ) ≤ g + epkt .
This proves the same bound for the norm option.

                                                  78
    For the earlier test choose the prescribed marginal on J, then choose T uniformly in its whole
class. Take its earlier site Or = (Cr , VT , XT ), and let Dr record J and the assigned cap E(T ).
Within J, the map from atom indices to earlier sites has bounded multiplicity. Conversely, an
earlier site lies in only boundedly many geometric localizers, because its center lies in a fixed
enlargement of the cell in (9.12). Extra tags cost at most log1/ϵ (S). It follows that

           H(Or ) ≥ H(J) + E log1/ϵ (Nall ) − epkt ,     H(Or | Dr ) ≥ E[g − w(E)] − epkt .

The second inequality uses the full assigned fibers in (9.28), with their actual probabilities under
the uniform class law. If one earlier site appears with several coefficient tests, use the largest
squared pairing there; this is still an admissible test. Thus its logarithmic mass is at least the
corresponding log1/ϵ (m) − epkt . At r = 0, (9.18) gives the same conclusion with the canonical mass.
Finally, the earlier aperture weight is 2b∗ + w(E), by the rescaling computation. Substitution in
the score proves (9.33).

9.6   The preliminary growth bound
Proposition 9.6 (A finite upper bound for packet growth). The supremum βq is finite and βq ≤ 1.
This conclusion uses no identification of the original coherent mass with a positive particle law.

Proof. Split into fixed aperture-width bins, at a cost tending to zero. Apply Lemma 9.3 with
r = b∗ = 0, then the class and winner construction. Restrict to the mass floor as justified above.
Work within one tagged localizer and its retained class. By (9.30), the original conditional score is
at most the score of the winning superposition plus epkt . For the remainder of this conditional
calculation, mO denotes the winning-superposition masses. They satisfy the inherited floor

                                     mO ≥ (1 − q)2 S −2 ϵA0 ∥f ∥22 .

The factor 3 log1/ϵ (1 − q)−1 is included in epkt by reducing ϵ0 ; for subpower tag counts the
                               

displayed floor still has a fixed polynomial exponent. Let Z = (Ct , P ), where P records both velocity
and back-transported position X + (C0 − Ct )V isotropically at depth a. Set mZ = O:O7→Z mO .
                                                                                          P

The log-sum inequality on each fiber shows

           H(O) + 12 H(O | D) + 32 E log1/ϵ (mO ) ≤ H(Z) + 21 H(Z | D) + 32 E log1/ϵ (mZ ) .    (9.36)

For clarity, the unconditional log-sum inequality bounds H(O | Z) + E log1/ϵ (mO /mZ ) by zero;
the same conditional on (Z, D), using a sum over a subset of the fiber, bounds its conditional
counterpart by zero. Their sum with coefficients one and one half is precisely the displayed
inequality.
    Let TZ consist of all class atoms compatible with P up to the enlargement in (9.19). The earlier
position uncertainty and the endpoint velocity uncertainty change these coarse columns by at most
Cϵ−η ϵa , since a ≤ ut ≤ u0 . Discarding interactions outside TZ is permitted by the quantitative
tail lemma. After fixing the coefficient cutoff B, increase only the transport integration order so
its error exponent Atr also permits summation over this fiber and comparison to that coefficient
floor. This does not require another coefficient truncation. Bessel at the fixed time Ct then gives

                                          mZ ≤ Cϵ−ξ m #TZ                                       (9.37)

after absorbing those tails. More explicitly, before absorption the right hand side has an added
term CAtr ϵ2Atr −Q ∥f ∥22 ; choosing 2Atr − Q > B + 1, and then reducing ϵ0 , absorbs it whenever

                                                   79
TZ ̸= ∅. The output floor excludes a nonempty retained fiber with no potential contributor. The
constant in (9.37) also absorbs the fixed factor from squaring the sum of a main term and its error.
    Only now introduce a positive auxiliary law: keep the given law of (Z, D), and choose T
uniformly in TZ , independently of D conditional on Z. Exactly,

                              H(T | Z, D) = H(T | Z) = E log1/ϵ (#TZ ) .

The chain rule gives the contributor calculation

                         H(Z) + 12 H(Z | D) + 23 H(T | Z)
                         = H(T ) + 12 H(T | D) + H(Z | T ) + 21 H(Z | T, D)
                         ≤ H(T ) + 12 H(T | D) + H(Ct | T ) + 23 H(Z | T, Ct ).                    (9.38)

Here Ct is determined by both Z and D. Thus H(Z | T ) = H(Ct | T ) + H(Z | T, Ct ), and
H(Z | T, D) ≤ H(Z | T, Ct ). This keeps exactly one conditional time entropy. The remaining term
measures the uncertainty in the coarse observation after the contributor and time are known.
   In the present root-referred observation, (T, Ct ) locates Z to at most Cϵ−Cη possibilities. Hence
H(Z | T, Ct ) ≤ Cη + O(1/ℓ). Combining (9.38) with (9.37) yields

 H(Z) + 12 H(Z | D) + 32 E log1/ϵ (mZ ) ≤ H(T ) + 23 log1/ϵ (m) + 12 H(T | D) + H(Ct | T ) + epkt . (9.39)

    For a fixed aperture label D, its potential contributors lie in a ϵ−Cη enlargement of the root
aperture of widths (b, a) obtained by referring its rectangles back to C0 . The velocity error is at
most Cρt ; the position error is at most Cϵ−η σ0 + Cρt . Both are bounded by the corresponding
enlarged coarse widths, because a ≤ ut . Covering this enlargement and applying (9.27) gives

       H(T | D) ≤ g − Ew(D) + epkt ,          H(T ) ≤ log1/ϵ (N ) ,    H(Ct | T ) ≤ t + O(1/ℓ).

Adding the half-weight to (9.39) therefore bounds the conditional endpoint score by AJ + t + epkt .
Restoring J adds H(J), with the recorded tag error; here b∗ = 0. Proposition 9.5 at r = 0 gives

                           Pt ≤ H(J) + EAJ + t + epkt ≤ K0 + t + 2epkt ,

because N ≤ Nall . First take ϵ → 0 at fixed depths, η, ξ and integration orders, then send η, ξ → 0.
This proves βq ≤ 1.


10     Finite packet composition and the uncertainty boundary
The packet decomposition gives two different comparisons at an earlier time: an upper bound for
the root score of a retained superposition, and a lower bound obtained by testing the original
input on the whole cap class. Keeping these comparisons separate is essential. We first give a
finite version of their composition, including the loss when the class is subsequently restricted.

10.1    An error budget and a finite composition inequality
Write ℓ = log(1/ϵ); all entropies and all logarithms denoted by log1/ϵ (·) in this section are divided
by ℓ. A fixed collection of bounds B will specify bounded ranges for HP , L, t, r, bounded spatial
and velocity domains, the phase and amplitude bounds, and a positive lower bound for each time


                                                   80
gap to which a growth estimate is applied. In particular, B is fixed before ϵ tends to zero. Its size
may change in a later outer sequence.
    For an array of conditional systems these bounds, all grid and observation-count bounds,
and every vanishing-error envelope are common deterministic bounds for the whole array. This
condition is preserved here: the translated phase bounds are uniform over the localizers, the
velocity supports are cut into a fixed bounded domain, and a single truncation order, coefficient
threshold, depth mesh, and tag-count bound are used for every retained J. Thus every selection
of one conditional system at each precision is an admissible sequence with the same bounds.
Individual extendibility to unspecified vanishing-error envelopes would not suffice.
    There is a useful precise way of using the definition of βq . For systems obeying B, let
                                                               
                                  ωB (ϵ) = sup Pd − K0 − βq d + ,                                 (10.1)

where the supremum also includes the bounded translated systems in Lemma 9.3, and d ranges
over the prescribed compact subset of (0, ∞). Enlarging the fixed bounds slightly to accommodate
rounded grids is understood. Then ωB (ϵ) → 0. Indeed, a failure would select a sequence of
admissible systems with a fixed positive excess; a subsequence of their depth parameters converges,
contradicting the definition of βq . This is a modulus for a fixed class, not a modulus uniform over
growing values of B. At duration zero the corresponding estimate is the definition of K0 , with
zero error.
    Here is one error budget that will be used below. Choose a depth mesh ξ > 0, a packet
enlargement exponent η > 0, and let Sϵ ≥ 2 bound the number of auxiliary choices at a localizer:
shape bins, mass bins, cap-class tags, winning tags, and any prescribed further subdivisions. The
geometric localizer itself is not one of these choices; its entropy will be kept. On the output tests
under consideration assume
                                            mO ≥ ϵA0 ∥f ∥22 .                                  (10.2)
Take the order of the packet truncation large enough that its amplitude error is at most CA ϵA ∥f ∥2 ,
with A > A0 /2, and put
                                       qϵ = CA ϵA−A0 /2 < 21 .
For a sufficiently large fixed CB , we may use

                                               1 + log(2 + ℓ)              1
                                                                                 
      R = CB (η + ξ + log1/ϵ (Sϵ )) + CB,η,ξ,A                + 3 log1/ϵ              + ωB (ϵ).   (10.3)
                                                      ℓ                  1 − qϵ
Each occurrence below of a fixed enlargement of B is included in this choice. One may instead use
the sum of the separate error bounds in Lemmas 9.3 and 9.4 and Proposition 9.5; only their upper
bound by R is needed.
    With S = Sϵ and q = qϵ , the winner estimate (9.30) gives the two contributions 3 log1/ϵ (Sϵ )
and 3 log1/ϵ (1/(1 − qϵ )) to the loss in the score’s mass term. Its argument uses only qϵ < 1/2,
so the present choice A > A0 /2 suffices. The other terms in (10.3) cover the tag entropies, cap
enlargement and binning, bounded grid ambiguities, and the logarithmically growing root cutoff.
    The floor also preserves saturation. After normalizing ∥f ∥2 = 1, the calculation at (9.29), with
the fixed weight bound from B, lets us choose A0 so that every low-mass law has score below
−1, whereas K0 ≥ 0. The two-part split costs at most 3 log 2/(2ℓ). For βq > 0, the low-mass
probability therefore tends to zero on a saturating sequence, and the complementary conditional
law still saturates.



                                                 81
Proposition 10.1 (Finite packet composition). Fix 0 ≤ r < t, a shape 0 ≤ b ≤ a ≤ min(L − t, ut ),
and 0 ≤ b∗ ≤ b. Choose 0 < η < (ur −b∗ )/4, and make the decomposition and cap-class construction
of Section 9, with the mass floor (10.2). Within the localizer J, including its auxiliary tags, let
Nall (J) be the size of the whole cap class, N (J) > 0 its retained size, m(J) its coefficient mass
bin, and g(J) its cap maximum bin. Set

                 AJ = log1/ϵ (N (J)) + 23 log1/ϵ (m(J)) + 12 g(J),                              (10.4)
                                       h                                           i
                 Kr∗ = H(J) + b∗ + E log1/ϵ (Nall (J)) + 32 log1/ϵ (m(J)) + 12 g(J) .           (10.5)

Let KJ denote the canonical local root score, including its norm option, for the retained local
superposition. Let PJ be its score at duration t − r, with the conditional output law and the winning
superposition masses. All expectations here use the current marginal of J after the indicated
preliminary split.
    With R as in (10.3), enlarged by a fixed constant if necessary, the following four quantities are
nonnegative:

                                    d0 = K0 + βq r − Kr∗ + 2R,
                                 d1 (J) = log1/ϵ (Nall (J)/N (J)) ,
                                 d2 (J) = AJ − KJ + R,
                                 d3 (J) = KJ + βq (t − r) − PJ + R.                             (10.6)

Up to the displayed error allowances, these are the deficits in growth before the cut, class retention,
the local root comparison, and growth after the cut. The next inequality shows that saturation at
the endpoint forces these four comparisons to be nearly sharp on average; the subsequent selection
estimate specifies the branches on which this remains true. Writing ∆t = K0 + βq t − Pt , one has
the finite bound
                                 d0 + E(d1 + d2 + d3 ) ≤ ∆t + 5R.                                (10.7)
There is also an earlier test on the retained uniform classes, with that same current marginal of J
and the assigned cap labels, whose score satisfies

                                       Prret ≥ Kr∗ − 32 Ed1 − R.                                (10.8)

At r = 0 this is an actual test for the original canonical K0 ; the norm option is not used in this
lower bound.
    More quantitatively, put Rt = ∆t + 5R. For an event B measurable in J with probability p > 0
and any θ > 0,                                            
                                P B ∩ {d1 + d2 + d3 > θ} ≤ Rt /θ.                            (10.9)
In particular, taking θ = 2Rt /p leaves at least p/2 of the current probability in B, and bounds each
of the three local deficits by 2Rt /p. If Rt = 0, all three vanish almost surely.

Proof. The localizer consists geometrically of Cr , velocity at depth b∗ , and position transported to
Cr at depth r + b∗ . It is determined by both the endpoint observation and its aperture, up to
the grid ambiguities already charged in R. The phase and input rescaling in (9.14)–(9.16) give
local Planck depth HP − r − 2b∗ and horizon L − r − b∗ . The returning aperture weight gains 2b∗ .
Entropy chain rules and the winning-class estimate consequently give

                                     Pt ≤ H(J) + b∗ + EPJ + R.                                 (10.10)

                                                  82
Here the potentially large entropy H(J) has not been discarded. Only the extra ambiguity and
choice tags have been charged to R.
   Proposition 9.5 gives KJ ≤ AJ + R. Its whole-class earlier test gives Prall ≥ Kr∗ − R. The
universal estimates

                       Prall ≤ K0 + βq r + R,                   PJ ≤ KJ + βq (t − r) + R

hold uniformly in J by (10.1). For r = 0 the first is instead the exact inequality P0all ≤ K0 . These
facts prove nonnegativity in (10.6). Moreover, direct cancellation yields

                    d0 + E(d1 + d2 + d3 ) = K0 + βq t − H(J) − b∗ − EPJ + 4R.

Using (10.10) proves (10.7). Markov’s inequality gives (10.9).
    For clarity, the retained-class assertion has a finite proof that does not assume that every
assigned fiber retains a fixed proportion. Fix J. Write ne and re for the full and retained sizes
of the fiber assigned to cap e, and PE , QE for the assigned-cap laws under the uniform full and
retained populations. Terms with re = 0 are omitted. The exact identity

                                      nE                      Nall           KL(QE ∥PE )
                                                                  
                     EQE log1/ϵ                = log1/ϵ                  −               ≤ d1 (J)   (10.11)
                                      rE                      N                  ℓ

and the assigned-fiber estimate log1/ϵ (ne ) + w(e) ≥ g − R show that

                             Hret (T | E) + Eret w(E) ≥ g − d1 (J) − R.

The ordinary earlier entropy is log1/ϵ (N ), and the coefficient mass exponent is log1/ϵ (m) up to
the bin error. Returning to the original coordinates and allowing the bounded multiplicities gives
(10.8); increasing the fixed constant in R absorbs its several geometric uses. The coefficient tests
at r = 0 are bounded by the canonical root tests, so the last assertion follows as well.

10.2    What saturation permits one to select
Definition 10.2 (Successive saturation arrays). After normalizing duration to one, a saturation
array consists of rows indexed by j, with small parameters ϵj,n ↓ 0 within each row. Row j obeys
one fixed collection of analytic, domain, and depth bounds Bj , and, for numbers ∆j ↓ 0,
                                                                               
                                  lim sup K0,j,n + βq − P1,j,n ≤ ∆j .
                                      n→∞

The corresponding lower limit is nonnegative by the universal bound for that row. Bounds Bj may
grow with j; the outer array is not asserted to be one admissible small-parameter sequence. All uses
of a universal modulus are made in a fixed row, before the outer limit. For a finite construction, its
mesh and truncation parameters are chosen within row j before the inner precision; their limiting
error budget can be prescribed as ηj ↓ 0. Such arrays exist by the definition of βq : choose a family
with limiting rate within 1/j of the supremum, pass to a subsequence realizing that rate, and
normalize its duration.

   In the remainder of the packet argument, saturation and its successive limits have this meaning.
The class of arrays includes the arrays obtained by the finite selections and fixed positive duration
normalizations below. Here is the relevant closure justification. At a fixed cut, the four nonnegative
composition deficits have total mean at most ∆j + on (1) plus the chosen finite error budget. On

                                                              83
any event of mass at least a fixed p0 > 0, their conditional mean is bounded by this quantity
divided by p0 . Markov selection therefore gives conditional local families whose outer deficits tend
to zero. They obey fixed enlarged bounds Bj′ within each row, independently of which localizer is
selected. The earlier test retains its full selected localizer marginal and has the same conclusion by
the retained-score inequality.
    For each row, make only finitely many cuts with fixed positive gaps. Depth and score units
have been divided by the initial duration. Then choose the depth meshes, packet enlargement
exponents, and the permitted exponent of the additional tag count as small as required. Choose
the truncation order after the mass floor and these parameters; finally choose ϵ small enough for
(10.3) and all of the finitely many universal moduli to have the required size. Equivalently one
may take the inner ϵ → 0 limits first, then send the mesh and enlargement parameters to zero,
and only afterwards take the outer extremizing sequence. No estimate here requires uniformity as
HP , the aspect, or the phase bounds grow in that outer sequence.
    If ∆t → 0 in this order, all four nonnegative deficits vanish in the sense of (10.7). In particular
KJ = AJ + o(1) and PJ = KJ + βq (t − r) + o(1) on selected typical conditional systems. The
retained earlier law has deficit tending to zero by (10.8). To be explicit, its deficit is at most

                                     d0 + 32 Ed1 + R ≤ 23 Rt + R.

For this earlier test one keeps the whole marginal on J. Selecting a single value of J is appropriate
when testing a local growth bound, and does not replace that marginal in the earlier test.
    Further splitting has an equally finite justification. If a label B takes at most S values, the score
of a law and the average scores of its conditional laws differ by at most 32 H(B) ≤ 3 log1/ϵ (S) /2; this
follows directly from the two entropy terms in the packet score. The input and its canonical root
score are the same in these comparisons. Add the uniform growth error to make the conditional
deficits nonnegative, and use Markov’s inequality. Thus a split made before composition preserves
saturation on typical branches, also within any event whose probability is bounded below. For an
event of probability p tending to zero, the required condition is explicitly that the available deficit
and error be o(p); subpower probability by itself is not this condition. More generally, if a sum of
nonnegative shifted deficits has mean D, its mean on a current event of probability p is at most
D/p; outside a set of relative probability at most q there, the sum is at most D/(pq) pointwise.
Polynomially many geometric localizers are handled by retaining their entropy, rather than by
declaring the cost of fixing one negligible. The conditional local estimate, on the other hand, is
pointwise and uniform in the inner limit, so a violating local system can always be selected without
a probability loss argument.
    One can also specify the probability of a chosen tag. If a union of tag branches has probability
p, discard tags of probability below p/(4S), losing at most p/4. If their total nonnegative shifted
deficit is at most R, discard branches with conditional deficit greater than 4R/p, losing at most
another p/4. Some remaining tag has probability at least p/(4S) and conditional deficit at most
4R/p. Its logarithmic retention cost is at most log1/ϵ (4S/p). For fixed p > 0 and log1/ϵ (S) → 0,
this is a subpower retention with a quantified deficit. Apply composition anew on that tag, keeping
its marginal on J for the earlier test. Repeating this argument a fixed number of times gives, for
any prescribed accuracy ϑ > 0, a branch on which the endpoint deficit, the mean retained-count
loss, the local root comparison deficit, the earlier retained-law deficit, and the upper error in each
specified admissible score-replacement comparison are all at most ϑ. For the last assertion, the
universal bound for a competing endpoint test gives Ptnew − Pt ≤ ∆t + ωB . Here p is the probability
of the relevant event in the current normalized law. The displayed simultaneous selection divides
the mean deficit by p, not by the probability of the individual tag; it requires no estimate of the

                                                   84
form R = o(1/S). For a subsequent event, apply the same rule to its probability in the then
current law and choose the incoming errors accordingly. Thus a finite subsequent argument may
use ϑ to denote the maximum of these selected errors; it need not identify it with the original
unsplit deficit.
    These rules also apply to any fixed finite list of observations and cuts, using the sum of their
errors and a union bound. They concern score differences. They do not require the separate scores
to have finite outer limits, or a uniform differentiability modulus for any limiting entropy profile.

10.3    Faithful classical tests away from the boundary
Lemma 10.3 (Exclusion of a fixed uncertainty slack). Assume Theorem 4.2 and βq > γ/2. A
saturating packet law at any fixed positive duration has

                                           a = ut + o(1)                                      (10.12)

on its typical fixed-shape branches. More precisely, if r = t − 2h > 0 and a + 2h ≤ ur − 2η, the
construction above with b∗ = b gives

                               Pt ≤ K0 + βq r + γh + CR + 21 ωBcl (ϵ).                        (10.13)

Here ωBcl → 0 is the fixed-class error in the classical growth theorem, and the constant is fixed for
this cut.

Proof. Work within J and use the earlier retained grid centers to define exact lines with velocities
VT and positions XT at Cr . Coarsen the output to the isotropic observation Z of velocity depths
a, a, position depths a + t, a + t, and time label Ct . Let Tz consist of retained atoms compatible,
under (9.19), with at least one output in the z-fiber.
    The earlier position tolerance is Cϵ−η ϵr+ur . Since r = t − 2h, the hypotheses give

                               ϵr+ur −η ≤ ϵa+t+η ,     ρt = ϵut ≤ ϵa .

Thus a fixed contributor and time locate Z up to the ambiguities charged in R. They also give
the compatibility needed for positive transport after sampling. For every T ∈ Tz and every D
occurring over Z = z, choose an output OD paired with D and an output OT compatible with T
in that fiber. They are in the same isotropic z-cell. The displayed tolerances therefore put the
exact line and OD within Cϵa in velocity and Cϵa+t in position at Ct . These isotropic widths fit
both axes of the rectangles of D. Use a fixed enlargement of those rectangles as the aperture
represented by the same label D, at the same depths. The enlarged aperture is faithful for every
such pair, as permitted by Definition 4.1.
    First sum the winning coherent masses over each Z. Bessel and the separately chosen transport-
tail order give
                                       mZ ≤ ϵ−CR m #TZ ,
with the tails absorbed by the coefficient and output floors as in the proof of Proposition 9.6. Only
after this bound, keep the given law of (Z, D) and choose T uniformly in TZ , independently of D
given Z. The reverse determination above gives H(Z | T, Ct ) ≤ CR under this law. Equations
(9.36) and (9.38), with the displayed mass bound, now give

             PJ ≤ H(T ) + 23 log1/ϵ (m) + 12 [H(T | D) + wloc (D) + 2H(Ct | T )] + CR.


                                                 85
    The classical horizon is a − b + 2h. Its root cap menu fits the local packet menu: a + 2h ≤ ur
and a + t ≤ L are precisely the required upper bounds after subtracting b and r. For every root
test F , cap counting gives

                    H(T | F ) + Ew(F ) ≤ g + CR,        τ (T ) ≤ g − H(T ) + CR.

This support bound holds for the possibly biased contributor marginal on T . Theorem 4.2, applied
to the exact lines and the faithful enlarged aperture represented by D, gives

                    H(T | D) + wloc (D) + 2H(Ct | T ) ≤ g + 2γh + CR + ωBcl (ϵ).

Since H(T ) ≤ log1/ϵ (N ), we get PJ ≤ AJ + γh + CR + ωBcl /2. The whole-class earlier comparison
and the finite split give (10.13).
   If ut − a had a fixed positive lower bound on a saturating branch, choose a fixed h < t/2
smaller than half that bound, and then choose η smaller still. Let the finite errors tend to zero in
the specified order. Equation (10.13) contradicts the positive gap (2βq − γ)h.

10.4     The two ways to continue backwards
For a packet system write K0≥u0 −ν for the supremum of its actual root aperture scores with smaller
width b ≥ u0 − ν, excluding the total norm option. Call a duration-one saturation array in the
sense of Definition 10.2 forced at the root if there are fixed ν, κ > 0 and an outer subsequence of
rows on which
                                         K0≥u0 −ν ≤ K0 − κ                                  (10.14)
in each row’s inner limit. The quantifier ranges over all such saturation arrays, including arrays of
normalized conditional systems. Thus the negation excludes a forced array obtained by selecting
and rescaling local systems as well as an originally chosen one.
    The constants ν, κ > 0 must be fixed across an outer subsequence of rows, with the asserted
gap holding in each row’s inner limit (and with a smaller fixed gap if necessary). A window or
gap tending to zero with the row index does not constitute a forced array. In the forced case the
backward step h is chosen once from these fixed constants and the fixed number of steps. In the
absence of a forced array, a fixed cap excess at a fixed cut would produce one by the local-root
comparison; hence the isotropic alternative applies with a step chosen once from its fixed time
interval and the number of steps. In either case h is fixed before j → ∞, so the outer deficits are
o(h).

Proposition 10.4 (Boundary alternatives and finite chains). Assume βq > γ/2. For every fixed
integer q ≥ 1 and every fixed M > 0, a saturation array approaching βq admits q successive
backward cuts at times

                            tj = t0 − 2jh > 0   (0 ≤ j ≤ q),     utj > h,

with h > 0 fixed in the inner limits, satisfying one of the following alternatives.
  1. A forced array exists. One can use the same such array, choose a fixed ν > 0 from (10.14),
     and take all the times early enough that the saturated tests satisfy

                 aj = utj + o(1),      aj − bj ≥ ν/2 + o(1),     aj − bj ≥ M h/γ + o(1).

       Every cut uses b∗ = bj at its later endpoint.

                                                 86
   2. No forced saturation array exists. At all the indicated times one can use exact isotropic
      boundary tests aj = bj = utj , and every cut uses b∗ = 0.
These assertions remain valid when a prescribed finite number of subdivisions with vanishing tag
cost are made at each step, before the earlier test is constructed. In particular the earlier law
is uniform on the retained classes produced by those subdivisions. For any prescribed positive
accuracy, all the assertions about saturation hold at sufficiently small finite precision with that
accuracy; the required precision can depend on the outer bounds, q, h, ν, M , the subdivision costs,
and the selected branch.
Proof. Suppose first that (10.14) holds. A saturated fixed-shape branch at a positive time cannot
have b ≥ u0 − ν with a fixed inward margin: composition down to r = 0 with b∗ = b and (10.8)
gives actual root aperture scores arbitrarily close to K0 , all with smaller widths at least b. This
contradicts (10.14). Allowing the shape mesh to vanish gives b ≤ u0 − ν + o(1). By Lemma 10.3,
for t ≤ ν this yields

                    a − b ≥ ut − u0 + ν + o(1) = ν − t/2 + o(1) ≥ ν/2 + o(1).

Start with a backward subdivision of the duration-one extremizer to a time 0 < t0 < min(ν, 1/4).
Finite composition supplies a saturated earlier test there. Choose
                                              t0       γν
                                                                
                               0 < h < min         ,         .
                                           2(q + 1) 4(M + 1)
Since the original normalized Planck depth is at least one, these early times also have utj > h.
Repeated composition gives the claimed chain. Its first two comparisons are reapplied after every
prescribed split, so the argument uses the actual retained classes.
   For the second alternative, consider a cut 0 < r < t from a saturated law and take b∗ = 0.
Equation (10.12) implies

                          L ≥ ut + t − o(1) = ur + r + (t − r)/2 − o(1).

Hence the local root packet menu contains the exact isotropic test of depth v = ur . We show that,
on selected saturating branches,
                                        g = 2v + o(1).                                     (10.15)
A single atom fits an isotropic cap at the natural root packet scale, so the cap-count bound gives
g ≥ 2v − CR. For the reverse inequality fix ζ > 0 and consider an excess g ≥ 2v + ζ. A root cap
with smaller width at least v − α contains at most Cϵ−4α−CR earlier sites. The pointwise canonical
mass bound is ϵ−CR m: the cross-Gram kernel at the common root packet scale has a uniformly
                                                                             √
bounded absolute row sum, and every coefficient has size at most ϵ−ξ/2 m. This estimate is
uniform over the canonical test amplitude. The total canonical mass is at most ϵ−CR N m. The
two log-sum inequalities for the root score consequently give

                        KJ≥v−α ≤ log1/ϵ (N ) + 32 log1/ϵ (m) + v + 2α + CR.                 (10.16)

This estimates actual aperture scores, not the norm option.
    By (10.7), typical local systems satisfy KJ = AJ + o(1) and PJ − KJ = βq (t − r) + o(1). If a
fixed positive excess ζ persisted on such selected systems, choose α < ζ/16 and then make their
composition errors small. Equations (10.4) and (10.16) would give

                                       KJ − KJ≥v−α ≥ ζ/4.

                                                87
Normalizing these systems by their fixed positive duration t − r produces a forced duration-one
saturation array, with root-width window α/(t − r) and gap ζ/(4(t − r)). This contradicts the
hypothesis of the second alternative. This duration normalization divides the depth exponents by
t − r. The frequency λ, and hence the root cutoff 1 + log λ, is unchanged.
    Here is the selection detail in this argument. One first splits on a g-bin common across the
geometric localizers, along with the other required tag bins, and reapplies composition using its
conditional deficit bounds and its full marginal on J. Equation (10.9) permits selection within any
excess event of probability bounded below; otherwise that excess event has probability tending to
zero. Since g ≥ 2v − CR, one may therefore retain a saturating branch on which |g − 2v| ≤ ζ + CR,
and subsequently send ζ to zero. Thus no passage through expectations of unbounded outer values
of g is being used.
    On this branch the exact isotropic test at r, using the retained uniform classes, has score at
least
                                        h                             i
                       Priso ≥ H(J) + E log1/ϵ (N ) + 32 log1/ϵ (m) + v − CR
                            = Kr∗ − Ed1 − 21 E(g − 2v) − CR.

Its conditional entropy term is merely nonnegative; no assigned-fiber entropy gain is needed. Finite
composition and (10.15) show that this score saturates at r. The same construction works for the
first cut even if its later endpoint test was not isotropic. Make that first cut to an early t0 < 1/4,
choose h < t0 /(2(q + 1)), and repeat.
     Finally fix q and the requested finite accuracy. At each of the finitely many selections take the
current deficit and error small enough relative to its retained probability and the next requested
accuracy, using (10.9); for an earlier test use (10.8) or the displayed isotropic bound. Choose the
inner precision only after these finitely many requirements have been imposed. This proves the
stated finite approximation and its stability under the additional subdivisions. In the elongated
alternative the orientation precision of an aperture is ϵaj −bj ; thus the asserted aspect lower bound
supplies any fixed separation between this precision and ϵh/γ needed in the subsequent orientation
argument.


11     Finite packet repayment and closure
We complete the proof of Theorem 9.1 by a finite backward iteration. A cut of length 2h records
the loss from coherent superposition as fine velocity information. At a growth rate βq > γ/2, the
repayment estimate forces this quantity to increase at each cut by (4βq − 2γ)h, up to the finite
errors specified below, while its capacity is at most 2h up to those errors. A sufficiently long
finite chain is therefore impossible. The information carried between cuts is encoded by a uniform
conditional probability bound, so it can be retained through the subsequent branch selections.
    Write ℓ = log(1/ϵ) and divide all entropies by ℓ. We use the finite budget of Section 10: a
conditional list of size ϵ−α costs α, and a fixed factor C costs (log C)/ℓ. Depth bins have width
ξ, and packet tails are cut at distance ϵ−θ in earlier packet units, with θ < h/10 at fixed depths
and h > 0. Constants C∗ count finitely many geometric and entropy losses, independently of the
aspect exponents and cut locations. Fixed phase and amplitude constants enter through their
logarithms divided by ℓ. We may increase C∗ a finite number of times.
    Figure 3 explains the mismatch between the earlier packet’s transported uncertainty and the
output position precision. The fine velocity information below accounts for this mismatch.



                                                 88
                                                position at Ct

                                                    ϵut +h




                          ϵut +t−h                                           ϵut +t

                                                                             velocity



                                                      ϵu t
                                     earlier packet, transported from r = t − 2h
                                     output packet at t


Figure 3: One coordinate pair of packet uncertainty cells at a common output anchor, with
0 < h ≤ t/2, t ≤ HP and ut = (HP − t)/2. The earlier packet has ur = ut + h: its velocity
cell is finer by depth h, while its transported position uncertainty is coarser by depth h. Both
displayed rectangles have the same area, ϵut +h ϵut +t−h = ϵut ϵut +t = ϵHP . The rectangles are drawn
concentrically to compare their scales and represent comparable widths after transport; fixed
factors, shear and subpower tail enlargements are suppressed. An earlier packet can therefore
contribute across several fine output position cells. Its transported center alone does not determine
the fine output position label.

11.1    The incoming fine-velocity information
Here is the precise meaning of the velocity information carried between steps. At time t, let
       ut = (HP − t)/2,        Pf = (Ct , isotropic velocity and position cells of width ϵut −h ).
The position width in this formula is ϵt+ut −h . Fixed dilations and translations of the grids are
allowed. Additional tags are allowed only if their conditional number, given the geometric part of
Pf , is at most ϵ−α ; their cost is included in α.
    In the elongated case nf (Pf ) is a unit normal, modulo sign, and the normal of the aperture D
is within Cϵh of nf (Pf ). The label Wf locates the velocity component along nf (Pf ) in intervals
of length ϵut . In the isotropic case Wf locates both velocity components at that precision. An
incoming credit kf ≥ 0, with error αf , means that on the normalized law being used,
              sup P(Wf = w | Pf = p) ≤ ϵ kf −αf              for every p of positive probability.    (11.1)
               w

In particular H(Wf | Pf ) ≥ kf − αf . On the first step we set kf = 0 and impose no incoming
direction or fine-velocity condition.
    We will use the following exact retention rule. It specifies how (11.1) is maintained when
selecting branches.
Lemma 11.1 (Retention of a conditional probability bound). Suppose a finite law satisfies
P(W = w | P = p) ≤ ϵk−α . Let A have probability pA > 0, and let ζ > 0. Under the law
conditioned on A, the set of fibers on which
                                              P(A | P ) < pA ϵζ

                                                       89
has probability at most ϵζ . On every remaining fiber,

                                P(W = w | P, A) ≤ ϵ k−α−ζ−log1/ϵ(1/pA ) .                       (11.2)

Removing the exceptional fibers does not change this conditional bound. The same assertion holds
when P includes other previously fixed labels.
Proof. The exceptional probability, in the conditioned law, is

                         p−1
                                      X
                          A                              P(P = p)P(A | P = p) ≤ ϵζ .
                               {p: P(A|P =p)<pA   ϵζ }

On a remaining fiber divide P(W = w, A | P = p) ≤ P(W = w | P = p) by P(A | P = p).
Conditioning subsequently on a set of P values does not alter the conditional law given P .

   Every removal below is also a score split under Section 10; its probability alone does not control
the remaining score.

11.2    Constructing the finite cut
A prepared cut starts with a boundary step from r = t − 2h > 0 to t, with ut > h, as supplied
by Proposition 10.4. The current endpoint law will be denoted by π in . It is a probability law on
a finite sample space Ω. A sample ω records O(ω), D(ω), all incoming labels Pf , Wf and their
extra tags when present, and any auxiliary randomness already used to choose those labels. In
particular, an incoming label need not be a deterministic function of (O, D). For any endpoint
law ν on Ω, write Pt (ν) for the score of the original input with its original masses. If kf > 0, the
bound (11.1) holds under π in , with its current error. On the first step kf = 0.
    The finite geometry is the following. The endpoint shape has a = ut to depth error at most η.
In the isotropic alternative the apertures are isotropic and b∗ = 0. In the elongated alternative
b∗ = b, the endpoint aspect e = a − b is at least e0 > 0. In that case require

                                            C∗ (h + η) < γe0 .                                  (11.3)

The grids and aperture menu admit all coarsenings below. We take C∗ ≥ 2. Thus in the elongated
case b ≤ ut + η − e0 ≤ ut − h, after increasing C∗ if necessary; in the isotropic case b∗ = 0 < ut − h.
We will use
                                             b∗ ≤ ut − h                                        (11.4)
when comparing the incoming coarse label with the localizer.
    Use the fixed analytic, domain, depth, grid, and count bounds of Section 10. For supremum
masses, fix at each site a test within a fixed factor of the supremum and call its mass mO (f ). This
changes any endpoint score by at most C/ℓ, included in R, and the Bessel bounds are uniform
in these choices. Apply the floor mO (f ) ≥ ϵA0 ∥f ∥22 of (9.29) and the fixed-shape score splits,
recording their probabilities for credit retention.
    Fix the depths, then θ < h/10 and ξ. By (11.4), θ < (ur − b∗ )/4, as required by Lemma 9.3.
Choose its amplitude error CA ϵA ∥f ∥2 with A > A0 /2 + 1. If Q0 bounds all relevant count
exponents, choose the coefficient cutoff

                                          B > 2A + 3Q0 + 10.                                    (11.5)

uniformly over geometric localizers. The later transport order may be increased after this coefficient
family is fixed.

                                                           90
    For one geometric localizer J0 , let ι range over its coefficient bins and greedy cap classes. The
associated whole class is a finite set Tιall of earlier atoms, with coefficient mass bin m(ι), cap
maximum bin g(ι), and assigned cap Eι (T ). Lemma 9.4 gives the cap upper bound on this set
and the lower bound on each of its whole assigned fibers. At this point ι indexes available classes;
no class has yet been assigned to an endpoint sample as its final winner. The buffer used next is
chosen for all possible contributors in this finite family, before any of its classes is subdivided.
    During preparation, a replacement loss is the increase in score when the aperture is changed at
fixed masses, evaluated on the specified current marginal. Section 10 supplies these losses from
the current deficits and charges each split. This also applies to the two-copy marginals in the next
lemma, since adjoining the unsplit conditional copies preserves the old marginal.
    For every possible contributor, the velocity difference from its output center is O(ϵut ). After
referring the positions to Cr , their difference is
                 O(ϵr+ut ) + O(ϵ−θ ϵr+ur ) = O(ϵr+ut ),     ur = ut + h,   θ < h/10.
Use isotropic cells with side Lgrid times ϵut in velocity and ϵr+ut in position, where Lgrid is a
sufficiently large fixed constant. A uniformly random translation places any output center farther
than these possible differences from every face with probability at least 3/4. Averaging over
translations therefore gives one translation for which a part of at least that probability has the
coarse label
                                P = (Cr , Vut , (X + (Cr − Ct )V )r+ut )
in common with every possible contributing earlier atom. Retain this part as a score split and
denote its normalized endpoint law by π buf . Its probability and deficit are charged by the finite
selection rule above. This completes the geometric preparation in the isotropic case.
Lemma 11.2 (Prediction of a normal from isotropic replacements). Assume the elongated geometry.
On π buf , suppose the replacement losses for the isotropic apertures of widths a and b are included
in C∗ η. Take two copies (O, D), (O′ , D′ ), independent conditional on P , and set
                                    Asep = {∠(n(D), n(D′ )) ≥ ϵ4h }.
If this event has probability at least 1/4, require the same replacement bounds on both marginals of
the law conditioned on Asep . Then there is a deterministic normal n(P ), defined on every coarse
cell used by the atom partition, such that
                                         ∠(n(D), n(P )) ≤ Cϵ4h
on a part of π buf -probability at least 3/4.
Proof. Let e = a − b, and let BP be the isotropic coarsening of P to width b. Grid list errors will
be included in C∗ η. Only 2h extra depth for time and 2h for each position coordinate are missing
from P in order to specify O, so
                                     H(O | P ) ≤ 6h + C∗ η.                                  (11.6)
Replacing D by the isotropic aperture of width a gives
                                   H(P | D) ≥ (1 + γ)e − 6h − C∗ η.                            (11.7)
Indeed the weight increases by (1 + γ)e, and the new conditional observation entropy is nonnegative.
Replacing D by the isotropic width-b aperture at t, which has at most 6h + C∗ η information
beyond BP , gives
                               I(P ; D | BP ) ≤ (1 − γ)e + 6h + C∗ η.                        (11.8)

                                                  91
Both inequalities are comparisons on the marginal law on which they are invoked.
    Suppose Asep has probability at least 1/4, and condition the two-copy law on this event. For
the resulting law, conditional dependence of the two copies given P is at most log(1/P(Asep ))/ℓ ≤
(log 4)/ℓ. This follows by comparing its density with the original conditional product and using
the chain rule for relative entropy. The marginal replacement estimates (11.7)–(11.8) continue to
hold with their charged conditional deficits.
    Each aperture, referred to Cr , constrains both columns to a normal strip of width a fixed
multiple of ϵa−C∗ η . Two such strips whose normals have angle at least ϵ4h have intersection of
diameter at most Cϵa−4h−C∗ η . Covering both columns by P cells consequently gives

                                     H(P | D, D′ ) ≤ 16h + C∗ η.                                 (11.9)

Here a strip intersection is used separately for velocity and root position, each in two dimensions;
hence 4(4h) = 16h. The coarse label BP has bounded ambiguity given either aperture. The
identity
                I(P ; D′ | D, BP ) − I(P ; D′ | BP ) = I(D; D′ | P, BP ) − I(D; D′ | BP )
and conditional product comparison therefore imply that its left side is at most C∗ η. The first term
is at least (1 + γ)e − 22h − C∗ η, by (11.7) and (11.9); the second is at most (1 − γ)e + 6h + C∗ η,
by (11.8) for the other marginal. Thus 2γe ≤ 28h + C∗ η, contrary to the eccentricity hypothesis
after fixing C∗ . It follows that the complementary angle-proximity event has probability at least
3/4. For each P = p, choose one normal maximizing the conditional mass of a ball of radius Cϵ4h .
Averaging the two-copy probability shows that the single-copy part predicted by these normals
has mass at least 3/4.
    On coarse cells of zero probability choose an arbitrary normal. This extends the table n(P ) to
every cell that will index an atom subdivision, without changing the asserted event.

    For an earlier atom T , let P (T ) be its buffered coarse cell. In the elongated case first mark the

This divides each whole class into aligned and misaligned atoms; both parts remain available when
the winner is chosen. In the isotropic case no such mark is needed.

each P , bin

Lemma 11.3 (Alignment of the selected earlier aperture). For the elongated prepared cut, let

                      p [2γe0 − C∗ (h + η)] ≤ Eν [1B (d1 + 3d2 + 3d3 )] ≤ 3Rt .            (11.16)

Proof. Because B is J-measurable, ϱ( · | J) = ν( · | J) on B, so the local deficits are inherited.

The whole-fiber count has thus cost d1 (J) for the retained, possibly biased law.

                                  H(T | D, E) ≤ g − 2e + C∗ (h + η).

                       I(T ; D | E) ≥ (1 + γ)(e − j) − d1 (J) − δJ − C∗ (h + η).

Average under ϱJ , absorbing the common-bin errors:

                    IϱP (T ; D | E, J) ≥ (1 + γ)(e − j) − Eϱ (d1 + δJ ) − C∗ (h + η).             (11.19)

As E is a function of T given J, and determines Mj up to the charged lists,

                             IϱP (T ; D | E, J) ≤ IϱP (T ; D | Mj , J) + C∗ η.

    For the reverse estimate, coarsen D to widths 0, j, using the center of Mj . Its normal projection
is confined by the coarsened strip, and the tangent width is zero-depth, so the resulting labels form
a charged list determined by D. Enlarge each rectangle by a fixed factor to contain the entire Mj
cell; its projections are bounded by its diagonal. The enlargement depends only on the rectangle
label. Resample it conditional on (Mj , J), independently of T , and call it U . It is faithful for every
atom in that cell, has weight (1 − γ)j, and the cap bound gives on each J fiber

                                I(T ; U ) ≥ H(T ) − g + (1 − γ)j − C∗ η.

Adjoin these kernels under ϱJ , retaining the notation ϱP . The joint law of the enlarged coarsening
and Mj is preserved on each fiber. Since Mj is a function of T given J,

                   IϱP (Mj ; D | J) ≥ IϱP (Mj ; U | J) − C∗ η = IϱP (T ; U | J) − C∗ η.

Average the cap bound and the second inequality in (11.17). Combining them with this display
gives
                   IϱP (T ; D | Mj , J) ≤ (1 − γ)(e − j) + 2Eϱ δJ + C∗ (h + η).      (11.20)
The lower and upper bounds imply

                            2γ(e − j) ≤ Eϱ [d1 + 3(d2 + d3 )] + C∗ (h + η).

The common shape bins give e − j ≥ e0 − h − O(η). Multiply by p, use pEϱ [·] = Eν [1B ·], and use
nonnegativity of the di and (10.7). This proves (11.16).


                                                    95
    For the elongated application, first select a branch with qualifying common earlier shape bins
by the weighted rule, perform credit retention when needed, and recompose. Let ν be this current
law and let B be all its misaligned winners. This event is J-measurable. If ν(B) = 0, the law is
already aligned; otherwise (11.16) applies to its entire current mass. At sufficiently small current
deficit, the aligned complement has positive probability, tending to one with the positive gap fixed.
Restrict to that complement, then apply the current-law retention and recomposition rule. In
either alternative, apply the same rule to any remaining common numerical-bin selections and
recompose after the last restriction. Denote the resulting endpoint law on Ω by π, and now set
Pt = Pt (π), the score of the original input with its original masses. Every final elongated winning
class is aligned. The buffered property, and prediction when present, persist pointwise. The
incoming bound is

               sup π(Wf = w | Pf = p) ≤ ϵkf −αf                   for every p with π(Pf = p) > 0,        (11.21)

where αf includes the initial error and the sum of (11.15). When kf = 0, this estimate will be
replaced by nonnegativity of conditional mutual information.
    Every expectation in the rest of this cut uses the selected marginal πJ , unless a different law is
explicitly displayed. In particular, set

               d1 (J) = log1/ϵ (Nall (J)/N (J)) ,

This statement holds for either earlier aperture assignment under µ, which is uniform within each

11.4    From winning masses to candidate masses
Before stating the repayment estimate, we finish the finite comparison of masses that its proof will
use. Work in the translated coordinates of a selected localizer J, put u = ut − b∗ , and write

                   ρ = ϵu ,    s = ϵu+h ,        σ = ϵu+2h = s2 /ρ,         λloc = s−2 .

within C0 Rs of that cell, where R = ϵ−θ and C0 is a fixed constant large enough for the transport
and cell-boundary enlargements. Absorb C0 into the fixed constants in the coherence estimates.
Write NJ,Q = #TJ,Q and                           X

carried by ω. On the full sample space write Q(ω) = Q(J(ω), O(ω)) and likewise for Z(ω).

11.5     The repayment estimate
The prepared cut supplies µ and the capacity bound (11.10). The remaining estimate shows that
a near-maximal endpoint score increases the fine-velocity credit.

Proposition 11.4 (Finite packet repayment). Fix 0 < γ < 1 and a comparison rate bq ∈ [0, 1].
For the prepared cut constructed above, let η bound the composition budget R, θ + ξ + (log C)/ℓ,
all logarithmic class, winner, tail, and label costs, the current mean Eπ d1 , and the finite classical
growth error on the fixed class of lines, after a fixed increase of C∗ . The selected endpoint and
earlier replacement and composition deficits required in the construction are at most η on their
specified current laws, including the two conditional prediction marginals. The common shape and
count bins have width at most η; in the isotropic case they also give the error in (11.26). When
kf > 0, assume (11.21) under π with its accumulated error αf ≤ η. Finally suppose

                                        Pt ≥ Br + 2bq h − ∆.                                   (11.32)

Then the prepared statistic P, n(P ), W, k under µ satisfies
                                                                          (
                                                                           1   elongated,
       0 ≤ k ≤ dh + C∗ η,     k ≥ kf + (4bq − 2γ)h − 2∆ − C∗ η,      d=                        (11.33)
                                                                           2   isotropic.

Its pointwise probability bound is (11.27). In the elongated case the continued aperture normal is
within Cϵh of n(P ). The returned score satisfies (11.25) in that case and (11.26) in the isotropic
case. All these bounds are finite at fixed ϵ.

11.6     A coherent estimate on the full set of output sites
We next prove the mass estimate before introducing any positive law on candidate packets. The
output amplitudes may be chosen separately at every site.

Lemma 11.5 (Packet coherence on a strip or a cell). Use local coordinates with root time r,
endpoint duration 2h, and put

                   ρ = ϵu ,     s = ϵu+h ,     σ = ϵu+2h = s2 /ρ,      λloc = s−2 .

Fixed multiplicative errors in these relations change only constants. Let P be a buffered isotropic
cell of width ρ. Let Q specify P, Ct and a position cell of side s at Ct . Candidate atoms have this


                                                   98
P , velocity spacing s, earlier position spacing s, and transported position within Rs of that cell,
where R = ϵ−θ . Let NQ be their number, and suppose

                                             |cT |2 ≤ ϵ−ξ m.

Partition their velocities into K ≤ ϵ−k−ξ bins of width s, in a fixed normal direction for d = 1
or in both directions for d = 2. Let Z add to Q the output position at precision σ in those d
directions. Let mZ be the sum of squared packet tests over all output sites mapping to Z, on the
input truncated to these candidates. Then

                             mZ ≤ Cϵ−ξ NQ m,
                       X

                       Z|Q

The constant is uniform over all admissible choices of the site-dependent amplitudes. Without
candidate truncation, the square root of the mass estimate has the additive tail error provided by
Lemma 9.3.

Proof. The first inequality is the synthesis Bessel estimate followed by the analysis Bessel estimate
of Lemma 9.2. It holds for any subset of output sites and any subset of atoms.
    Suppose first that d = 2. If AOT is the normalized atom/test matrix, then

                                |AOT | ≤ Cρ−1 ∥gT ∥1 ≤ Cs/ρ = Cϵh .

A full velocity bin contains a bounded number of velocity lattice centers. At each such center the

output sites. Cauchy–Schwarz first within one bin and then across its K bins gives
                                            X
                         mZ ≤ CKR2 ϵ2h           |cT |2 ≤ Cϵ2h−k−2θ−2ξ NQ m.
                                             T

    For d = 1, write n for the bin normal and τ for its orthogonal unit tangent. Fix one velocity

   Here are details that also justify using all output sites with independently chosen amplitudes.
Write v = VP + ρz on a common bounded patch and surround each output center XO by a square
BO of side comparable to σ. For x ∈ BO , remove the scalar value at v = VP from

                      ψO ((v − VO )/ρ) exp{iλloc [Φ(Ct , XO , v) − Φ(Ct , x, v)]}.

All derivatives in z are uniformly bounded, because λloc ρσ = 1. A common bump and a Fourier

                                    |bν (O, x)| ≤ CM (1 + |ν|)−M

uniformly in both O and x. Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the squared transform at XO by a
fixed rapidly summable combination of squared transforms at x, each with a common bump and a
linear modulation eiν·(v−VP )/ρ . These factors are independent of x. No derivatives with respect to
the site index have been used.

   Integrate this bound on BO , divide by its area, and sum. The squares have bounded overlap,
and their union lies in a strip of normal width Cσ and tangent length Cs. The normalization
outside the resulting continuous position integral is

                                               ρ−2 σ −2 = s−4 .                                              (11.36)

It is enough to prove the estimate for each of the common bump/modulation factors, uniformly in
its Fourier index; the factor has bounded modulus and is independent of x.

definiteness of ∂p ζ give

                        ∂τ {Φ(Ct , x, v) − Φ(Ct , x, v ′ )} = τ T A(x, v, v ′ )(v − v ′ ),

where A is an average of those definite matrices. If the tangent separation is a sufficiently large

by Cj |v − v ′ |. Repeated integration by parts in the rescaled tangent variable thus bounds its kernel

The possible overlap of velocity supports changes only the constant in this estimate. Sum the

11.7    The candidate law and its entropy bound
We now prove Proposition 11.4. For a value J = j of positive πJ -probability, let πj denote the
selected endpoint law conditional on J = j. Define the finite entropy expression

                  Ej = Hπj (Z) + 12 Hπj (Z | D) + 23 Eπj log1/ϵ (mZ ) + 12 Eπj wloc (D).

All entropies here concern the displayed geometric observations, rather than the entire sample ω.
The latter carries extra labels only so that later augmentation preserves them. Finite composition
first compares the original score with the conditional winning scores. Equation (11.29) then replaces
their masses by the candidate-truncated masses. Finally, conditional log-sum on each O 7→ Z fiber
bounds the two entropy–mass terms by those in Ej . The unconditional fiber calculation uses all
sites mapping to Z; conditional on D, its support is a subset of those sites. Thus

                        Pt ≤ Hπ (J) + b∗ + EπJ EJ + R + 3 log1/ϵ (1/(1 − qtr )) .                            (11.37)

This is a comparison of finite expressions under the same endpoint law. No growth bound has
been applied to the Q-dependent inputs.

                                                        100
   Lemma 11.5 now gives its two exact bounds for the masses mZ in (11.31). Only after these
analytic inequalities do we introduce a positive candidate law. Adjoin an earlier atom to the full

                                                   1{T ∈TJ(ω),Q(ω) }
                                  π

the atom is uniform on the candidate set and independent of every other endpoint label. Its
marginal on atoms is generally biased; it is different from µ, which is uniform within each retained
class.
     For the next calculation fix J = j, and write Hj , Ej for entropy and expectation under

11.8    A faithful aperture and the incoming information
The candidate atoms carry exact straight lines through their earlier positions with their assigned
velocities. In a localizer J, let FT record Ct and both columns of this line at isotropic width
u − h at the endpoint. Its local position precision is ϵu+h = s. Thus FT is a function of (T, Ct , J);
conditional on J, it need not be a function of T alone.
     In the elongated case coarsen the orientation of D and its aperture data to widths 0, u − h,
computing the latter from the center given by FT . In the isotropic case use widths u − h, u − h.
Denote this intermediate observation by Y . Transport proximity makes Y determined by D,
conditional on J, up to a list of size at most ϵ−C∗ η .
     Enlarge each rectangle of Y by one fixed factor so that it contains the entire corresponding
isotropic FT cell. The factor works for every orientation: projections of a square have length at
most its diagonal, and rounding the center adds only a fixed grid spacing. This costs at most
(log C)/ℓ in depth. Call the enlarged label Ȳ . It is a function of Y , so it has the same conditional
list bound from D. Let κ(g | FT , J) be the conditional law of Ȳ under π      e , and adjoin G by that
kernel:
                                 π
                                 e (ω, T, G) = πe (ω, T )κ(G | FT , J).

We keep the same notation for the augmented law. It preserves the entire old law of (ω, T ) and
the joint law of (FT , J, Ȳ ) with Ȳ replaced by G. The new G is independent of T conditional on
(FT , J), contains Ct , and is faithful for every line with that FT , J value. No T -dependent cell is
appended to its label.
    Apply Theorem 4.2 in each localizer with duration 2h and horizon u + h. The strict eccentricity
condition, or b∗ = 0 with ut > h, gives u > h. Its root caps have widths at most u + h = ur − b∗ ,
and the endpoint horizon a + t ≤ L, with its stated depth error, also places them inside the local
packet menu. For every such root test F , the support upper bound gives

                                  Hj (T | F ) + Ej w(F ) ≤ g(j) + C∗ η.

This uses only the retained support, so it holds for the biased candidate marginal. The positive
root supremum is consequently at most g(j) − Hj (T ) + C∗ η. Positive growth yields, on each J = j
fiber,
                        Ej w(G) + Hj (T | G) + 2St,j ≤ g(j) + 2γh + C∗ η.                   (11.43)

                                                    102
   When kf > 0, the preserved endpoint marginal supplies an averaged information lower bound

                                     I(T ; D | FT , J) ≥ kf − C∗ η.                            (11.47)

Here is the finite-list verification. In absolute units, FT and Pf have identical velocity depth ut − h
and position depth t + ut − h. The candidate position differs from the observed one by at most
ϵ−θ ϵr+ur , exactly an ϵ−θ multiple of that position unit, and the velocity difference is O(ϵut ). The
localizer has the coarser width in (11.4); its remaining tags have the charged conditional count. It
follows that
                             H(FT , J | Pf ) ≤ C∗ η,     H(Pf | FT , J) ≤ C∗ η.
Because πeΩ = π, (11.21) still holds for its endpoint labels. It is not asserted after conditioning
separately on J. The chain rule gives

                     H(Wf | FT , J) ≥ H(Wf | Pf ) − H(FT , J | Pf ) ≥ kf − C∗ η.

     Neither coarse label alone determines Wf . The pairs (D, FT , J) and (T, FT , J), however, each
determine a list of at most ϵ−C∗ η possibilities. First list the possible Pf ’s supplied by FT , J.
For each listed value, nf (Pf ) is known, with no continuity assumption on this function. Use
that value’s coarse velocity center as reference. The remaining velocity has size O(ϵut −h ). The
incoming alignment of the normal of D with nf (Pf ) is O(ϵh ), so changing between them changes
the measured component by O(ϵut ), one Wf unit. Thus D resolves that component to the stated
list. The exact velocity of T does so as well, since it differs from the output velocity by O(ϵut ).
For isotropic tests use both velocity coordinates. Consequently

                            H(Wf | D, FT , J) + H(Wf | T, FT , J) ≤ C∗ η.

The elementary information inequality

             I(T ; D | FT , J) ≥ H(Wf | FT , J) − H(Wf | D, FT , J) − H(Wf | T, FT , J)

proves (11.47). This is a conditional information averaged over J under π
                                                                        e , which is exactly what
the averaged bounds need. If kf = 0, use nonnegativity of conditional mutual information instead.

Comparison with (11.32) proves the lower bound in (11.33). Its upper bound is (11.10).
   Under µ at time r, P has depth ut = ur − h and W has depth ur . Thus (11.27) and the earlier

                                                  103
11.9    A finite backward chain
Suppose, for a contradiction, that βq > γ/2, and set

                                              c = 4βq − 2γ > 0.

Choose an integer q with qc > 2. Proposition 10.4 supplies q backward steps of a common positive
length 2h, with all times positive and ut > h. In the forced alternative first take h sufficiently
small compared with the fixed aspect gap times γ; this verifies (11.3). In the other alternative all
the back tests are exactly isotropic.
    Fix this chain and start with k0 = 0. At cut i, apply the rule for a prepared cut and call its
final endpoint law πi ; the returned µi is the next endpoint law. Let ∆i , ηi be the actual selected
errors for πi , with J-averages under (πi )J . They include the exceptional-fiber score splits and the
accumulated ζ + log1/ϵ (1/pA ) with every pA measured in its current law. Choose the finitely many
retention parameters and current-law branches so this finite cost sum is as small as required.
    Within each fixed outer row j, choose the finite inner parameters θ, ξ and the finitely many ζ’s,
then the initial packet tail orders and coefficient cutoff, the later transport order, and finally the
precision ϵ. These choices make the inner errors, including both mass-comparison costs, arbitrarily
small in that row. Finite composition and (11.23) leave the selected deficits bounded by that row’s
∆j , with the fixed finite selection factors, plus those inner errors. With the target shape and g
accuracies fixed before choosing the late row, these factors are bounded across the subsequence:
only uniformly positive current masses of qualifying unions and retained complements divide
deficits, while individual tag probabilities enter only logarithmic costs. The selected deficits are
not sent to zero inside the row. Only then choose a sufficiently late outer row, with q and h already
fixed across the subsequence, and choose finite inner precision in that row so that the total error
bound below holds. No transport order uniform in unbounded outer aspect parameters is required.
    Apply Proposition 11.4 with bq = βq . Iteration of (11.33) gives
                                       q
                                       X
                         kq ≥ qch −          (2∆i + C∗ ηi ),   kq ≤ 2h + C∗ ηq .
                                       i=1

Choose the finite errors so that
                               q
                               X
                                     (2∆i + C∗ ηi ) + C∗ ηq < (qc − 2)h.
                               i=1

The two inequalities are incompatible. Consequently βq ≤ γ/2, completing the proof of Theo-
rem 9.1.


12     Oscillatory integrals and Bochner–Riesz multipliers
We now turn packet growth into the multiplier bound. The first step is an oscillatory integral
estimate with loss λν for every ν > 0, uniform over fixed phase and amplitude classes. After
a change of variables, this gives norm Oδ,ν (λ−δ+ν ) for a Bochner–Riesz kernel shell of radius λ.
Choosing ν < δ makes the dyadic shell sum converge. We keep the data bounds explicit because
the shell amplitudes vary with the scale.

Theorem 12.1 (Oscillatory integral estimate). Fix bounded input and output regions Kv , Kx ⊂ R2 .
For the input patch Kv , fix a class of smooth real phases satisfying the phase assumptions of

                                                      104
Section 9, with common domain [0, 1] × R2 × Uv , where Uv is an open neighborhood of Kv . All

rescaled patches described in that section, with the resulting uniform bounds. Throughout the phase
domain,
                                     ∂v Φ(c, x, v) = ζ(v, x − cv).

either

Lemma 12.2 (Endpoint expansion of the kernel). The kernel is bounded on R3 . For R ≥ 1 it has
an expansion
                                                            (j)

Proof. Boundedness follows from mδ ∈ L1 . Integrating first over the disks perpendicular to e3
gives the exact formula
                                                 Z 1

Choose a smooth partition into neighborhoods of the two endpoints and a compact subinterval of
(−1, 1). At z = 1 write t = 1 − z and extract e2πiR . Its remaining integral has amplitude t1+δ a(t),
with a smooth and supported in a fixed small interval [0, t0 ). Its jth R derivative has amplitude a
constant times t1+δ+j a(t). The part t ≤ R−1 is bounded by CR−2−δ−j . On a smooth dyadic shell
t ≍ h ≥ R−1 , N integrations by parts give the bound

                                        Cδ,j,N R−N h2+δ+j−N .

For an integer N > 2 + δ + j the sum over these shells is at most Cδ,j R−2−δ−j . The same argument
at z = −1 gives the other symbol. The middle integral and every derivative decrease faster than
any inverse power of R; after multiplication by e−2πiRR it can be included in b+ . This proves (12.6).
Radial integration of its absolute-value bound gives 1∞ R2 R−2−δ dR < ∞ when δ > 1.

   Take a smooth radial partition of unity into a bounded region and shells |x| ≍ λ, λ = 2j ≥ 2.
The bounded piece of Kδ is integrable. By Lemma 12.2, each shell is the sum of two kernels

                                       λ−2−δ e±2πi|x| aλ,± (x/λ),

where the amplitudes are supported in a fixed annulus and all their derivatives are bounded
uniformly in λ. In the convolution operator set x = λz, y = λy ′ . The factor from volume is λ3 , so
the rescaled operator is

Partition input and output space into unit cubes with smooth cutoffs of bounded overlap. The

The eigenvalues of ∇2 g(q) are (1 + |q|2 )−1/2 and (1 + |q|2 )−3/2 . They are bounded away from zero
on the fixed argument region of the integral, but the smaller eigenvalue tends to zero at infinity.
The packet decomposition uses global definiteness when solving (9.22) for every Fourier mode. We
therefore modify g outside the argument region to obtain the global hypothesis of Theorem 12.1.
   Choose R0 > 1 so that all arguments in (12.9) have norm less than R0 . Let χ be a nondecreasing
smooth function, zero on (−∞, R0 ], one on [R0 + 1, ∞), with

                                         |χ(j) (r)| ≤ C j+1 (j!)2 .

Such a function is obtained by integrating and normalizing the bump exp[−1/t − 1/(1 − t)] on
0 < t < 1, extended by zero. For completeness, Cauchy’s estimate on a complex disk of radius a
small fixed multiple of t gives a derivative bound j!(C/t)j e−c/t near t = 0. Maximizing t−j e−c/t
gives C j+1 (j!)2 ; the estimate at t = 1 is identical, and away from the endpoints analyticity gives
the bound directly.
    Let h(r) = 0 for r ≤ R0 and, for r > R0 , let

the remaining compact region the original Hessian is uniformly positive. Thus

The analytic factors q/|q| in the radial Hessian obey the same bound after the product rule.
Outside the transition annulus, h(r) is a quadratic polynomial in r plus a linear term and a
constant, whose radial Hessian has the same bounds. Near the origin there is no modification. The
original g has uniform analytic Hessian bounds. Formula (12.11), with ge in place of g, therefore
satisfies all the required global hypotheses, including the mixed v, p derivative bounds.
    For each fixed y3 , Theorem 12.1, applied after this change of variables, bounds the sliced
