in L3 of the output variables. The transformed amplitudes have uniform seminorms, and the

cube pair. Finally, if ak is the input L3 norm in cube k, the output norm in cube j is at most

follow from the bounded neighbor counts in both directions. Summing the finite angular partition

Proof of Theorem 1.1. Combining (12.7) and (12.8) shows that the shell of radius λ has L3 operator
norm at most Cδ,ν λ−δ+ν . For each fixed δ > 0, choose ν = δ/2. The sum over λ = 2j converges,
and the bounded kernel piece is controlled by Young’s inequality. The resulting operator is bounded
on L3 (R3 ).
   For a Schwartz input, convolution with Kδ equals F −1 (mδ fb): writing Kδ as the inverse
transform, Fubini is justified by ∥mδ ∥1 ∥f ∥1 < ∞. The spatial partition also converges to this
convolution pointwise by dominated convergence, since Kδ is bounded and f ∈ L1 . Its operator-
norm sum therefore represents the same multiplier. Density of Schwartz functions in L3 supplies
the asserted bounded extension.

12.3    The full strict range
We record the consequence for other exponents. Its proof uses the positive-order theorem at
arbitrarily small, fixed orders; no uniformity as the order tends to zero is needed.
Lemma 12.3 (Complex orders from a real order). For ℜz > 0 and R > 0, let Tz,R have multiplier
(1 − |ξ|2 /R2 )z+ , and write Tz = Tz,1 . Suppose r > 0 and Tr is bounded on Lq for 1 ≤ q < ∞. If
a > r and N is an integer with N > r + 1, then

Corollary 12.4 (Strict Bochner–Riesz range in three dimensions). Let 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ and
                                                 1 1  1
                                                                   
                                     δ > max 0, 3 − −   .                                    (12.15)
                                                 p 2  2

Then the multiplier (1 − |ξ|2 )δ+ is bounded on Lp (R3 ). The same bounds hold uniformly for every
positive radius after dilation. This assertion concerns the strict inequality (12.15).
Proof. First let 3 < p < ∞. Put
                                3
                     θ =1−        ∈ (0, 1),    c = δ − θ > 0,           z(w) = c + w.
                                p
On the boundary ℜw = 0, Lemma 12.3 with r = c/2 and Theorem 1.1 give a polynomially growing
L3 bound for Tc+iτ . On ℜw = 1, apply the same lemma with r = 1 + c/2 > 1. Lemma 12.2
supplies an integrable real-order kernel and hence a polynomially growing L∞ bound for T1+c+iτ .
    This is an admissible analytic family. For Schwartz functions f, g its scalar pairing is the
integral over the unit ball of
                                      (1 − |ξ|2 )c+w fb(ξ)gb(ξ);
on compact subsets of ℜw > −c all its derivatives are dominated by integrable powers of | log(1 −
|ξ|2 )|. On the closed strip 0 ≤ ℜw ≤ 1, its L2 norm is at most one. Approximation by Schwartz

                                                   110
functions therefore extends weak analyticity in the open strip and scalar continuity on the closed
strip to all L2 pairs, including bounded simple functions of finite support. Indeed, the scalar
pairings converge uniformly on the strip by the L2 bound. These are admissible tests for analytic
interpolation. To make the polynomial boundary growth harmless, fix κ > 0 and use the analytic
family
                                                 2
                                          eκ(w−θ) Tc+w .
                                                                                      2      2
Its boundary norms are bounded because the scalar factor has modulus eκ((ℜw−θ) −(ℑw) ) . The
analytic interpolation theorem [33], applied between L3 and L∞ , gives an Lp bound at w = θ,
since
                                   1    1−θ
                                      =      ,    c + θ = δ.
                                   p      3
The Gaussian factor equals one there, so this is the required bound for Tδ .
    For 2 ≤ p ≤ 3, interpolate the fixed real operator Tδ between its L2 bound from Plancherel and
its L3 bound from Theorem 1.1; here (12.15) is exactly δ > 0. Its real multiplier is self-adjoint on
Schwartz functions, so duality proves the corresponding bounds for 1 < p < 2. The symmetry of
(12.15) under p ↔ p′ gives precisely the asserted range. Finally, for p = 1 or p = ∞ the condition is
δ > 1, and Lemma 12.2 and Young’s inequality give the result directly. Dilation gives the assertion
about radii.

    For completeness, the norm-convergence assertion in the introduction follows from the same
uniform radius bounds. If 1 ≤ p < ∞ and fb ∈ Cc∞ , then Tδ,R f → f in the Schwartz topology: on
the fixed Fourier support, every derivative of (1 − |ξ|2 /R2 )δ converges to the corresponding
derivative of 1. Inverse Fourier transforms of Cc∞ functions are dense in Lp . The uniform bounds
and a density argument therefore give Tδ,R f → f in Lp for all f in that space whenever (12.15)
holds.
